The 5 Best Noah Wyle ‘ER’ Episodes of the '90s, Ranked

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

pNBC began airing ER in 1994, and the show quickly became one of the most believable medical dramas of all time. A reason behind this is the beloved character Dr. John Carter, portrayed by Noah Wyle. Noah's character became one of the best television doctors, and critics noticed his uniqueness by the end of Season 1.

Noah received a string of Emmy and Golden Globe nominations for his portrayal, along with four consecutive Actor Awards. He starred on the show for 11 seasons and returned for other story arcs from start to finish, yet his performances were not enough for award recognition after 1999. Here's a list of Dr. Carter's best ER episodes of the '90s, with stories that made this beloved character prove he belongs in an emergency room.

Every County General employee needs a close friend, and for a short time, Carter's was fellow surgical intern Dr. Dennis Gant (Omar Epps). The pair were prepared to become the best surgeons they could be, and stuck by each other when doing so. Their behavior toward one another began to change as time went on, and it made Carter unable to see the signs Gant was struggling. It wasn't until Gant was struck and killed by a train in the episode prior that Carter realized he didn't see what was right in front of him.

Gant's death was one of the most tension-filled times in Carter's life as he couldn't help but wonder if his late friend jumped in front of the train. The survivor's guilt was eating at Carter, and the lack of care he had when expressing his anger toward co-workers was surprising to see so early in his career. The police ultimately ruled Gant's death an accident, and it made doctors a little bit at ease. The cause of death didn't change what Carter felt, and it was stunning to see him question his behavior as a friend, and not as a fellow intern.

The family lives of doctors have been woven into plot lines before, with Carter's being known for their intensity and privileged background. He did maintain positive relationships with some of his relatives, including his cousin, Chase (Jonathan Scarfe). Carter was shocked to learn his cousin was a heroin addict, but he believed that as a doctor and close relative, he could help him get clean. He put in the time and effort with Dr. Anna Del Amico (Maria Bellow), and thought it worked. Carter's plan backfired, and Chase landed in the hospital from a heroin overdose.

Despite Chase's history, Carter's shock was felt throughout the entire hospital room as he tried to resuscitate his cousin. That shock turned into fear while admitting to his grandparents that he was aware of Chase's addiction, and did not tell them about it. Carter had longed for unconditional love from many family members, yet he always had it from Chase. Chase suffered permanent brain damage from his overdose, but Carter sleeping next to his bedside was enough to know that neurological issues would not make him love his cousin any less.

A normal day at County General turned into chaos following a chemical spill. Employees and patients were unsure of what to do, and Dr. Kerry Weaver (Laura Innes) being exposed to the fumes only made things crazier. Carter put his panic aside, and took charge of the ER without hesitation. He directed staff and patients to rooms based on health status while conducting research and communicating with emergency officials.

Carter brought intensity to a whole new level after he was put in charge, and it showed how much he'd grown as a medical professional. He gained more self-confidence just by doing his job, and didn't even realize it until he knew everyone was safe. Carter's work and determination saved hundreds of lives, but he did take a short pause from his leadership duties after finding out his elderly patient had died. Carter snapped out of commander mode when Weaver recovered from her fume exposure and smiled at the employees praising him, realizing that County General's ER was his home.

It's hard to imagine Carter practicing something other than emergency medicine. After figuring that out himself, Carter fought tooth and nail to convince his superior, Dr. Donald Anspaugh (John Aylward) of the same thing. Anspaugh initially refused due to budgetary reasons and Carter signed a contract to complete the County General's surgical program. He changed his mind once Carter explained why he would never be a great surgeon.

It was tough to predict Anspaugh's final decision. He and Carter's reasons behind their opinions of a specialty change were understandable. Carter obviously wasn't backing down, and even if it meant leaving his surgical mentor, Dr. Peter Benton (Eriq La Salle), he couldn't stop fighting for what was right for him. Carter's fight was inspirational for anyone brave enough to follow their hearts, and it led to him becoming one of the best emergency medicine doctors at County General.

Carter was a third-year medical student anxious to become a surgeon on the first episode of ER. He walked in wearing a "tailored white coat," looking nervous and terrified. Carter was eager to begin treating patients, even though he never learned how to start an IV. Something he wasn't afraid of doing when he walked in was interacting with patients, and threw up outside after doing so.

Carter walked back into the ER with his head held high after talking with Dr. Mark Greene (Anthony Edwards). He continued to be imperfect, but he didn't let that get into his head. It was beautiful to see a young student believing in himself on the first day working in a Chicago hospital ER. That beauty is what helped him become a phenomenal doctor who saved millions of lives worldwide.

What happened last time Mauricio Pochettino interviewed for Man Utd job?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Mauricio Pochettino has long been of interest to Manchester United, having twice previously interviewed for the manager job.

Pochettino continues to enjoy a stellar reputation in the Premier League thanks to his work at Tottenham over five-and-a-half years, during which time he twice came close to winning the Premier League title and took Spurs to a Champions League final.

The Argentine's time with Southampton, previous to Spurs, was also eye-catching, while his one-season spell with Chelsea, which ended two years ago, showed signs of promise before his mutually agreed exit.

So it was Pochettino's success at Spurs which twice previously attracted interest internally at Manchester United.

And as Pochettino emerges as the favourite to become the new Man Utd manager, we look back on the previous times he has been sounded out for the job.

Man Utd reportedly came calling in late 2018, following the sacking of Jose Mourinho.

According to The Mirror, Pochettino - who at the time had Spurs in third place in the Premier League standings and was six months away from guiding them to the Champions League final - was a standout candidate and had the approval of Sir Alex Ferguson.

However, Ole Gunnar Solskjaer was eventually given the caretaker role and then the permanent gig, and remained in charge for just under three years.

Pochettino interviewed for the Man Utd job in March 2022, while Ralf Rangnick was in caretaker charge, and eventually lost out to Erik ten Hag.

Pochettino was set to be sacked by Paris Saint-Germain, despite going on to win the Ligue 1 title that season, after being knocked out of the Champions League by Real Madrid.

Warhammer Survivors Review: A Brutal New Bullet Heaven Game

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Warhammer Survivors is a brutal spin-off of Vampire Survivors, pitting heroes from Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar against endless hordes of enemies while maintaining the addictive gameplay loop.

Few games have a title as accurate or descriptive to its core content as Warhammer Survivors. In a nutshell, itt’s Vampire Survivors gameplay, but with Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar characters, monsters, and levels, but with the difficulty turned up to eleven.

Well, that’s a bit of an unfair introduction. If Warhammer Survivors was just a cosmetic upgrade, they would have just released it as DLC in Vampire Survivors, rather than giving it its own dedicated game. After all, Vampire Survivors has invited Castlevania to its roster and added Contra too, so surely Space Marines wouldn’t look out of place?

Thankfully, Warhammer Survivors shakes up the gameplay and adds new tweaks to the familiar formula that makes it a worthwhile purchase for people who aren’t familiar with the tabletop game. Long-time Vampire Survivors fans just need to be aware that its Warhammer equivalent is a fair bit harder than the content they’ve beaten before.

At its core, Warhammer Survivors has the same gameplay loop as Vampire Survivors and its many clones. The player picks a hero, who then has to survive against endless hordes of enemies. The main gameplay gimmick is that the player cannot control how the character shoots: just how they move across the battlefield.

Throughout each level, the player earns experience points from killing enemies, and when they level up, they are given new weapons and upgrades that make it easier to destroy more foes. As time goes on, the enemies get stronger and grow in numbers, meaning careful movement becomes even more important.

There are also bosses that appear throughout the stage, which are outlined in a neon green aura. Slaying these bosses results in crates being dropped with level upgrades. These are important, as if a weapon is sufficiently levelled, then a crate could carry its improved version, such as the Boltgun transforming into the Heavy Boltgun, which has a drastically improved fire rate.

Dying on a run isn’t the end of the world, as the player can find coins strewn throughout each stage that can be spent on static buffs and upgrades. These make future runs easier, so the player has a greater chance of surviving later stages.

Warhammer Survivors keeps this gameplay loop intact, though it splits everything across two worlds. On the main menu, the player selects the “Universe”: Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar, the latter of which is the modern replacement for Warhammer Fantasy (RIP). They have their own stages, characters, and enemies, with no direct crossovers between the two.

Technically, there is content that connects the two, as the upgrades unlocked are shared between the two, meaning working hard in one helps out the other. There are also non-franchise-specific levels that they share, like the Hobby Table, but for the most part, the Warhammer 40,000 and Age of Sigmar parts of the game are separate.

A new indie inspired by Vampire Survivors tasks players with defending their home computer from an onslaught of 2000s-era viruses.

So, if Warhammer Survivors is just Vampire Survivors with Ork Boyz, then is it worth buying? Well, there are some key differences that justify it existing as its own game, rather than DLC for Vampire Survivors.

In Vampire Survivors, all the weapons are tied to the player character. Warhammer Survivors changes this with its Follower system, as characters are now unlocked alongside their weapon when the player levels up. This means they follow the selected hero around, and their attacks will originate from them.

The Follower system might not seem like a big addition, but needing to account for the changing starting position of attacks makes the gameplay considerably more challenging. This is especially true for the Garlic-type weapon users, which have a static area-of-effect centred on their hero. In Warhammer Survivors, the party members move around after the player, meaning the player has to guide them, like a kite, into the enemy hordes.

The other big difference in Warhammer Survivors is how it handles end of stage bosses. In Vampire Survivors, if the player spends too long in a stage, they’ll eventually be attacked by the Grim Reaper and quickly killed. Warhammer Survivors replaces the manifestation of death with the Extremis Bosses: foes that appear at the twenty-five minute mark for an epic fight.

The Follower system might not seem like a big addition, but needing to account for the changing starting position of attacks makes the gameplay considerably more challenging.

The Extremis Bosses can’t be faced without preparation, however, as the player needs to unlock a specific item in each stage to be able to damage them. If the player doesn’t have this item, then the boss will be invincible, and it will automatically kill the hero in a cutscene.

The enemies the player faces on each stage can also have unique gimmicks, usually tied to their bosses. It’s possible for Tyranids to buff their weaker units, making them considerably faster, while the followers of Nurgle can summon massive poison-spewing chimneys, creating damaging areas for the player to walk through.

The Orks of Warhammer 40,000 easily have the best gimmick, as they can call a mighty “Waaagh”, bringing endless waves of units against the player until it ends. Surviving a Waaagh can be one of the trickiest parts of the Ork stage, unless the player prepares lots of AoE attacks to survive the horde.

The studio behind Vampire Crawlers has revealed how the game came about, thanks to its relationship with the creator of Vampire Survivors.

The new additions to Warhammer Survivors make the game considerably harder than the source game. And make no mistake: I adore Vampire Survivors and have purchased it on multiple platforms. I have endured countless runs with my boy Poe, facing the creatures of the night with nothing but a piece of Garlic and a dream, and came out the other side.

Warhammer Survivors ramps the difficulty up in a few ways. The Follower system means the origin point of your weapons keeps moving around, making it harder to accurately fire at enemies. This isn’t as much of a problem with targeted weapon users, like grenades, but AoE and shooter characters are harder to predict, as they keep moving while shooting.

The changes to bosses are also a point against the game, because Warhammer Survivors does an extremely poor job of conveying exactly what items are needed to be “Prepared” for facing the Extremis Bosses. The best clues the player gets is seeing what items are always available in each stage, as well as blanked out menus that fill out if they happen to use the correct upgrades.

There’s far too little information about what items are needed to face the Extremeis Bosses, especially as the blanked out menu only appears after a twenty-five minute run, which is hard to reach on a good day. Most players aren’t going to bother doing trial and error: they’ll just Google the answers as soon as the game launches, so why bother obfuscating it?

It also bears mentioning that the Age of Sigmar characters are way less useful than their 40,000 equivalents. The Warhammer 40,000 roster includes gun and grenade users, while the ranged Age of Sigmar characters tend to be much slower. Thankfully, the upgrades menu stays the same between rosters, so it’s possible to grind with the Warhammer 40,000 cast for stat upgrades, in order to help out their fantasy equivalents.

There are also times when the game will just kill the player with no recourse. The Age of SIgmar stages are particularly bad for this, with enemy cavalry units randomly appearing and rushing across the stage, pushing the player into a wall and instantly killing them in a way that’s impossible to avoid.

The sub bosses fought in each stage also have far, far too much health. I’ve sat there for what felt like an eternity with maxed out weapons, stunlocking a boss into place, while damage numbers just rack up, and they won’t die. It’d be fine if their gameplay made them challenging foes to match their mighty health bar, but many of them are just regular enemies with bigger sprites.

The changes to Warhammer Survivors do provide an extra layer of challenge for those coming from the source material, and I’d be lying if I said I didn’t enjoy the new additions and wrinkles: it just needs a few more tweaks and balance checks for the game to be as good as it can be.

This review covers Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun 2 and why players should play it.

The Warhammer franchise has a reputation for being extremely dark in tone, especially 40,000. The Imperium of Man isn’t exactly a lovely place to exist, between the constant threat of Xeno invasion, the corrupting influence of Chaos present in the souls of mankind, and the rampant corruption within society itself.

Warhammer Survivors is a rare example of Warhammer being tongue-in-cheek and referential. This can be seen through its many nods to the actual game and its history, especially the weapons, such as exploding d6s hitting the board, Nuln Oil slowing foes down, the measuring tape increasing damage AoE, and the experience point booster being called White Dwarf.

The highlight of this approach is the Hobby Table stage, where the player fights off monsters on a crafting table, the kind used by many real-life fans of the tabletop game. The developers had mentioned in the past that the Hobby Table stage is a nod to Micro Machines, and it shows, being easily the most fun level in Warhammer Survivors.

The visuals and overall aesthetic of Warhammer Survivors are close to that of Vampire Survivors, and like the Boltgun series, the approach is mired in retro goodness. Few Warhammer games have managed to cram as many foes onto the screen as this one, making Warhammer Survivors one of the few titles to ever capture the essence of the beloved artwork from the series, save shown through a 16-bit filter.

Warhammer Survivors doesn’t try to tell a story: it’s about throwing iconic heroes from the franchise against familiar foes. It’s clear the people who made the game are huge fans of the source material, and that love shines throughout the experience.

The new layer of difficulty in Warhammer Survivors can be frustrating at times, but it also feels necessary for helping distinguish it from Vampire Survivors. If the player can soldier through the hard moments, then they’ll get another massive dose of Survivors gameplay and content, which is likely a huge selling point on its own for people who are still addicted to the original.

Warahmmer Survivors justifies itself as a separate entity throughout plenty of new characters and stages, with enough gameplay gimmicks to ensure it’s not just a reskin. The difficulty can be frustrating at times, but people don’t sign up for a Survivors-like to have an easy time. Warhammer Survivors will satisfy people looking for more of the familiar but different enough to not be a rehash, while still paying tribute to the universes that won over fans worldwide.

2026's best game so far is finally coming to more platforms, letting mobile audiences become as hopelessly addicted as console and PC players.

‘Walk to Stop the SERPent Pipeline’: Connecticut Groups Protest Proposed Eversource Pipeline

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

EAST HAMPTON, Connecticut — On Saturday, November 7, Sierra Club Connecticut supporters, other community groups, and allies walked for four hours across seven miles from Hurd State Park to Eversource Headquarters in East Hampton to protest the utility’s proposed Southeast Resilience Pipeline. Fifty people attended the Walk to Stop the SERPent Pipeline. Of the 50, 37 participated in the seven-mile walk to Eversource Headquarters.

Sierra Club and partner organizations have given the proposed Eversource pipeline the name “SERPent” to highlight its potential harm to public health, energy affordability, climate, and the Connecticut River which, if approved, the pipeline will run under.

“Prioritizing profit over the health of the environment is to our own detriment,” said Erin Day, Owner of Ten Summit Yoga and Earth Based Wellness. “Protecting our natural landscapes is essential, as the connection to the earth is what sustains our own well-being. Preserving this land from the pipeline is a vital step in honoring our relationship with nature and upholding our responsibility for a healthy future.”

“Building communities resilient to climate and economic crises requires improving people’s health and environments while preventing the danger and destruction posed by this pipeline and similar projects,” said Diana Martinez, of Chicks Ahoy Farm. “We can’t allow our futures to be sacrificed for corporate gain.”

“Eversource wants to build a pipeline under the Connecticut River because they make more money building pipelines than they do selling gas; they are a for profit company making a billion IN PROFIT per year so we know what their priorities are,” said Martha Klein, Sierra Club Connecticut and the No Pipeline Expansion Northeast Coalition. “Eversource is blocking the transition to the modern energy economy by expanding fracked gas infrastructure in our state, which locks us into methane use for decades. Burning fossil gas kills people, disrupts the climate and makes energy unaffordable, but it has been very profitable for Eversource. The time for change is here.”

“As young people, we are critically aware of the fallacies embedded in the short-term logic of fossil fuel infrastructure,” said Jo Almond, Sunrise Movement Wesleyan. “I grew up in a town with one hundred and forty-four active methane gas leaks; a result of relying on natural gas pipelines imposed by our utility company. I want to be part of the last generation who has to deal with the toxicity that fossil fuels introduce into our communities; this pipeline would guarantee that Connecticut’s youth will be growing up alongside poisoned water, air, and soil for generations to come.”

“Environmental racism harms all communities and it is important to hold corporations and utilities accountable for expanding poisonous methane gas into our communities and exposing us to environmental harms that exacerbate health, housing, education and economic barriers,” said Tenaya Taylor, Nonprofit Accountability Group.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

Alzheimer’s may have early warning signs outside the brain

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Alzheimer's disease is best known for its effects on memory and thinking, but new research suggests that some symptoms may develop in a surprising place: outside the brain.

Scientists at the University of Central Florida (UCF) have discovered that genetic mutations associated with Alzheimer's can interfere with the nerves that control muscle movement, even without involvement from the brain or spinal cord. The findings could help explain why some people with Alzheimer's experience changes in walking, balance, or coordination long before noticeable memory problems emerge.

The discovery raises the possibility that movement difficulties could provide earlier warning signs of the disease and that treatments focused exclusively on the brain may overlook important problems elsewhere in the body.

The research was led by James Hickman, a professor at UCF's Nanoscience Technology Center, and Research Professor Xiufang "Nadine" Guo, working alongside scientists at the healthcare technology company Hesperos. Funded by the National Institutes of Health's National Institute on Aging, the study used laboratory systems built from human cells to investigate how inherited Alzheimer's mutations affect movement. The findings were published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association.

"Motor deficits may be an earlier indication [of Alzheimer's]," Guo says. "If we can detect those changes and intervene earlier, that could help delay the onset of central nervous system symptoms."

Why Alzheimer's Symptoms May Appear Before Memory Loss

Alzheimer's is generally associated with progressive memory loss, confusion, and declining cognitive abilities. However, doctors have also observed that some patients develop physical symptoms, including changes in balance, coordination, and gait (manner of walking), years before clear cognitive difficulties become apparent.

These observations have raised an important question: Are such movement problems simply a consequence of damage occurring in the brain, or could Alzheimer's also interfere directly with nerves elsewhere in the body?

To investigate, the UCF team focused on familial Alzheimer's, a rare inherited form of the disease that typically develops earlier in life (from 40 to 65 years of age) than the more common form.

Their experiments revealed that motor neurons carrying mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's could disrupt the connections responsible for controlling muscles, even when the brain and spinal cord were completely absent from the experimental system.

Motor neurons are specialized nerve cells that transmit signals to muscles, telling them when to contract and produce movement. The new findings suggest that Alzheimer's mutations can interfere with this process directly, rather than affecting movement only through damage to the brain.

"This is the first time it's been demonstrated that deficits in the peripheral nervous system can arise directly from these mutations," Hickman says. "It means drugs that target the brain may not fix problems in the rest of the body."

The peripheral nervous system includes the nerves located outside the brain and spinal cord. These nerves carry signals between the central nervous system and the rest of the body, helping control movement, sensation, and other essential functions.

Guo also points out that preserving the ability to move could have benefits beyond physical health. Regular physical activity is associated with better cognitive well-being, so protecting motor function may also help support brain health.

How a Miniature Human Cell System Revealed the Discovery

To examine the relationship between Alzheimer's mutations and muscle movement, the scientists used an innovative laboratory platform known as "human-on-a-chip" technology.

Developed and manufactured through Hesperos, a company co-founded by Hickman, these miniature systems bring different types of human cells together under controlled conditions. They allow researchers to recreate specific biological functions and observe how diseases interfere with them.

Unlike conventional laboratory experiments that examine isolated cells, these systems can reproduce important interactions between different tissues. They also provide an alternative to animal models, which do not always accurately reflect how human diseases behave.

For this study, the team created a miniature model of the neuromuscular junction, the connection where a motor neuron communicates with a muscle cell.

The model was deliberately designed without a brain or spinal cord. This was a crucial part of the experiment because it allowed the researchers to investigate whether Alzheimer's-related changes could cause movement problems independently of the central nervous system.

The scientists combined healthy muscle cells with motor neurons derived from stem cells that carried genetic mutations associated with familial Alzheimer's disease. Stem cells are cells that can develop into specialized types of cells, making them valuable tools for studying human diseases in the laboratory.

The researchers then examined how effectively the altered nerve cells communicated with otherwise healthy muscle tissue.

The results showed that the Alzheimer's-associated mutations could interfere with normal nerve-to-muscle function. This suggests that at least some movement difficulties linked to the disease may arise directly in the peripheral nervous system rather than being caused entirely by degeneration in the brain.

Why Damaged Nerve Connections Can Affect Walking and Balance

Every voluntary movement depends on successful communication between the nervous system and the muscles.

At the neuromuscular junction, an electrical signal traveling along a motor neuron triggers chemical communication that causes a muscle to contract. This process makes everyday activities possible, from walking and maintaining posture to lifting objects and performing precise movements.

When the connection becomes impaired, muscles may respond less effectively to nerve signals. Depending on the severity and location of the problem, this can contribute to weakness, reduced coordination, and difficulty sustaining physical activity.

To evaluate these effects, the research team measured how consistently nerve signals produced muscle contractions and how long the muscles could continue contracting before becoming fatigued.

These measurements provided a direct assessment of how well the nerve and muscle cells worked together. They also resemble aspects of the functional testing physicians use when examining patients with movement disorders.

"You can't move unless the motor circuit works," Hickman says. "When a doctor taps your knee to check your reflex, they're testing that exact connection."

By demonstrating impaired nerve-to-muscle communication in a system containing no brain or spinal cord, the study provides evidence that mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's can produce effects beyond the central nervous system.

However, the experiments do not establish that these changes necessarily occur before memory loss in patients. Further research will be needed to determine how closely the laboratory findings reflect the progression of Alzheimer's in people, particularly those with the more common forms of the disease.

Could This Discovery Lead to Earlier Alzheimer's Detection?

The findings suggest a potentially important new direction for Alzheimer's research: looking beyond memory and other cognitive symptoms when investigating the disease.

If future studies confirm that changes in motor function are detectable early in certain patients, tests of walking, coordination, or nerve-to-muscle activity might eventually help identify disease-related changes before more obvious neurological symptoms develop.

The results also raise questions about treatment. Many Alzheimer's therapies are designed to address processes occurring in the brain, but medications that target the central nervous system may not necessarily correct abnormalities affecting peripheral nerves and muscles.

Understanding these separate effects could help researchers investigate treatments that protect physical function alongside cognitive health.

Human-on-a-Chip Technology Could Transform Disease Research

Beyond the implications for Alzheimer's, the researchers see considerable potential in using human-on-a-chip systems to investigate other diseases and evaluate possible treatments.

Because these platforms contain human cells and reproduce measurable biological activities, they can help scientists observe disease-related changes in conditions that more closely resemble human physiology than some conventional experimental models.

This approach may be particularly useful in drug development, where researchers need to understand not only how a potential treatment affects individual cells but also how it influences interactions between different tissues.

Such systems can also uncover biological effects that may be difficult to detect in animal experiments, potentially helping researchers identify promising therapies and recognize limitations earlier in development.

For Hickman, the progress represents the culmination of three decades of work devoted to understanding disease through more realistic laboratory models.

"These systems let us study disease in a way that's closer to what actually happens in the human body, and that's what we need to develop better treatments," he says.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/advocate-health-increase-total-employee-compensation-846m-2027" hreflang="en">Advocate Health to increase total employee compensation by $846M for 2027</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Advocate Health to increase total employee compensation by $846M for 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Nonprofit health system Advocate Health will invest an additional $1.24 billion in its workforce beginning in 2027, with approximately $846 million dedicated towards compensation and $390 million towards incremental benefits.

Advocate will increase its minimum wage to $20 an hour, building on its $18.85 per hour minimum wage established this year.

For a 40-hour workweek, that wage would pay over $26,520 more per year than the federal minimum wage, it said.

Compensation investments will also include merit increases, incentives, pay-range updates and other investments in pay, executives said in the Nov. 5 announcement.

“The most important investment we make isn’t in buildings, technology or equipment. It’s in our people,” said Eugene A. Woods, Advocate Health CEO, in a statement. “Healthcare is a 24/7 calling and every single day our 170,000 teammates bring extraordinary skill, compassion and commitment to the patients and communities we serve. Their dedication is what brings our purpose to life for the millions of people who count on us each year.”

The country’s third-largest nonprofit health system currently covers approximately 83% of employee medical costs, and plans to invest an additional $138 million for what it says will absorb “more than 90% of the projected $149 million increase in medical plan costs.”

Moreover, the health system’s 2027 benefits package extends fully paid parental bonding leave to four weeks enterprise-wide; expands paid bereavement leave; continues coverage for GLP-1 medications for weight loss; provides no-cost mental health support for employees and family members; continues sustained retirement contributions of up to 6% of eligible pay and offers education assistance across more than 1,300 eligible programs.

The package also offers student loan repayment assistance, with lifetime support of up to $21,000 for eligible full-time employees and $10,500 for eligible part-time employees.

“The benefits that matter most are the ones that make a real difference in people’s lives,” said Nakesha Lopez, Advocate Health senior executive vice president and chief people and culture officer, in a statement. “We want our benefits to reflect what teammates need at different stages of their lives, while keeping affordability at the forefront and providing meaningful support for their health, families and financial futures.”

Plant-Based Milk Alternatives May Not Give You Enough Iodine. Here’s Why That Matters

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Replacing dairy with plant-based alternatives (PBAs), even partially, could substantially lower iodine intake, especially if those alternatives aren’t fortified, according to a recent study published in the Lancet Planetary Health.

The United Kingdom-based researchers found that only 12% of UK plant-based milk, yogurt, and cheese products analyzed were fortified with iodine. This compares with 61% for calcium and 46% for vitamin B12.

In the United States, the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) provides guidance on the nutritional content of PBAs, but fortification with iodine is voluntary.

The study found that replacing as little as 20% of dairy intake with plant-based alternatives could increase the risk of inadequate iodine intake, particularly among adolescent girls and women of reproductive age.

“People often worry about a decline in calcium intake when they replace some of their dairy with plant-based alternatives. As a result, iodine is frequently overlooked. This study serves as a valuable reminder of iodine’s significance as a nutrient,” said Victoria Finn, MD, board certified endocrinologist at Medical Offices of Manhattan, who wasn’t involved in the study.

The study included analysis of UK plant-based milk, cheese, and yogurt products, as well as dietary data from 3,500 people.

The researchers used statistical modeling to predict how swapping dairy for PBAs might affect iodine and other nutrients.

The people who are most vulnerable to iodine inadequacy are adolescent females and people at reproductive age, especially those who are pregnant or who plan to become pregnant.

The researchers estimated that replacing 20% to 35% of dairy intake with PBAs would increase the proportion of people with inadequate iodine intake:

When the researchers modeled the replacement of all dairy products with PBAs, the proportion of adolescent females with inadequate iodine intake rose to 80%. Among females ages 19 to 49, that proportion remained relatively stable as replacement rates increased.

It is important to keep in mind that the study used dietary modeling rather than direct measurement of iodine status.

Iodine is important for thyroid hormone production, metabolism, and both fetal and child brain development.

“Your thyroid gland requires iodine to produce thyroid hormones, which play a crucial role in regulating metabolism and supporting normal growth and development,” said Finn.

She added that if you consistently consume too little iodine over an extended period, the thyroid gland may enlarge and eventually struggle to produce enough hormones. This condition is known as hypothyroidism, which can lead to various symptoms such as lethargy, sleepiness, weight gain, dry skin, and more.

“Hypothyroidism can be particularly dangerous during pregnancy, as the baby’s brain, nervous system, and organs rely heavily on the mother’s thyroid hormone during the early stages of development,” Finn added.

In the United Kingdom, dairy is a major iodine source due to cattle feed supplementation, not because dairy is naturally high in iodine.

In the United States, dairy is also an important source of iodine for the same reasons.

However, the risk profile for iodine deficiency in the U.S. may differ from the U.K., as iodized salt is also a significant source of iodine and is widely available in the U.S., but not in the U.K.

According to the National Institutes of Health (NIH), the recommended daily intake of iodine depends on your age.

Plant-based or plant-forward diets have grown in popularity, in part due to the health benefits they offer. For example, consuming less meat can reduce your risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes, obesity, and high cholesterol.

However, as this study suggests, it may be possible to reduce your iodine intake when transitioning to a plant-based diet. There are ways you can protect your iodine intake when choosing a more plant-forward diet, though.

Check the labels on plant-based dairy alternatives to find those fortified with iodine. You can also read the nutrition label. Iodine may be listed as potassium iodide or potassium iodate.

Seafood, eggs, and iodized salt are all good sources of iodine.

You can also discuss iodine supplements with your healthcare professional.

“Iodine supplements should be taken with caution, especially without checking your thyroid levels,” said Finn. “Additionally, be cautious with kelp supplements, as their iodine content can vary, and excessive iodine intake can disrupt thyroid function.”

Collision of two trains in Egypt kills at least four

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

President orders prompt investigation after one train derails in collision in al-Ayyat about 30 miles from Cairo

At least seven people were killed and more than 100 others injured when two trains collided on Sunday south of Cairo, authorities have said.

The crash occurred in al-Ayyat district in Giza province, about 30 miles (50km) south of the capital, when a train travelling to the south-western Fayoum province crashed into the rear of a train heading to the southern province of Aswan, according to the transport ministry. It said one of the trains had derailed.

The health ministry said at least seven people had been killed and more than 100 injured. The injured were taken to nearby hospitals for treatment, it said.

The president, Abdel Fatah al-Sisi, said in a social media post that he had ordered a prompt investigation into the crash to hold those responsible accountable.

Emergency teams and cranes were dispatched to the scene to clear the wreckage and restore services to their normal schedule as soon as possible, the Egyptian railway authority said.

A train driver and his assistant were injured in September last year when two freight trains collided in the same district.

Scientists Discover a Hidden Eye Disease That Can Steal Your Night Vision

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Researchers can spot signs of a newly identified genetic eye disease before light-sensing cells are lost by measuring how vision recovers in darkness.

People with a newly identified genetic eye condition can see well for most of their lives before gradually losing peripheral vision and struggling to see at dusk or at night. The condition is linked to a gene already associated with a different eye disease, one that affects central vision first.

The two conditions arise from different variants, or versions, of the EFEMP1 gene. The newly identified variant, p.Arg140Trp, causes abnormally thick material to build up between cells in the eye. Researchers are calling the condition EFEMP1-associated late-onset retinal degeneration, or L-ORD, although it has not been formally named.

L-ORD begins slowly at the periphery of the retina, the tissue at the back of the eye that converts light into signals the brain interprets as sight. Its gradual progression makes it difficult to detect. One useful clue is unusually slow recovery of vision when a person moves from a lighted setting into low light. This adjustment involves rod photoreceptors, the retinal cells specialized for seeing in dim conditions.

“We can detect a major abnormality in the way rod photoreceptors recover in darkness with retinas that still look structurally intact,” said Artur V. Cideciyan, PhD, an ophthalmology researcher and co-director of the Center for Hereditary Retinal Degenerations. “This gives us a functional marker of disease at a stage when photoreceptor cells have not yet been lost.”

The investigation began with a clinical description of a family in the United States in 1998.

“The different disease expression in this family was thought to be similar to a condition caused by C1QTNF5 gene variants. However, none of the variants associated with that disease were found,” Cideciyan said. “Our collaborators then discovered a new variant in the EFEMP1 gene that normally causes a different disease where the macula, which is tied to central vision, is affected first.”

The genetic finding allowed researchers to expand the investigation to multiple European families. They studied three unrelated families in different countries that shared the p.Arg140Trp variant and compared them with a separate family carrying p.Arg345Trp, the EFEMP1 variant responsible for the already-known condition affecting central vision.

Published in JAMA Ophthalmology, the research involved an international team that included scientists from the Scheie Eye Institute of the Perelman School of Medicine at the University of Pennsylvania. Researchers from the University of Edinburgh, the University of Basel, Charles University, and the University of Bonn also contributed.

The researchers do not yet know how widespread p.Arg140Trp is or how many people have this form of L-ORD. They suspect it may be relatively rare, but larger studies are needed to establish its prevalence.

“Aging is a risk factor for many medical conditions, including retinal diseases such as age-related macular degeneration, the most common retinal condition of aging,” said Tomas S. Aleman, MD, a professor of ophthalmology and the other co-director of the Center for Hereditary Retinal Degenerations. “Lessons learned from these infrequent, late-onset genetic eye diseases offer glimpses at possible mechanisms of disease for much more frequent retinal problems.”

Reference: “Widening the Spectrum of Disease Expression due to Heterozygous Variants in EFEMP1” by Chloe M. Stanton, Georg Ansari, Kristina Pfau, Theresa Lipsky, Mihail Halachev, Lukas Gerasimavicius, Camilla Drake, Joseph A. Marsh, Caroline Hayward, Alexander Sumaroka, Bohdan Kousal, Petra Lišková, Mathieu Quinodoz, Carlo Rivolta, Tomas S. Aleman, Artur V. Cideciyan and Maximilian Pfau, 10 September 2026, JAMA Ophthalmology. DOI: 10.1001/jamaophthalmol.2026.3828

This study was supported by grants from the University of Edinburgh Chancellor’s Fellowship award, RS Macdonald Charitable Trust Seedcorn Award, the BrightFocus Foundation (M2024009N), the German Research Foundation (532367710), the Medical Research Council (MC_UU_00035/9, MC_UU_00007/10), the Swiss National Science Foundation (204285), the Ministry of Health of the Czech Republic (NW24-06-00083), Charles University in Prague (UNCE/24/MED/022 and SVV 2600631), and the Samuel G. Jacobson, MD, PhD, Memorial Fund.

This 80-Year-Old Uses Two Bodyweight Moves to Stay Strong and Independent—No Gym Needed

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Personal trainer Nicola Bretherton shared her mom's home routine, which helps her maintain functional strength, coordination, mobility and balance.

Fat-loss coach Nicola Bretherton helps women build fitness, confidence, and strength for today and for life.

“Especially as you get older...cardiovascular fitness is important but so is building and maintaining muscle,” the founder of the Next Level You program said on Instagram. “[It's necessary] for your strength, bones, and long-term health.”

She highlighted that if changing body composition is your goal, strength training is especially valuable.

“If you’re trying to lose fat and change your body shape, you need to give your muscles a reason to grow and that means challenging them and progressively getting stronger,” she said.

A prime example of maintaining strength and physical capability with age is Nicola’s own mother, whose home exercise routine she recently shared online. The moves build full-body functionality and the ability to carry out everyday tasks.

“This is my mom who will be 80 in October,” she said on Instagram.

“She is a strong lady! She lifts weights at home, does lots of bodyweight exercises—full press-ups! She can even do the splits! I’m so proud of the woman she is—strong, capable, and independent,” she wrote.

“Most 80-year-olds can stand up from a chair, but did you know that most 80 year olds cannot get up from the floor?” Bretherton pointed out. “And that's often because of a lack of strength, balance and mobility.”

The movement also requires coordination and flexibility.

“Most 80 year olds cannot perform a full body weight press up from the floor,” noted Bretherton. “A proper press up requires upper-body pushing strength, core stability and shoulder stability.”

She reminded readers of the real-world transferability of the movement.

“Imagine being able to push yourself back up after a stumble or catching yourself with your hands. This is why you don't train for the body you have now, but the body that you will need later.”

Lost in the Labyrinth of Nicotine Products

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

— Clinicians need a better way to talk about nicotine risk

Americans increasingly navigate a fast-changing marketplace of nicotine and tobacco products. Walk into a convenience store today and you will encounter a dizzying array of products: cigarettes, e-cigarettes, nicotine pouches, smokeless tobacco, snus, and heated tobacco products, often sold in bright packaging and flavors designed to appeal.

Accompanying this is a confusing information environment. Public health campaigns warn about the dangers of using any tobacco, while the FDA has authorized certain products to carry a modified-risk claim. News stories describe an epidemic of youth vaping, while research reports find that e-cigarettes can help adult smokers quit. Marketing of nicotine and tobacco products often features young, healthy-looking individuals engaged in athletic activities and promises "smoke-free" or "cleaner" experiences, while warnings on products emphasize the dangers of use.

A recent op-ed asserting that the risk of nicotine and tobacco products operates on a continuum illustrates the confusion. Although the byline was attributed to Tom Price, MD, former HHS secretary, a disclosure at the end of the article indicates that it was, in fact, produced in partnership with and paid for by PMI Corporate Services Inc. (Philip Morris International -- a major tobacco company).

While no tobacco products are completely safe, the FDA has recognized that combustible tobacco products (i.e., products that are burned and smoked) like cigarettes pose the greatest health risks and that noncombustible products generally fall lower on the risk spectrum. Reflecting this, the agency has permitted certain products to be marketed with a modified-risk claim that using those products instead of cigarettes reduces the risk of several serious diseases.

As researchers in tobacco communication and regulatory science, we argue that public health agencies and healthcare practitioners have a responsibility to help people navigate this complicated environment. As the nicotine and tobacco marketplace changes, so should our communication about the harm of these products. Individuals in the U.S. are confronted with an assortment of seemingly conflicting messages about nicotine and tobacco products. It is easy to see why people might not fully understand that different products present varying harms.

For someone who does not use any nicotine or tobacco products, the advice is straightforward: Don't start. Nicotine is addictive, and products containing nicotine present a variety of risks, including cardiovascular diseases. For someone who smokes cigarettes, quitting entirely is the best option, and approved treatments such as nicotine patches, gum, and lozenges, and prescription medications should generally be considered as first-line approaches.

But real life is rarely so tidy. Some people try repeatedly to quit and cannot. Others are not yet ready to give up using nicotine and tobacco products. For them, understanding the differences in risk between continuing to smoke cigarettes and using nicotine and tobacco products that are not burnt can impact their health. Eliminating cigarette smoke exposure, even if someone continues to use nicotine and tobacco, can lead to a meaningful reduction in harm.

Clinicians have recommended considering e-cigarettes as a smoking cessation tool for patients who smoke. However, as the marketplace expands far beyond e-cigarettes, doctors, public health agencies, and other trusted voices should be prepared to explain a continuum of risk among various nicotine and tobacco products rather than centering on any single product. This does not mean handing smokers a shopping list. It does not mean calling vapes, nicotine pouches, or smokeless tobacco harmless. And it certainly does not mean encouraging people who do not currently use nicotine to start.

It means being transparent about what we know: Cigarettes are extraordinarily dangerous, and nicotine is what makes them so hard to quit. Some other products can deliver nicotine, but carry substantially lower risks to one's health. Switching completely matters, and using cigarettes alongside another product does not deliver the same risk reduction.

To be clear, we do not know for certain the best way to deliver the information to people who smoke. It could be conversations between healthcare providers and their patients, public education campaigns, a trusted source anyone can consult easily, or some combination of the above. Each approach has trade-offs in reach, cost, and the risk of unintended consequences. What we need is evidence on which approaches help smokers understand relative risk without encouraging nicotine and tobacco product use among people who otherwise would not start.

One key principle is ensuring that whatever the format this communication takes, the information comes from credible and independent sources with no stake in selling these products.

This conversation cannot wait because people are already hearing claims in ads and on social media about reduced harm of certain nicotine and tobacco products. Influencers portray nicotine pouches as a lifestyle, with talk of "upper deckies" and what it means to be a "Zynner." Increasingly, people will see modified-risk claims that the FDA has authorized. If health professionals respond with silence or oversimplified messages, the tobacco industry, social media, and word of mouth will fill the gap. Communication revolving around nicotine and tobacco product harm should help people make sense of the growing array of products and understand how each one works and what risks they carry.

Some worry that acknowledging reduced-risk products sounds like endorsing the tobacco industry. Given the industry's history of deceiving consumers about the risks presented by tobacco, skepticism is warranted. But withholding meaningful information, supported by evidence from the scientific community, on the risk differences is not the answer.

We can hold two ideas at once: No nicotine or tobacco product is safe, and some products are far more dangerous than others. For decades, public health has taught Americans how dangerous cigarettes are, and we should not undermine that achievement. But an evolving marketplace demands an evolving message -- one that tells people not only what to avoid, but how products differ, what switching can and cannot accomplish, and where the evidence remains uncertain. Americans navigating an increasingly complicated nicotine marketplace deserve an accurate roadmap to protect the public's health.

A New Fiber Supplement Could Be a Game Changer for Millions With IBS

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

For people with IBS, a specially designed fiber blend could support a healthier gut without added pain or bloating, a trial alongside a low-FODMAP diet suggests.

Cutting out foods that leave you bloated or in pain can bring welcome relief. For millions of people with irritable bowel syndrome, or IBS, however, a diet that eases discomfort can come with a trade-off. Restricting certain foods can also reduce fiber intake and the beneficial bacteria that help support gut health.

Researchers at Edith Cowan University (ECU) in Australia have tested a specially formulated fiber blend that could help address that dilemma. Called ur gut, the supplement combines high-amylose maize resistant starch with psyllium husk powder. In a small clinical trial, the blend changed the composition of participants’ gut bacteria without worsening their abdominal pain, bloating, diarrhea, or constipation.

IBS is common worldwide and affects nearly 11 percent of Australians. Its cause remains unknown, although stress, infection, and medication are potential triggers. Certain foods, poor sleep, psychological stress, hormonal fluctuations, and lifestyle factors can aggravate symptoms.

One common recommendation is a low-FODMAP diet, which limits certain carbohydrates that gut bacteria readily ferment.

“For years we’ve known that a diet low in fermentable oligosaccharides, disaccharides, monosaccharides, and polyols (FODMAPs) can relieve symptoms, but they can also reduce beneficial gut bacteria and negatively impact fiber intake over time,” said lead author Dr. Tina Yan.

“We wanted to find a way to build better gut health while following a low-FODMAP diet.”

Scientists at ECU’s Nutrition and Health Innovation Research Institute (NHIRI) developed the blend and trialed it in people with IBS who were following a low-FODMAP diet. Adding fiber in this setting requires care because bacteria can produce gas as they break it down.

“Traditionally, fermentable fibers can be difficult for people with IBS because they can increase gas production and discomfort,” Dr. Yan said.

“What’s exciting about this study is that participants maintained stable symptoms while showing meaningful improvements in their gut microbiota as well as in their gut-related anxiety.”

The changes in participants’ gut bacterial composition were significant, and their extent stood out to ur gut® co-creator Professor Amanda Devine.

“Changes of this magnitude are rarely seen with such a short intervention,” she said.

“The study demonstrated that our targeted dietary fiber blend positively reshaped the gut microbiome in people with IBS following a low-FODMAP diet, supporting beneficial bacteria linked to gut and mental health. This was great to see.”

The researchers suspect the explanation lies in how the blend’s two ingredients, resistant starch and psyllium husk, work together. Resistant starch escapes digestion in the small intestine, leaving it available for bacteria to ferment in the colon. Psyllium appears to slow that fermentation, allowing the starch to travel farther into the colon before bacteria break it down. The researchers believe this enhances the production of beneficial metabolites, substances the bacteria produce as they process the starch.

“For many people with IBS, success has traditionally meant simply avoiding certain foods,” said ur gut® co-creator Associate Professor Claus Christophersen.

“Our findings suggest we may also be able to actively rebuild gut health and the microbiome while not making IBS symptoms worse. That’s an important step forward.”

Reference: “Dietary fiber blend improves gut health in patients with irritable bowel syndrome following a low FODMAP diet: a randomized, double-blind control trial” by Ran Yan, Amanda Devine, Johnny Lo, Evania Marlow, Ian C. Dunican, Kanita Kunaratnam, Lesley Andrew and Claus T. Christophersen, 24 September 2026, European Journal of Nutrition. DOI: 10.1007/s00394-026-04113-5

Mammograms May Offer Early Insight Into Cardiovascular Disease

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Mammograms are widely used to help detect breast cancer, but they may also offer insight into heart health.

The routine X-ray screening may identify breast arterial calcification (BAC), which is the presence of calcium deposits within the walls of the small arteries in the breast.

On a mammogram, BAC is commonly identified as thin, white, parallel lines that give a “tram-track” appearance.

While BAC is harmless to the breasts, it is associated with a significantly higher risk of cardiovascular disease. A 2022 study found women with BAC were 51% more likely to develop heart disease or experience a stroke than those without it.

Laxmi Mehta, MD, a cardiologist at The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, said BAC is associated with cardiovascular risk factors, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol. Additionally, BAC has been connected to:

“The reasons for BAC resulting in higher risks for cardiovascular disease are not definitely determined; however, there are some potential mechanisms, one being that they share similar risk factors that result in vascular injury or metabolic stress that result in vascular calcification and atherosclerosis,” Mehta told Healthline.

Cheng-Han Chen, MD, interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center, added that because BAC and heart disease share many of the same risk factors, “calcium in breast arteries therefore suggests disease in the heart (coronary) and brain arteries as well,” he told Healthline.

While 1 in 39 females in the United States dies from breast cancer each year, cardiovascular disease causes one out of every three deaths, resulting in 1 death per minute.

“Cardiovascular disease is still the most common cause of death in premenopausal women,” Richard Reitherman, MD, PhD, a radiologist and medical director of breast imaging at MemorialCare Breast Center at Orange Coast Medical Center, told Healthline.

“The presentation of cardiovascular disease in premenopausal women is notoriously different than in postmenopausal women, primarily [because] there are no typical symptoms such as chest pain.”

Reitherman said that a prevalent and common health screening procedure such as a mammogram can lead to the early identification of women with silent cardiovascular disease.

“Cardiovascular outcome studies that have included breast arterial calcifications as a risk factor are increasingly reported in the medical literature,” he said.

“The hope is that identifying younger women with silent cardiovascular risks [like BAC along with] appropriate integrated risk identification, analysis, and interventions can significantly reduce mortality in this age group.”

Mehta said there are several reasons BAC has been overlooked as a risk factor for heart disease.

“Mammograms are completed for assessment of breast cancer, and BAC has been historically an ‘incidental’ finding, which is not clinically relevant from an oncologic standpoint,” she said.

Additionally, there is no standardization on quantification of BAC severity, nor is it a required component of mammogram reports, said Mehta.

However, routine reporting of BAC on mammograms is an urgent goal of the American College of Radiology, Society of Breast Imaging, and Medicare, said Reitherman.

In fact, it is gaining traction in the United States. A new Maryland law, passed on October 1, requires mammography centers to notify women when a BAC is detected on their mammogram.

“Automated identification and reporting of these calcifications detected on screening mammograms will become increasingly prevalent in the coming months [and] years,” said Reitherman.

The next step is to identify this group of women to primary care physicians so that appropriate risk evaluation can take place, Reitherman added.

In addition to doctors routinely screening for conditions related to heart disease, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol, he hopes BAC will also be routinely considered.

“Under current development are protocols to identify patients needing a cardiologist’s training and expertise to appropriately stratify this group of patients,” said Reitherman.

“The common healthcare behavior [of] having regular mammograms not only reduces risk of morbidity and mortality from breast cancer but will hopefully contribute to the reduction of morbidity and mortality from cardiovascular disease in younger women.”

He said this type of integrated healthcare approach demonstrates cross-specialty integration and coordination among women, radiologists, and their healthcare providers to improve women’s lives.

“This is an amazing game-changing opportunity,” Reitherman said.

Mehta said it’s reasonable for women to ask if BAC is present on their mammogram report.

“But if it is not uniformly reported by the radiologist, the individual clinician may not have the answer,” she said.

More research is needed before BAC on mammograms can be used routinely to predict heart disease.

Finding out your BAC could provide additional information to help you and your doctors decide how aggressively to address your cardiovascular risk factors, Chen said.

Reitherman agreed. “The goal of reducing morbidity and mortality from any cause such as cardiovascular disease is based on recognition of risk factors, followed by appropriate lifestyle and pharmacologic interventions,” he said.

10 Deadliest Weapons in Transformers History

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Across its many continuities, the Transformers franchise features an extensive and varied arsenal of weaponry shaped by the conflicts between its two major warring factions. These weapons serve purposes ranging from strengthening individual warriors in battle to threatening entire civilizations, while some carry historical significance through their origins and connections to Cybertronian history.

Their reputations across the series’ many interpretations are shaped not only by destructive force, but also by the circumstances surrounding their creation, ownership, and use in battle. Some are tied to ancient Transformers lore, while others emerge from later technological advancements and the demands of continuing warfare across competing forces.

The Underbase is an immense repository containing the accumulated knowledge of the entire Cybertronian race, preserved as patterns of light within Marvel’s Generation One continuity. Its contents also possess considerable destructive power, and as a result, long before the main events, Optimus Prime sent it into space to deny Megatron access to its dangerous capabilities.

That threat, however, resurfaced millions of years later when the Underbase approached Earth, drawing rival Decepticon groups into a race to secure it. Starscream secretly pursued the archive for himself, absorbing part of its power and massacring most of the unbonded Autobots and Decepticons before its full force ultimately obliterated him.

Among Megatron’s most recognizable pieces of equipment is the Fusion Cannon, a powerful arm-mounted weapon that has accompanied the Decepticon leader across numerous Transformers continuities. In Generation One, its technical profile describes a particle-beam system capable of converting small quantities of matter into enormous amounts of explosive nuclear energy to power its attacks.

The weapon can discharge energy across distances reaching twelve miles, with enough force to level a small town in a single blast. His specifications also describe an interdimensional link to a black hole, allowing the cannon to draw antimatter at considerable cost to Megatron’s own systems during even one discharge.

The Spark Extractor is a fearsome Decepticon weapon featured in Transformers Prime, designed to remove a Cybertronian’s spark from its mechanical body. It can attack every Transformer within its operational radius simultaneously, draining the life force that sustains them and leaving their mechanical bodies lifeless within moments, without even requiring direct contact.

After the Autobots recovered it, Smokescreen carried the relic into their battle for the Omega Lock on Cybertron. He dropped it among Megatron’s Vehicons before phasing away, killing the surrounding troops and forcing Megatron to destroy the weapon with his Dark Star Saber to prevent its field from reaching him.

The Cybonic Plague is a Decepticon-engineered virus from Transformers Prime, developed under Megatron’s biological warfare program and responsible for millions of Cybertronian deaths during the war. Spread through contact with infected Energon, it gradually contaminates a Cybertronian’s fuel supply and can cause fatal system failure if a cure is not administered.

Its danger resurfaced when Optimus Prime contracted the dormant infection while investigating a centuries-old Autobot vessel that had crashed on Earth. With Ratchet unable to find a remedy, Bumblebee infiltrated Megatron’s subconscious through a Cortical Psychic Patch and recovered the formula from the Decepticon leader’s memories before Optimus’s condition became irreversible.

The Staff of Merlin is an ancient Cybertronian artifact that originated with Quintessa, who intended to use its power to restore her homeworld. Its ability to drain Unicron’s life energy made it central to her plan for restoring Cybertron, placing Earth in danger once its identity as Unicron became known.

After Quintessa brainwashed Optimus Prime, she ordered him to recover the artifact and bring it to Cybertron, where its power could extract Earth’s life energy. Megatron eventually seized the staff and delivered it to her, allowing the process to begin through the Knights’ submerged ship while the Autobots fought to stop her.

The Requiem Blaster is one of the formidable Mini-Con weapons in Transformers Armada, assembled from three members of the Space Mini-Con Team. Astroscope, Payload, and Sky Blast combine to form its cannon configuration, with Astroscope’s transdimensional research enabling it to draw energy from cosmic phenomena, including supernovas, quasars, and black holes.

Sky Blast summoned Astroscope and Payload with a song, awakening the trio’s combined form as the Autobots struggled against Tidal Wave. Optimus Prime then wielded the completed weapon, using its immense output to drive the Decepticon giant back and protect his allies during the desperate coastal battle for the Autobots’ survival.

The Star Saber is another powerful Mini-Con weapon featured in Transformers Armada, formed when Jetstorm, Runway, and Sonar combine their bodies into a single sword. Its power enhances whoever wields it with immense strength and destructive energy, making its three components coveted prizes throughout the war between the Autobots and Decepticons on Earth.

The sword’s reputation predates the modern conflict, as an ancient civilization collapsed after its people fought over possession of the assembled weapon. On Earth, Hot Shot became its first prominent wielder, using its enhanced power to overwhelm Decepticon opponents before the weapon passed into enemy hands through Sideways’s calculated betrayal.

Also in the Armada continuity, the Hydra Cannon is a colossal Mini-Con superweapon developed by the Decepticons during their campaign to leave Earth. Its firing system combines the Star Saber, Skyboom Shield, and Requiem Blaster, three legendary weapons formed by nine individual Mini-Cons, channeling their collective energy through one devastating discharge.

The Decepticons installed the weapon aboard a lunar warship, constructed by enslaved Mini-Cons under Megatron’s command and intended to transport his forces toward Cybertron after their time on Earth. During its first test, the cannon obliterated an approaching comet in seconds, prompting Megatron to order Starscream to target the Autobot base.

The Planet Buster is a moon-sized heat-ray cannon deployed by the Vok in Beast Wars, concealed within Earth’s second moon until the aliens judged their experiment compromised. Designed to ignite the Energon deposits scattered beneath the surface, its beam would trigger a chain reaction capable of tearing the entire planet apart.

The Vok channelled power from an alien installation on Earth, and the firing beam began disturbing the orbiting stasis pods as it charged. Optimus Primal commandeered a modified stasis pod, rigged its transwarp cell with explosives, and piloted it into the Planet Buster's core, sacrificing himself when Megatron’s sabotage prevented his escape.

The Star Harvester is an enormous machine featured in Revenge of the Fallen. It was built by the original Primes to convert a star’s energy into Energon for the AllSpark and their people. Its use was forbidden near inhabited worlds, since draining their suns would also endanger every civilization dependent upon those solar systems and nearby planets.

After the machine was constructed beneath ancient Egypt, the Fallen attempted to violate the Primes’ agreement by activating it to harvest Earth’s sun. His six fellow Primes confronted him, reclaimed the Matrix of Leadership, and sacrificed themselves to hide it inside a tomb built from their own bodies for millennia.

Richard Lloyd Dies: Guitarist With Influential Punk Pioneers Television Was 75

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Richard Lloyd, whose intricate guitar work for the pioneering punk band Television was a fiery counterpart to the sinewy stylings of the band’s co-guitarist and singer Tom Verlaine, died today of the bile duct cancer called cholangiocarcinoma at his home in Chattanooga, Tenn. He was 75.

His death was reported by his family to The New York Times. Lloyd had been diagnosed with the cancer in March.

With his Television bandmates Verlaine, bassist Richard Hell (later replaced by Fred Smith) and drummer Billy Ficca, Lloyd was among the innovative, even revolutionary, New York bands in the mid-1970s to establish the East Village dive bar CBGB as the epicenter of a new downtown music scene. Along with Patti Smith and the Ramones, Television led the way in creating and developing a new sound – defined later (and loosely) as punk – that was often as literate and playful as it was sonically powerful. Related Stories News Paul Howard Dies: Son Of Three Stooges' Moe Howard, Keeper Of The Flame Was 91 News Kimmarie Johnson Dies: 'Beverly Hills, 90210' & 'Dude, Where's My Car?' Actor Was 50

Formed in 1973, Television grew out of the teenage friendship of two Delaware boys, Thomas Miller and Richard Meyers, who by the early ’70s had changed their names to, respectively, Tom Verlaine and Richard Hell and set about developing the spiky guitar sound and keening vocal style (courtesy Verlaine) that would reach its apotheosis in the band’s 1977 masterpiece Marquee Moon. Watch on Deadline

By the time of their 1974 debut at CBGB, Verlaine and Hell had recruited Lloyd to be the group’s second guitarist, a move that gave Television its signature and distinctive dueling Strat-versus-Jazzmaster sound. Unlike the more power-chord-heavy guitar bombast of other bands on the scene, Television specialized in complicated, even byzantine interplay between the six-stringed instruments. And like Patti Smith, Verlaine sang lyrics that signified a devotion to poetry as surely as did his chosen name.

Born Richard John Alt on Oct. 25, 1951, in Pittsburgh, Lloyd grew up in Manhattan’s Greenwich Village and began playing guitar before he hit his teens. He became a regular in the audience of the famed Fillmore East rock palace, mesmerized by the music of The Grateful Dead and other bands of the era. He would be forthcoming about working as a male hustler to support himself during the lean years.

With an early residency at CBGB, Television quickly won over audiences as well as fellow musicians like Patti Smith, Lou Reed and David Bowie, who had caught the band during a trip to New York and set about spreading the word to anyone who would listen. Bowie would later record the group’s song “Kingdom Come” for his 1980 Scary Monsters album

The band’s initial line-up didn’t last long, though, as the old friends Verlaine and Hell began to bicker over everything from their music to the way it was performed on stage. Hell quit in 1975 and formed a sort of punk supergroup called The Heartbreakers with ex-New York Dolls members Johnny Thunders and Jerry Nolan. He eventually left that group to form Richard Hell and the Voidoids.

Hell was replaced in Television by ex-Blondie bassist Fred Smith (not to be confused with Patti Smith’s husband Fred “Sonic” Smith).

Despite its quick success, Television called it quits in 1978, in part because of Lloyd’s increasingly dire heroin addiction and mental health issues. Lloyd would release nine solo albums after Television, and he and Verlaine, despite a prickly relationship, occasionally reunited the band. Neither guitarist would ever match the success or influence of Television in its genre-building heyday.

Lloyd is survived by wife Sheila O’Keefe Lloyd; son Dylan; and brother Eric Lloyd.
