Time between pregnancies may shape how fast mothers age

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Each pregnancy adds biological age that takes months to come off, and birth spacing may decide how much a mother recovers before the next one.

Tests suggest that biological age rises during pregnancy and falls again in the months after birth. Researchers are now asking whether the time between pregnancies affects how fully those changes reverse.

Two Harvard researchers propose that the time a woman gets between pregnancies, not only how many she has, may shape how fast she ages for decades.

If the next pregnancy begins before that recovery is finished, the extra age may not be fully reversed, the authors wrote. The effect could then build with each closely spaced birth.

Stephen Albright and Jesse Poganik of Harvard Medical School and Brigham and Women’s Hospital laid out the idea in a review article. It pulls together studies from Europe, the United States, Israel and the Philippines.

The result is a hypothesis with testable predictions attached, not a new trial, and Poganik said the changes it describes don’t necessarily amount to damage.

“Our previous research has found that biological age transiently rises during pregnancy, peaking around the third trimester or at the time of delivery,” said Poganik, the corresponding author.

“If we accept the idea that these short-term changes in biological age occur, the question is how we can relate those changes to the overall trajectory of aging over the course of a lifetime.”

Biological age is an estimate of how much age-related change a body’s cells and tissues have accumulated, separate from the years on a birth certificate.

One way to estimate it is to read chemical tags on DNA that shift in a predictable pattern as people get older. Researchers call those readouts epigenetic clocks.

Run those clocks on blood drawn through a pregnancy and the number climbs as the pregnancy goes on, then partly falls after the birth.

Women on three continents show the same pattern, and so do mice. The authors wrote that this level of agreement is uncommon in their field.

The changes behind the number are physical. Blood volume rises by about half.

Cortisol, a stress hormone, climbs through pregnancy, and estrogen runs at roughly 100 times its usual level. The body also builds a new organ, the placenta, from nothing.

A detailed picture of the recovery comes from Israel, where researchers analyzed 76 routine lab tests from more than 300,000 pregnancies.

About half of those markers needed anywhere from three months to a year to return to where they had been before the pregnancy.

An aging clock built from those same lab results showed a dip before a large rise. In the first trimester a woman’s results looked about five years younger than her age.

From there they rose by about 20 years toward delivery, and it took roughly a year for them to recover.

Not every system recovers on the same schedule. Among more than 200,000 people, the reading from a clock built from wearable pulse sensors rose by about 3.5 years during pregnancy, then declined only slowly after birth.

Brain scans of first-time mothers showed gray matter volume falling across nearly all of the cortex during pregnancy and only partly recovering afterward.

The most direct evidence comes from a 2026 study that followed 130 women from pregnancy through nearly four years after the birth.

Among women who became pregnant again, the decline in their measured pace of aging was interrupted, and two other aging measures moved in the same direction.

Albright and Poganik call the time between a birth and the next conception the recovery window, and their hypothesis is simple to state.

Each pregnancy adds some biological age, and given enough time the body can undo at least part of it.

Under their hypothesis, another pregnancy could add to any biological-age increase that has not yet reversed.

Two women with the same number of children could therefore end up aging at different rates, depending on how far apart the births were.

The authors wrote that this is their central prediction, and it hasn’t been directly tested. No study has yet measured the spacing of pregnancies alongside biological age tracked for decades afterward.

Mothers of many children tend to die earlier in some populations and not in others, and the reasons remain unclear.

In the Finnish Twin Cohort, the risk of dying was highest among women with no births and women with five or more, and lowest among women with two or three.

Among adoptive parents, death rates declined as the number of adopted children increased.

A study of 2,356 women aged 35 to 74 linked more births to small increases in epigenetic age, measured well after their last pregnancy.

In 1,735 young women in the Philippines, with an average age of about 22, each additional pregnancy was linked to roughly two to three months of extra aging.

Spacing could explain why the count alone gives such mixed results, the authors wrote. Where women with many children tend to space them well, the count would carry little cost.

“It’s possible that this could change the overall trajectory of aging,” Poganik said.

“We know that some level of parity, or carrying a pregnancy to term, is actually beneficial to a person’s lifespan.”

The World Health Organization has recommended since 2005 that women wait at least 24 months after a live birth before conceiving again.

The advice rests on evidence linking short gaps to more preterm and low-weight births.

One review of 129 studies and nearly 47 million pregnancies linked gaps of 18 to 23 months to the best outcomes for babies. Gaps under six months were most consistently linked to harm for both baby and mother.

When a new pregnancy began six months after a birth, instead of 18 months, only women 35 and older faced a substantially higher chance of dying or becoming severely ill.

The age pattern fits their idea, Albright and Poganik wrote, because an older mother starts the recovery period biologically older, with less reserve.

Some factors were associated with differences in recovery.

Among about 120 women in one cohort, those who breastfed exclusively had a lower biological age three months after birth, whatever their body mass index.

A higher body mass index before pregnancy predicted a higher biological age afterward.

Sleep loss and chronic stress are suspected of slowing recovery too, though the authors found little or no research on either after birth.

Not every population fits. Tsimane women, who farm and forage for a living, have many children and little spare food.

Over a 10-year study their nutrition and immune markers largely held up however many children they had.

But those measures weren’t combined into a biological age, the authors wrote, so the cost they propose can’t be ruled out there.

Whether the extra age is wear, or the body remodeling itself for a demanding job, isn’t settled either. Clocks designed to tell the two apart are one way to find out.

Poganik said the work has a nearer-term purpose as well.

“What motivates me on a more near-term basis is addressing current clinical challenges, including the huge maternal mortality crisis in the United States compared with other industrialized countries,” he said.

“Any insights we can gain that can improve the health of mothers makes this a worthwhile research endeavor.”

The question underneath all of it is whether an incomplete recovery leaves a mother permanently older on the clocks, or whether the deficit fades given enough time.

Answering it means measuring biological age through several pregnancies, then following those women for 10 to 20 years, and no cohort has done that.

Until then, the 24-month guidance rests on pregnancy outcomes. Poganik said any advice based on a mother’s biological age would have to wait for that longer follow-up.

The review was published in the journal Trends in Endocrinology & Metabolism.

‘Ferritin Face’: What Low Iron Actually Does (and Doesn't Do) to Your Appearance

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Social media influencers claim that having a “ferritin face” may indicate you’re low in iron.

Ferritin is a protein that helps your body store iron. You need iron in order to make healthy red blood cells, which carry oxygen from your lungs to the rest of your body. Iron is also important for bone marrow, organs, healthy muscles, and brain development.

As “ferritin face” makes its way across social media platforms, users are describing several tell-tale signs, including:

Users have also shared images of their face before and after taking iron supplements or getting an iron infusion. Videos about “ferritin face” have garnered millions of views on various platforms.

However, medical professionals state that your appearance alone is not enough to diagnose iron deficiency.

“It’s important to understand that ferritin face is not a medical diagnosis, nor is it a confirmed symptom of iron deficiency. While some people with iron deficiency can appear pale due to low hemoglobin levels in their red blood cells, there are many other symptoms to consider,” said Daniel Atkinson, a general practitioner in the United Kingdom and clinical lead at Treated.

“Notably, these symptoms are also present in many different conditions, and their presence does not necessarily mean iron deficiency anemia or even low ferritin levels. Similar features are seen in people with iron processing and absorption issues, such as gluten intolerance, or in urgent sudden blood loss scenarios,” added Jonathan Jennings, MD, board certified internist at Medical Offices of Manhattan.

Iron deficiency can affect your skin and appearance. These signs are often subtle, but may include:

In some ways, the social media videos are correct. Your skin and face can change when your iron levels are low.

“The facial signs people point to, like pale skin and a washed-out look, mostly come from anaemia, when your haemoglobin has dropped. But iron deficiency and anaemia aren’t the same thing,” said Thom Phillips, a general practitioner in the United Kingdom and Chief Medical Officer at Forth. “By the time low iron shows on your face, your stores are usually close to empty.”

It is important to speak with a healthcare professional, who will run blood tests such as a complete blood count and ferritin levels.

It is also important not to take iron supplements without first checking with your healthcare professional, because having too much iron in your body can also cause negative effects.

Too little and too much iron could raise your risk of infection, which is why it’s important not to take supplements without medical supervision.

The signs and symptoms of iron deficiency can depend on whether you are anemic or not. Some of the early signs may include:

“These symptoms typically occur because your body produces fewer red blood cells, which results in less oxygen being delivered throughout your body, making you feel tired and weak,” said Atkinson.

While your facial appearance can be a sign that something isn’t right, healthcare professionals consider other symptoms and how you feel overall.

They also rely on blood tests to confirm the diagnosis and guide treatment.

“The misinformation is the idea that you can spot it yourself. That leads to two problems: people with low iron who look fine assume they’re OK, and people who look tired start taking iron supplements they don’t need,” said Phillips.

Atkinson also noted that factors such as different settings and environmental conditions can affect how your face appears.

That being said, the “ferritin face” trend may help bring awareness to certain symptoms and when to get them checked.

“[This] trend may raise some awareness about iron deficiency anemia and how low iron levels can impact daily life,” said Atkinson.

“Fatigue and lightheadedness are common side effects of low iron, but it manifests in other ways too, such as paler skin or increased hair shedding, which can be concerning for many people. If you experience these symptoms, you should let your doctor know so they can help treat the condition appropriately,” he said.

Ordine: "Milan, servirebbe un leader. Amorim ha svelato chi potrebbe essere"

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Sulle pagine del Corriere dello Sport, Franco Ordine ha commentato così il momento del Milan dopo il brutto ko in Europa League contro il Ferencvaros: "Per identificare il 'virus' che sta mortificando il nuovo Milan di Cardinale e Amorim bisogna tornare all’origine di questo ultimo ciclo di alti e bassi che non possono certo autorizzare sogni di gloria, tipo quelli ascoltati dopo il derby di sabato scorso. E per ottenere una ricostruzione attendibile bisogna rivolgersi a chi ha frequentato bene Milanello e ha fornito di recente una preziosa testimonianza. Il riferimento all’intervista di Stefano Pioli, tecnico dello scudetto del 2022 esaltato poi con la semifinale di Champions League nella stagione successiva, è indispensabile. Raccontò Pioli al direttore Zazzaroni: 'L’arrivo di Ibra è stato decisivo perché ha innalzato l’intensità degli allenamenti ogni giorno'. Tutto vero, documentato. Per cogliere la differenza basta riannodare i fili della memoria al dicembre precedente all’arrivo dal mercato di gennaio di Ibra e Kjaer: infatti il Milan aveva subito una lezione storica a Bergamo dall’Atalanta di Gasperini, incassò una sconfitta umiliante, 5 gol e in porta c’era Donnarumma, non il giovane debuttante Lorenzo Torriani che pure mercoledì notte ha evitato che il risultato diventasse più pesante.

Improvvisamente quello stesso Milan, nei mesi successivi, intraprese una nuova strada e fu l’inizio di una rincorsa virtuosa che si concluse appunto nel maggio del 2022 con l’appuntamento di Reggio Emilia contro il Sassuolo. Badate bene: durante le ultime decisive settimane del torneo, Ibra era fuori per infortunio, il fisico cominciava a dare segnali di usura. Eppure la sua presenza quotidiana a Milanello, durante gli allenamenti, continuava a dare la sveglia, in particolare a qualche esponente di giovanissima anagrafe ed esperienza, tipo Leao, oppure Theo Hernandez o ancora Maignan che non a caso risultarono determinanti con una serie di gol e parate, contro Atalanta e Fiorentina per esempio.

Oggi a chi toccherebbe l’ingrato compito di prendere la frusta e cominciare a farla fischiare tutti i giorni a Milanello? Probabilmente a uno che non può essere considerato un veterano capace, col proprio carisma, di farsi ascoltare dalla truppa e di trascinare anche i più pigri. L’identità è stata svelata dallo stesso Amorim durante lo sfogo di mercoledì notte a San Siro. Ha detto: 'Pavlovic è pronto a morire in ogni allenamento!'. Ecco il riferimento. Perché il resto della truppa rossonera non è pronto a tenere alta l’asticella tutti i giorni. E a dirlo ancora una volta è lo stesso Amorim che non ha mancato di trasferire nelle sue parole una vera e propria denuncia: 'Non siamo ancora pronti per essere competitivi sui due fronti!'".

The 8 Best Sweater Polos for Men, According to Style Editors

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Your next best-dressed moment starts from the collar down.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

I CONSIDER MYSELF the king of sweaters. I love a good collared shirt, so polos are my go-to in the spring and summer. But once fall and winter roll around, I’m all about sweaters. Even better: a sweater polo. It’s the perfect mashup that combines the collar and button placket of a classic polo with the knit construction of a sweater, giving you a little more texture and warmth than your standard cotton pique. The best sweater polos offer that same versatility. You can dress things up or lean super casual depending on the day and occasion. And not all have to be long-sleeved either.

Short-sleeve versions are also great for tropical vacations or layers under a blazer for the office. Either way, they’re an easy way to switch up your usual sweater rotation from the same ol’ crews or turtlenecks. The Men’s Health fashion crew and I have tried more than 30 over the last three years, paying close attention to fit, fabric, comfort, and versatility to find ones that deserve a spot in your closet.

For the past five years, I’ve tested more than 25 sweater polos, but Uniqlo is easily one of my favorites. It checks all the boxes when it comes to excellent quality, fit, versatility and what really seals the deal—affordability. At just $50, you’re getting a premium knit that could pass for something more expensive. It’s made from a cotton-blend yarn in a structured Milano rib knit that looks and feels more like woven cloth than a stretchy tee. This one feels dense yet comfortable on the skin. I especially love the buttonless, open V-neck collar, which gives some breathing room and makes the whole thing feel a little relaxed.

In terms of fit, it’s roomy through the chest and flexible through the arms without looking oversized. I’ve worn it with jeans and dressed it up with tailored trousers and loafers for dinner. Plus, it comes in plenty of colors that you can rotate between office days and the weekend. After more than three wash cycles, it’s held up nicely. Just be sure to toss it in a mesh laundry bag to keep the knit in good shape.

You don’t have to spend a fortune to get your hands on a good sweater polo. Check out this one from Coofandy. It’s one of the lighter knits that I’ve tested, so it’s wearable through all the seasons. Yes, even in the summertime when the temps stayed around 80 degrees, I didn’t break a sweat at the backyard party. The polyester fabric feels soft against the skin and has a little stretch, which makes it easy to move around in.

It also has a V-neck polo collar to give it that laid-back vibe, while the ribbed cuffs and hem pull everything in for a more fitted, body-contouring look. Styling-wise, you’ve got plenty of options. I’d wear it untucked with jeans or chinos for an everyday fit, but you can also tuck it in pants with a belt for a day on the golf course. This one is not machine washable, so I’d recommend taking a trip to the dry cleaners.

I love mixing different textures in my polo sweater collection, so things don’t start looking too plain. That’s when I add in pieces like Charles Tyrwhitt’s textured knit to give some depth and dimension to my outfit without going overboard with loud patterns or prints. It’s made from 100 percent cotton that’s been garment-washed for a softer, broken-in feel, so I’m not dealing with a stiff, scratchy sweater.

The midweight material is substantial enough to wear on its own during cooler days but breathable enough to layer under a jacket without feeling too bundled up. I’d wear the petrol-blue shade with cream trousers and brown suede loafers for a sharp office look, or pair it with dark denim and boots for dinner. I’ve washed it more than four times and haven’t seen it shrink or pill, but my advice is to let it air dry.

When my brain can’t function in the morning to put an outfit together, I just rely on Abercrombie’s knit. It’s made from 100 percent merino wool, which sounds like it might be a recipe for itchiness, but surprisingly, it feels soft and smooth against the skin. I also dig the classic three-button collar, which gives it a slightly dressier edge than your average crewneck. The fit is also a nice balance, with enough room in the chest and stomach, in case I wolfed down a burger.

Now, I will say that the wool can get a little toasty, especially if you’re moving around a lot. But if your office blasts AC like it’s the middle of Antarctica, you should be just fine. Styling-wise, I’d wear it with straight-leg jeans and sneakers for a casual Friday, or swap in chinos and loafers when I want to look a little more put-together. It only comes in neutrals, which should be easy to pair with anything in your wardrobe.

There’s something about cashmere that makes even the simplest outfit feel a little more luxurious. And if you’re going to invest in a cashmere sweater polo, Quince is a great place to start. This one is made from 100 percent Grade-A Mongolian cashmere, with fine fibers that give the knit an incredibly soft, smooth feel. The 12-gauge construction gives it a refined look without making it feel overly chunky, and the ribbed cuffs and hem help keep its shape. And when I don’t want to wear a dress shirt, this is a great alternative. Throw it on with tailored trousers and loafers for the office, or pair it with dark jeans and boots for dinner.

I’m a big fan of merino wool because it gives you the warmth of a sweater minus all the extra bulk. Banana Republic uses it for this polo, which is a solid option that can transition from the office straight to dinner. No matter how toasty a room gets, I’m never sweating in it. The fabric stays lightweight and breathable, and has enough warmth to come in handy when temperatures drop.

I also like that the fit is pretty straightforward, with slightly dropped shoulders and ribbed cuffs and hem that give it a relaxed but clean finish. The buttoned collar makes it feel a little dressier than your average knit, especially when paired with chinos or tailored trousers. And unlike some wool sweaters that require a trip to the dry cleaner, this one is machine washable, which is always a plus in my book.

Cos gave the classic sweater polo a completely different spin with a buttonless collar that sits closed rather than opening into the usual V-neck. It’s a subtle detail, but one that makes people take a second look at my outfit. This neckline creates a cleaner, more sophisticated look without being overly flashy. I’d pair it with wide-leg trousers and leather loafers for a sharp office fit, or throw it on with relaxed jeans and sneakers for the weekend.

I especially like this kind of silhouette for guys who prefer a minimalist wardrobe but still want something with a little personality. Now, at around $130, it’s definitely on the pricier side, especially when you can find sweater polos for half that amount. But I’d argue you’re paying for a design that’s a refreshing departure from the sweater polos you’re used to seeing, and chances are, you won’t spot five other guys wearing the same thing.

Not every sweater polo needs long sleeves, especially when you can rock one under a blazer. Take this retro-inspired short-sleeve style from Bonobos, which I can wear year-round. My favorite way to style it is under a structured blazer with tailored trousers. It’s dressy enough to skip the traditional button-down shirt, whether you’re heading to an important meeting or a cocktail reception.

It’s also a great piece to pack for vacation, especially if you’re traveling somewhere tropical and need something sharp for a resort dinner. In that case, ditch the blazer and pair it with linen pants or even shorts for a more laid-back look. Plus, the breathable cotton fabric won’t make scorching temps feel even more unbearable. I wore it on a recent trip to Cancún and didn’t break much of a sweat. Depending on your personal style, Bonobos offers this sweater polo in a variety of colors, prints, and textures, so there’s plenty to choose from.

10 Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Tips For The First-Time Player

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Nintendo Switch 2The Legend of ZeldaThe Legend of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time

A happy Zelda weekend to you all! While a lot of us are going into this latest Zelda release confident and comfortable in our ancient (or, in my case, recent) knowledge of how to play Ocarina of Time, this game is 28 whole years old and there are many, many people who have never played it, don’t remember it, or weren’t even alive when it came out. And whew, this is a very different Zelda game than some of the more recent ones!

So I put together some tips for you all. Hopefully at least a few of them help you have a smoother adventure in Hyrule this weekend. Enjoy the ride, folks, this one’s a hell of a classic:

In Kokiri Forest, when you first get control of Link, you can immediately head out of your house and down the path to the left to reach the Know-It-Alls. The characters inside the house will give you some useful tips, while the fellow outside will show you some basic combat moves including rolling and dodging. He’ll then point you in the direction of a sword, after which point you can return and learn how to do the mighty jump attack (more on that momentarily). These are (almost) all of the combat moves you’ll need to know for the whole game, so it’s good to master them early.

The jump attack is powerful, doing double the damage of a regular swing. It’s also a little tricky to execute, requiring two button inputs instead of just one…seemingly. You can actually also do this move by tilting forward on the control stick (while targeting) and just pressing A. I recommend doing the attack this way, and I recommend using this attack aÂ lot.

Everyone in Hyrule wants you to follow the main story path. They are, in fact, very worried you’ll miss it. So you’ll constantly hear NPCs directing you: go to the Great Deku Tree! Go to Hyrule Castle! Well…maybe don’t. At least, not right away. Ocarina of Time has tons of secrets, items, and just generally stuff to do that isn’t on the main path, so don’t feel obligated to stick to it. Even in Kokiri Forest, there are extra items you can obtain early if you just wander around for a while. You also can (and should!) backtrack to areas you’ve previously visited at just about any time!

Once you reach Castle Town, you’ll start to run into minigames. Ocarina of Time has a lot of them, and many are located in shops off the square. In some Zelda games, you don’t really need to do the minigames on offer, and technically that’s true in this one. But I recommend you at least give them a whirl. Some of them have great rewards, like bigger storage bags for certain items, or empty bottles. Don’t be afraid to goof off a little on your adventure!

Which, speaking of empty bottles. these are by far your most precious resource. Get as many as you can as soon as you can. If someone’s got a bottle for you, drop everything and do what they say. Empty bottles are used to hold all sorts of things: items you need for quests and to unlock secret paths, potions to refill your health, and fairies that can revive you automatically if you die. There are a total of five empty bottles in the game: try to get them all!

It can be tempting to just hack and slash your way to victory in Ocarina of Time, but some enemies aren’t so easily taken down. If you’re struggling, it’s good to test out some of your other items on enemies and see which, if any, have an effect. You might be surprised what a well-placed hookshot or boomerang can do, or what you can accomplish with some Deku Nuts. A lot of enemies can be stunned, knocked away, or even killed from a distance, no close combat required.

I mean, really, you should just do this anyway. The writing in Ocarina of Time is often silly and worth reading on its own, but characters you speak to will also frequently give you hints about what to do next, or even about secrets you may not have found otherwise. Characters will also adjust their dialogue over time depending on what is going on in the game, so circle back with folks even if you think you’ve heard all they have to say.

And yes, Navi can help, too. She can be a bit grating, but her tips on fighting monsters are a huge help.

Again, this one seems pretty basic, but sometimes you’ll be stuck in a dungeon and have no idea how to solve a puzzle to proceed. I cannot emphasize enough how often the answer is just to look,Â really look around you. Look for weird symbols on the walls, or bits of floor that look funny. Look for fire, or places to light things on fire. Look for switches, or platforms directly below you that you didn’t see before. Look at how things connect to one another. Look underwater. Most of the time if you’re stuck, it’s because you’re not seeing some key element you need to move forward…and often, those things are hidden for a reason.

New to the remake of Ocarina of Time are fairy symbols, odd glowing runes you’ll find inscribed on walls or pillars or other out-of-the-way surfaces as you explore. You can use Navi to examine them. They’ll often give you hints about secrets, or other little bonuses, for interacting with them properly. Again, looking around pays off!

Finally, gold skulltulas. You won’t necessarily know these are important when you first start the game, but they absolutely are. There are 100 of these in the game total, and you get rewards for reaching certain milestones of collecting them. Many of these rewards are very, very good. You’ll want to listen for a scritchy-scratching noise as you explore, that indicates there’s one nearby. You can also find them by rolling into trees that seem to be shedding their leaves. In towns, you’ll only ever find them at night (in dungeons, they’re around all the time). Grab as many as you can!

The Cuccos are the chickens. Don’t attack them. You can pick them up, carry them around, and throw them. But don’t HIT them.

The incoming class of U.S. senators is full of historic firsts

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Josh Turek, Peggy Flanagan, and Dr. Abdul El-Sayed are among several Democratic Senators-elect who made history on Election Day 2026. Scott Olson/Getty Images; Dymanh Chhoun/Sahan Journal/CatchLight via Getty Images; Sylvia Jarrus for NPR hide caption

When the newest class of U.S. senators are sworn into office in January, it will include some of America's firsts.

The 2026 midterm election resulted in a significant shift in the landscape of Congress as Democrats won in a big way up and down the ballot. While not all the races have been called, Democrats have flipped more than 40 House seats and picked up 7 Senate seats, giving them control of both chambers of Congress.

Here are some of the new faces to expect in the Senate who made history on Election Day.

Josh Turek speaks during an election night event Tuesday, Nov. 3, 2026, in Des Moines, Iowa. Ellen Schmidt/AP hide caption

Turek will be the first Paralympian to serve in the Senate. The Democrat is a two-time gold medalist in wheelchair basketball. Turek is paraplegic and uses a wheelchair due to spina bifida. He beat Republican Ashley Hinson in the general election.

Julia Letlow speaks to media during an election night watch party on May 16, 2026, in Baton Rouge, La. Matthew Hinton/AP hide caption

Letlow has now made history twice. She was the first Republican woman elected to the U.S. House from Louisiana in 2021. Letlow ran for the House in a special election to replace her late husband, Congressman Luke Letlow, who died of COVID-19 in 2020. Now, Louisiana voters have decided to send her to the Senate, where she will be the first Republican woman from the state to serve. She will succeed Republican Sen. Bill Cassidy, who Letlow beat in the primary election.

Dr. Abdul El-Sayed visits a polling precinct on the campus of the University of Michigan on November 3, 2026 in Ann Arbor, Mich. Salwan Georges/Getty Images hide caption

The incoming Democratic senator is the son of Egyptian immigrants and is the first Muslim person elected to the upper chamber. El-Sayed is an epidemiologist and the former health director for the city of Detroit. He beat Republican Mike Rogers for the hotly contested seat.

Peggy Flanagan walks with her husband as they arrive to vote at a polling location on Election Day in St. Louis Park, Minn. on November 3, 2026. Tim Evans/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

Minnesota's Democratic Lt. Governor is the first Native American elected to the Senate. Flanagan is a member of the White Earth Band of Ojibwe. She also made political history more than once. She was also the first woman of color elected to state office in Minnesota. Flanagan will replace outgoing Democratic Sen. Tina Smith.

Chris Pappas walks onstage to deliver his acceptance speech at a watch party at the Puritan Backroom on November 3, 2026 in Manchester, New Hampshire. Joseph Prezioso/Getty Images hide caption

Pappas is the first openly gay man elected to the Senate and is also the first gay man to represent the state of New Hampshire. He served four terms in the House and beat Republican John Sununu in the election.

Dr. Annie Andrews takes the stage at an election night event in Charleston, S.C. on November 3, 2026. Mic Smith/AP hide caption

In one of the biggest election night upsets, Andrews pulled out an underdog win in deep red South Carolina. Andrews is the first woman elected to the U.S. senate from the Palmetto State. She beat Darline Graham, who was appointed to replace her late brother Lindsey Graham.

Democratic candidates James Talarico and Adam Hamilton won their Senate races in Texas and Kansas, respectively. Ronaldo Schemidt/AFP via Getty Images; Charlie Riedel/AP hide caption

Both states are reliably Republican and both states are sending pastors to Washington. This midterm election voters broke with the status quo and elected Democrats to represent them in the Senate.

In Texas, voters selected Democrat James Talarico as their next senator. It is the first time the Longhorn State has sent a Democrat to the chamber since 1988. Talarico won against Republican nominee Ken Paxton.

After nearly 100 years, Kansas elected a democratic senator to the chamber. Adam Hamilton will be the first Democrat that has served the state since 1932. He fended off a tough race with incumbent Republican Roger Marshall.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/pharma/cms-makes-it-official-codifying-medicare-drug-price-negotiations-rolled-out-under-ira" hreflang="en">CMS makes it official, codifying Medicare drug price negotiations established under IRA</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
CMS makes it official, codifying Medicare drug price negotiations established under IRA" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Against a backdrop of repeated industry challenges in court, the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) is locking in the drug price negotiation framework established under the Biden-era Inflation Reduction Act (IRA).

In turn, the process under which CMS selects certain high-spend Medicare Part B or Part D drugs and negotiates lower prices with manufacturers will carry on into 2029 and beyond. The rule codifies and expands policies previously established through guidance for the initial 2026-28 period.

The final rule is slated to go into effect Dec. 5, CMS said in a fact sheet accompanying Thursday’s announcement. By Feb. 1 of next year, CMS will select up to 20 drugs covered under Medicare Part B or Part D for price negotiations taking effect in 2029, with the potential to also select medicines included in previous rounds for renegotiation.

That upcoming group of drugs will make up the fourth cycle of pricing negotiations, with negotiated prices taking effect in 2029. CMS asserted that the rule gives both patients and drugmakers “a clear path forward as the agency builds on the program’s results and continues negotiating prices for high-cost drugs.”

The initial rounds of price negotiations have already included a slew of prominent brands, including Novo's semaglutide franchise of Wegovy and Ozempic and cancer meds like Bristol Myers Squibb’s Pomalyst, Pfizer’s Ibrance and AstraZeneca’s Calquence.

Meanwhile, the latest tranche of drugs selected earlier this year for negotiation, whose new prices would go into effect in 2028, includes notable entrants like Gilead’s HIV treatment Biktarvy, AbbVie’s Botox and Eli Lilly’s diabetes drug Trulicity, to name just a few.

Beyond codifying the framework for price negotiations in 2029 and subsequent years, CMS has also taken steps to fine-tune several elements of the program.

Namely, the rule finalizes a policy on how CMS classifies certain fixed-combination drugs, with the aim of addressing “potential program integrity concerns” that could encourage drugmakers to evade negotiated prices by steering patients toward new product formulations.

The rule also includes a provision establishing a temporary “small biotech floor” that prevents CMS from offering or accepting a counteroffer below a specified price floor for certain eligible small biotech drugs in 2029 and 2030. It also codifies two policies related to Medicare Part D intended to ensure plan coverage of medicines with a maximum fair price in effect and limit the prices plans pay pharmacies and other dispensing entities.

The IRA and the Medicare price negotiations it empowered have been frequent targets of the biopharma industry, which has broadly alleged that the policy is unconstitutional and that the pricing talks aren’t as voluntary as advertised. But attempts by major drugmakers and powerful trade organizations like the Pharmaceutical Research and Manufacturers of America (PhRMA) to push back on Medicare price negotiations have repeatedly stalled out.

In the interim, the U.S. government has made a separate and more recent push to rein in high prescription drug costs by leaning on President Donald Trump’s “most favored nation” (MFN) strategy.

“President Trump directed us to lower drug costs for Americans using every tool at our disposal,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in a statement announcing the new rule. “This rule provides additional tools to help CMS negotiate better deals, achieve greater savings for taxpayers and beneficiaries, and improve health outcomes for seniors who rely on the Medicare Prescription Drug Program.”

MFN, for its part, has yielded at times, controversial deals with more than two dozen drugmakers, generally involving commitments to price new medicines in line with prices in high-income comparator countries and offer MFN pricing on certain existing drugs through Medicaid.

Through those deals, which have also included investment commitments in the U.S., drugmakers have been able to secure temporary exemptions from the Trump administration’s threatened pharmaceutical import tariffs.

Around this week’s midterm elections, several Big Pharma executives have signaled that they expect little immediate change to their thinking around MFN. Nevertheless, the lack of transparency surrounding the deals has already drawn scrutiny from Democratic lawmakers. With Democrats set to control both chambers of Congress beginning in January, the party may soon press harder to uncover the details of those agreements.

The Cervélo Soloist—The Race-Inspired Road Bike That Makes Sense for the Way Most Riders Actually Ride

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A race-inspired road bike built for riders who want one fast machine for climbs, descents, and pacelines.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. How we test gear.

Over the last few years, many bike brands have tried to combine the benefits of their aero bike platform and climbing bikes into an all-arounder. When I managed a Trek store, the brand launched the Madone Gen8. Trek intended it as the next logical step in its road bike lineup by combining the Madone’s speed with the Émonda’s low weight. Other examples of this trend include the Specialized Tarmac and Cannondale’s SuperSix Evo.

Cervélo touts the Soloist as its do-everything road platform. While you’ll see its S5 and R5 at races like the Tour de France, the Soloist probably won’t show up at the starting line of any Grand Tours. That’s because the Cervélo-sponsored Visma-Lease a Bike team doesn’t need a third road bike; it already has race-winning aero bikes and dedicated climbing bikes for its athletes. Most days those riders use the S5, and on the steepest alpine climbs they can go with the R5.

But most of us don’t have the money, space, or desire to own multiple road bikes to choose between depending on the route we’re riding that day. One road bike that flies up climbs, inspires confidence on descents, and holds well in a paceline is the key for many riders. After almost three months of riding, I found Cervélo’s Soloist fits right into that sweet spot.

The Soloist mixes the pure speed of the S5 with the uphill float of the R5. It sits between both models in weight—about a pound heavier than the R5 and about two pounds lighter than the S5. Visually, the Soloist looks like a midpoint between both bikes. It brings the lines of the R5 while tying in the aero aesthetics of the S5, but the geometry charts paint a different picture. The Soloist leans toward the S5 when looking at the geometry charts for each model. When shopping the Cervélo lineup consider the pricing. The Ultegra Di2 model of the S5 and R5 are both listed at $10,100. The Soloist gives the benefits of each bike, and saves you almost $2,000 for the Ultegra Di2 model.

The Soloist lineup ranges from $6,800 for the SRAM Rival AXS model up to $11,000 for the SRAM Red AXS–equipped bike. I tested the Shimano Ultegra Di2-equipped version, which sells for $8,250. This bike comes stock with a 4iii power meter, an integrated carbon bar and stem, and carbon wheels. My 54cm test bike weighed 15.9 pounds without the bottles or the pedals—pretty respectable for an aero-ish bike with Ultegra components.

Straight out of the box, it was almost ready to roll. Although Cervélo equips this bike with many parts I’d choose for my own bike, I swapped out the stock 700x29c Vittoria Corsa Next TLR G2.0 tires after only a few rides. The rubber was okay on flatter rides, but lacked the grip and ride feel I wanted on twisting descents.

Installing 28mm Pirelli P Zero TLR Race tires was exactly what the Soloist needed to elevate my confidence on descents. The tires made the bike feel locked-in when leaning into turns (I even bagged a couple of Strava trophies on our local descents).

The Soloist ships with aero bottles. Cervélo claims the bottles save 4.3 watts compared to standard round bottles. However, that savings doesn’t matter if you’re wasting energy fumbling with your bottles. The aero ones fit so tight in their cages I almost fell on my first ride trying to remove one.

Even after weeks of testing, I didn’t get used to these bidons. I’ve tried other aero bottle setups and haven’t had the same issue. Plus, the stock aero cages that come with the bike don’t pair as well with traditional round bottles as some brands’ aero cages.

For my first ride, I dove right into the deep end by taking the Soloist up Fiddler’s Elbow. This notorious mile-and-a-half climb gains a little over 700 feet with grades that exceed 20 percent near its summit.

I’ve done this climb at least twenty times on my Trek Madone, with gearing that is a bit more favorable for climbing than the Soloist’s 36x34t lowest gear. But even with taller gearing, the Cervélo floated up the climb. The bike’s geometry put me in a comfortable position, and I appreciated the Soloist’s lower weight on steeper parts of the climb.

But the Soloist didn’t only excel on climbs. The backside of Fiddler’s features a twisting, high-speed descent where maintaining your line is critical, and the bike felt stable. Once I reached the bottom, I had rolling farm roads back to the office, similar to where I did the bulk of my daily rides with this bike.

The Soloist shines on undulating rural roads and group rides. Sometimes our office lunch rides lean toward a spicier pace, with a single or double paceline of up to eight riders. The Soloist is calm, collected, and easy to ride in groups, and its aerodynamics helped when I started to fall off the back and had to catch back to the group.

Lastly, since you don’t always ride on butter-smooth tarmac, I also tested the Soloist on roads outside of its typical scope. The bike felt confident, even on some of the chunky gravel roads. However, I don’t recommend doing this too often with the Soloist; it doesn’t have enough tire clearance for actual gravel tread.

The bike proved easy to ride and live with. I wish I didn’t have to send the Soloist back to Cervélo, as I’d gladly spend more hours on this bike.

Bicycling’s Test Editor, Dan Chabanov, felt like the previous model tried to mix the benefits of the R5 and S5, but missed the mark. “I found it pretty unremarkable. Not in a bad way, but compared to other bikes at the time it just kind of split everything down the middle. It wasn’t very aero but it was aero-ish. It wasn’t very light, but it wasn’t overly heavy. It did a lot of stuff well, but it never really wowed me,” says Chabanov.

The new model irons out those wrinkles. Cervélo shaved nearly a pound off the Ultegra model and added more aero features from the S5—notably the integrated handlebar and stem combination.

I’m impressed after a few months using the new-generation Soloist as my primary road bike. It climbs better than other comparable bikes I’ve tried, holds a pocket in a paceline well, and descends like a banshee. Many brands have tried to blend the watt savings of an aero bike and the weight of a climbing bike. Cervélo took a swing with the previous Soloist model, but refined the previous generation to create a well-blended machine.

This bike is the great choice for everyone from the local crit ripper to the Saturday coffee shop ride champion. If you get this bike, then pick up a pair of Pirelli P Zero Race tires or Continental GP 5000 tires to unlock its full potential. This bike is at the top of my list for those of us who want to have one road bike at home that can rip every ride.

Jason Griffin is Bicycling's Senior Health and Fitness Editor, bringing more than a decade of fitness experience and years in the cycling industry to every story. A former personal trainer and bike shop manager, he's lined up for crits, tackled enduros, but prefers getting lost on multi-day bikepacking adventures. Lately though, his toughest balancing act has been fitting trainer rides between family life and the next adventure.

Princess Kate’s Chic Outdoorsy Look Takes a Cue From Princess Diana

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

And other British royals who came before her

Trends may come and go, and generations may change, but one thing you can count on is that royal traditions will live on.

For years, the British royals have been dressing for England’s foggy, rainy weather, and for many of those years, they’ve reached to Barbour for appropriate outerwear.

The heritage brand, based in South Shields, England, is known primarily for its preppy outdoorsy jackets. And royal family fans have included none other than Queen Elizabeth II and Princess Diana. Inspired by their wardrobes, Princess Catherine and even Duchess Meghan have adopted the label.

(The company has long held a Royal Warrant, which is “granted as a mark of recognition to people or companies who have regularly supplied goods or services” to the royals, per the family’s website.)

Some favorites of Kate include the waxed defence jacket in olive, the Barbour x Alexa Chung Elfie coat in olive and khaki, the puffer-style Longshore quilted jacket in navy and marigold, and other diamond-quilted styles.

Today, in a series of photos shared on Instagram, the Princess of Wales wore the Alexa Chung collab style in sand and chocolate, layering it over a chunky navy sweater with a ribbed neck, and styling it with light-wash blue jeans.

To further elevate the look, she popped on some dangly diamond earrings and her diamond-and-sapphire engagement ring from husband Prince William (which once belonged to Lady Di).

She wore the ensemble to visit pioneering mental health charity A Dose of Nature. And it’s certainly one fit for a future queen of England.

An herbal extract may help people cut back on binge drinking

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Researchers tested an herbal approach to cutting back on alcohol, with some benefits persisting three months after treatment ended.

Prescription medicines can help treat alcohol use disorder, but they remain rarely used.

A new clinical trial tested another approach: kudzu root extract taken only on days when people expected to drink heavily.

In the trial, adults who used kudzu that way had fewer binge-drinking days than adults who took a placebo, a look-alike capsule with nothing active in it.

The difference was modest, and three months after treatment ended, it was still there.

Glenn-Milo Santos, a professor of community health systems at the University of California, San Francisco, led the work.

His team enrolled 120 adults with alcohol use disorder, the medical term for drinking that a person struggles to control.

Every volunteer also got a short counseling session each week, so kudzu was tested on top of that support.

“Binge drinking is incredibly common, but we don’t have many tools to offer people who want to cut back,” said Santos.

“Our goal is to expand that toolkit. What’s exciting about this approach is that people can use it when they know they may be at risk of heavy drinking, rather than taking a medication every day.”

For men, a binge means five or more drinks on one occasion. For women, it means four or more.

In a 2022 federal health survey, 17% of U.S. adults said they had binged in the past month, and those who did averaged about 4.6 binges a month.

Binge drinking accounts for more than half of the roughly 80,000 U.S. deaths each year blamed on excessive drinking.

Treatment hasn’t kept up with that toll. Four medicines are approved in the U.S. for alcohol use disorder, yet fewer than 10% of people in the U.S. with the disorder have ever received one.

Worry about side effects keeps some patients and the people treating them away from those drugs, the authors wrote.

Herbal supplements are sold over the counter and are often seen as having fewer side effects, which is part of why the team chose an herbal option.

Kudzu has been used as medicine in China and Japan since the 6th century.

Compounds from its root lowered drinking in animal studies. In one small human study, 14 heavy drinkers who took kudzu extract for a week drank fewer beers in a lab session.

The trial ran from 2019 to 2024 at the San Francisco Department of Public Health. The team recruited volunteers in sexual health clinics, needle exchanges, bars, and on social media.

Everyone had to have had sex while drinking in the previous three months, and 16% were living with HIV.

People with another severe substance use disorder, most often involving cocaine or methamphetamine, were excluded from the trial.

Half the volunteers got kudzu extract capsules, and half got identical placebo capsules.

Neither the volunteers nor the staff knew who received which capsules. Participants’ guesses at the end of the trial suggested that the blinding had worked.

Each person was told to take a dose at least 2.5 hours before a session where they expected to drink heavily.

That lead time came from a 2015 study of 20 adults, in which one dose taken that far ahead cut drinking in a lab session.

The pill bottles had caps that logged every opening. On average, people took about 2.7 doses a week, and they took one on about three in four of the days they expected to binge.

Treatment lasted 12 weeks, and the counseling focused on cutting back gradually rather than quitting outright.

At the start, both groups averaged 2.15 binge days a week. By the final treatment visit, the kudzu group was down to 1.42, while the placebo group was at 2.06.

Across all 12 weeks, the researchers estimated that people taking kudzu had about 27% fewer binge days than people taking the placebo.

The kudzu group also reported more weeks without any binge drinking.

In this group, the share of weeks that included at least one binge fell from 88% to 58%. In the placebo group, it went from 88% to 81%.

Side effects were about as common in one group as in the other. Nausea was reported by seven kudzu users and two placebo users, a difference that could be due to chance.

One kudzu user developed heart inflammation 82 days after their final treatment visit, and the team judged it unrelated to the supplement.

Kudzu didn’t clearly lower the total number of drinks people had in a month. It also didn’t change how often urine tests came back positive for alcohol.

The urine tests pick up drinking from the past one to three days.

About 60% of the kudzu group’s samples were positive at the start of the trial, and about 60% were positive at the end.

People on kudzu didn’t stop drinking, but they reported fewer binges. Their cravings didn’t change either, and neither did their depression scores.

A blood test for steady heavy drinking over the previous few weeks showed no clear difference overall.

Among the people who took kudzu at least 2.7 times a week, though, fewer tested positive than in the placebo group, and their binge days fell further.

The authors cautioned against reading too much into that subgroup. Frequent dosing may have picked out people who drank the most to begin with, and those drinkers had the most room to cut back.

The volunteers came back three months after their 12 weeks ended. Treatment had stopped by then, yet the kudzu group still reported about 32% fewer binge days than the placebo group.

The researchers can’t yet say why. They pointed to research on breaks such as Dry January, where some studies have found that a month without alcohol is followed by lasting cutbacks.

Drinking less during treatment may have given people a chance to rethink their relationship with alcohol and gain confidence in their ability to cut back, the team wrote.

Further studies, including interviews with participants, would be needed to test that idea.

Everyone in the trial was recruited in the San Francisco Bay Area.

Participants reported their drinking through computer-based interviews, and the researchers also collected blood and urine samples.

The weekly counseling may have helped the placebo group cut back too, which could have shrunk the gap. How kudzu performs without that support hasn’t been tested.

How kudzu works is still an open question. One idea is that its main compound, puerarin, widens blood vessels and changes how the body takes in alcohol. That may make the first drinks feel stronger and delay the urge for another.

The trial’s extract came from a commercial supplier, but store products differ in how they’re made and how strong they are.

Santos and his colleagues cautioned that store-bought supplements may not match the standardized capsules used in the trial.

Nor did the trial test whether kudzu helps people who want to quit completely, rather than cut back on heavy drinking.

The kudzu study was a mid-stage trial, and the authors wrote that larger, later-stage trials may be warranted to show whether targeted kudzu holds up as a treatment.

The full study was published in the journal American Journal of Psychiatry.

Did You Get Blamed for Everything in Your Family?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 6, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

The first time I learned about the concept of an “identified patient,” it immediately resonated with me. So many of my clients have had the experience of being the “problem” in their family. They were scapegoated, blamed, and gaslit for struggling with their mental health. Often a highly sensitive or neurodivergent child (or both), they felt the family’s turbulence deeply, unable to pretend everything was fine and ignore all the chaos.

The idea of an identified patient is used in family therapy, where sessions involve the whole family, not just the person (often a child or adolescent) who is struggling. It’s common in these sessions for other family members to say, “Well, they have all these problems, but we’re just fine.”

If this resonates with you, it’s likely you were blamed by your family for being “difficult,” “too sensitive,” or “selfish,” because you often got upset as a child. I even hear these descriptions of babies who cried a lot, didn’t sleep through the night, or struggled with colic, as if a baby could somehow control their behavior to be easy or difficult.

It’s true that babies are especially sensitive to their parents’ moods, facial expressions, and tone of voice, so they can react strongly to, say, a depressed mother or angry father. But babies can’t express their discomfort in words, so they cry—their only means of communicating distress. Even older children will struggle to express themselves verbally, as their emotions are so intense and the prefrontal cortex—the brain region we need for complex thought and emotional regulation—remains undeveloped until well into adolescence. That’s why babies and children of all ages are a lightning rod for problems in the family.

This is especially true for kids with highly sensitive nervous systems, because they pick up all the negative energy in a room, whether expressed or unexpressed. It hurts them, like a throbbing headache, invisible but impossible to ignore.

Let’s explore this idea through Jo’s story. Jo* was an unusually sensitive kid who struggled with chronic anxiety as far back as she could remember. “My brother always called me ‘scaredy-cat’ because I was terrified all the time. Dogs, strangers, loud noises—you name it, I was freaked out by everything,” she told me in our first session.

Like so many clients, at first, Jo told me her family was “totally normal.” “I had a regular, happy childhood,” she claimed, despite struggling with a raft of anxiety disorder diagnoses her whole life. When I pointed this out, she initially blamed herself for being too sensitive, too thin-skinned, just too much as a kid.

It took some slow, patient detective work to explore what lay beneath the surface of this “normal” family, which was anything but. As part of my psychoeducation with clients, I explain the idea from schema therapy of “core emotional needs,” which every child has, no matter their culture or country of origin. These include needs for love and a secure attachment, being kept safe and protected from harm, and feeling valued and accepted for who they are, not how they perform.

Jo’s father was a violent alcoholic who took his frustration and rage out on Jo’s poor mother. Tearfully, Jo recalled the “walls shaking” as her dad punched them or trashed their home. Jo would hide under the covers with her younger brother, cradling him in her trembling arms. He was too little to understand, and her dad left the family when he was just five, so the chaos and dysfunction didn’t affect him as much as it did her.

As she admitted to me how awful things had been, Jo began to feel more compassion for the little girl who was so traumatized by her father’s rage attacks. Little by little, we helped transform her self-blame into self-compassion.

Like so many of my clients who were stigmatized by their family, Jo was constantly gaslit by other family members. Even though she rarely saw her father, who continued to drink heavily, he would tell her she was “weak” and needed to “toughen up,” taking zero responsibility for his behavior or role in making her so anxious. Even her brother, whom she tried so hard to protect, said she was “self-centered” and “attention-seeking” for being so anxious all the time. This broke her heart, but she had long just accepted that everyone’s version of their shared reality was true.

We worked hard to change that. I was a tireless cheerleader for Little Jo, that sensitive kid who did nothing wrong, despite the critical family stories. Jo now had two daughters of her own, so I asked her how they would react if their dad smashed up the living room or hit Jo in front of them. She was horrified, knowing how profoundly that would affect them. This helped her see that the family stories told by her dad and brother were neither true nor fair. “You know, I’m starting to see it wasn’t my fault for getting so upset,” she told me one day. “Any kid would have struggled with all that anger and chaos. If anyone’s to blame, it’s my dad, not me.”

For Jo, seeing the truth of her childhood eventually set her free. She learned to treat herself kindly, as well as how to set boundaries and be assertive, even with her overbearing father. When our therapy ended, she was flourishing—loving and accepting herself as “highly sensitive and proud of it.”

If any of this resonates with you, I’m sorry—I know all too well how painful it can be to fill that identified patient role. But I also hope you understand that this was a label given to you by a family desperately trying to avoid responsibility for its own dysfunction.

You may need help from a skilled professional in untangling the knotty story of your childhood, but finally understanding that you were just a sweet, innocent kid in a painful environment might just change your life.

*All case studies in these posts are composites—I never tell the stories of real clients, as confidentiality is very important to me.

To find a therapist near you, visit the Psychology Today Therapy Directory.

What is EMDR therapy, and does it actually work?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, MBA

Maybe a friend swears EMDR helped her move past something she’d carried for years. Or maybe you came across it online while searching for ways to cope with a memory that still feels heavy. Whether you’ve heard of EMDR but don’t know how it works, or you’re familiar with the approach but skeptical, we’re here to break it all down.

If you’re uncertain, that’s understandable, because EMDR can sound unconventional: you briefly recall a painful memory while following side-to-side stimulation, like tracking a moving target with your eyes. But the research tells a more reassuring story. It’s a well-studied therapy for trauma, and it’s increasingly explored for anxiety and depression, too.

Here’s what EMDR involves, what the research shows, and what a session is like, so you have a clearer picture of whether it might make sense for you.

EMDR stands for eye movement desensitization and reprocessing. It’s a structured therapy that helps the brain reprocess distressing memories so they carry less emotional weight. Psychologist Francine Shapiro, PhD, developed it in the late 1980s after she noticed that certain eye movements seemed to ease her own troubling thoughts.

EMDR rests on Shapiro’s adaptive information processing model. The idea is that your brain normally files an experience away, so you can recall it later without feeling like you’re living it again. But a frightening or painful event can interrupt that process, leaving the memory raw and unfiled, still carrying the fear and detail of that moment. That’s why it can resurface later, as vivid as the day it happened.

According to this model, “processing” a memory means helping your brain finish the filing. You don’t forget what happened, and the facts don’t change. What changes is the emotional charge it carries, so recalling it feels less like reliving it and more like remembering it. To help that happen, EMDR uses a technique called “bilateral stimulation.”

EMDR uses a technique called bilateral stimulation. While you hold a difficult memory in mind, a trained therapist guides you through side-to-side stimulation.

Bilateral stimulation can take a few forms:

eye movements that follow your therapist’s fingers

gentle taps that switch between your hands or knees

tones that play in one ear, then the other

There’s a theory behind the side-to-side part, though researchers are still working out exactly why it helps. The leading explanation is about mental bandwidth. Your working memory, the part of your mind that holds what you’re focused on right now, can only handle so much at once. When you track side-to-side movement while recalling a hard memory, the two tasks compete for that limited space. The hard memory tends to feel a little less vivid, and with repetition it can lose its sharp edges, so it feels less overwhelming to hold.

💙 Learn more in our guide to bilateral stimulation.

EMDR has eight phases, though they tend to blend together in practice. The early phases cover your history and teach you coping skills, so you feel prepared before any hard memories arise. In the middle phases, you focus on a specific memory while following bilateral stimulation, which is meant to help your brain reprocess it. The final phases check that the memory feels less distressing and help you close out the work safely.

The evidence is strong for post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), a condition that can develop after a frightening or life-threatening event. A Cochrane review (a gold-standard summary of research on a topic) found that EMDR and trauma-focused cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) both reduce PTSD symptoms. They also outperformed other therapies when researchers checked in after treatment ended. A 2024 review in the Journal of Traumatic Stress identified more than 30 randomized controlled trials supporting EMDR for PTSD in adults and children. Major health organizations, including the World Health Organization, recommend EMDR for PTSD.

However, some guidelines, including those from the American Psychological Association (APA), rate the evidence for EMDR as less certain than for trauma-focused CBT. Researchers also debate exactly why EMDR works.

💙 Read more about how EMDR can help you cope with PTSD.

The picture is more mixed outside PTSD. Early research suggests EMDR may help with anxiety, as one meta-analysis found reduced symptoms of anxiety, panic, and phobia, though the studies were small and varied in quality.

Evidence for depression is emerging as well. Another meta-analysis showed reduced depression symptoms, with the strongest effects in more severe cases. These findings are promising, but they don’t carry the same weight as the PTSD research.

After you and a therapist finish the early phases, you bring a specific memory to mind and notice the thoughts and body sensations that come with it. Then your therapist guides you through the bilateral stimulation while you let your mind go where it goes. You can ask to pause or stop at any time. Unlike many talk therapies, EMDR doesn’t require you to describe the memory in detail.

Sessions usually run 60–90 minutes. Your therapist pauses often to check in on what you noticed. Over several rounds of bilateral stimulation, many people find the memory feels less sharp, even though the facts don’t change. Between sessions, a mindfulness practice, such as slow breathing or a body scan, can help you ease feelings that surface.

💙 Explore Calm's EMDR-Inspired Bilateral: Anxiety, a set of music tracks that alternate gently between your left and right ear. It draws on the bilateral stimulation used in EMDR, though audio-only versions haven’t been studied on their own the way clinical EMDR has. Think of it as a calming complement to therapy, not a replacement for a trained therapist.

The answer varies, since it depends on what you’re working through. For a single, recent trauma, some people notice their symptoms ease within a handful of sessions. More complex or long-standing trauma usually takes longer, sometimes many months. Therapists describe a typical course as 6–12 sessions, though it varies widely from person to person.

Along the way, some people feel worse before they feel better. Painful memories can stir up strong emotions in the short term, which is common and doesn’t mean EMDR isn’t working. Before reprocessing begins, your therapist teaches you coping skills, such as grounding exercises that anchor you in the present, so you have tools to use if those feelings get intense.

💙 Try the 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 practice, a quick way to ground yourself in the present, with Prof. Megan Reitz.

EMDR stands for eye movement desensitization and reprocessing. It’s a form of therapy that uses bilateral stimulation, such as guided eye movements, to help your brain process distressing memories so they feel less intense. Psychologist Francine Shapiro developed this therapy in the late 1980s.

EMDR may help with anxiety, though PTSD is where the evidence is strongest. The studies on anxiety are smaller and less consistent. If your anxiety is tied to a specific frightening experience, it may be worth asking a therapist whether EMDR fits your situation.

💙 Explore how mindfulness can help you manage your anxiety.

EMDR differs from talk therapy because it doesn’t require you to describe your experience in detail. You’ll still talk with your therapist, especially early on, but the reprocessing happens while you hold a memory in mind and follow bilateral stimulation. Talk therapies like cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) focus more on examining and reshaping your thoughts out loud.

The main benefit of EMDR is that it can ease the distress tied to painful memories. For many people with PTSD, that can mean fewer flashbacks and less of that constant, on-alert feeling.

Much of the debate is about why EMDR works, not whether it does. The evidence is strongest for PTSD, with early and more mixed findings for anxiety and depression.

Some studies suggest the eye movements themselves add little. The benefit may instead come from giving your mind a second task while you recall a memory, which can make it feel less vivid and emotionally intense. Other tasks that split your attention may have the same effect.

Certain guidelines, including the APA’s, consider the evidence for EMDR less established than for trauma-focused CBT. None of this takes away from the relief many people find with EMDR. It’s useful context as you weigh your options with a therapist.

EMDR is generally safe when a trained therapist delivers it, though you may experience some short-term effects. You might feel more emotional or tired between sessions, or have vivid dreams as your brain keeps processing the memory.

These reactions usually pass within a day or two. If they last longer or feel overwhelming, it’s a good idea to tell your therapist, who can adjust the pace with you. Serious side effects are uncommon when a therapist follows the full eight-phase process.

The number of sessions you need depends on the memory you’re addressing and how long it has affected you. Some people feel relief after a few sessions, while longer-standing experiences often take more time. A common range is 6–12 sessions, but your timeline may be shorter or longer. Your therapist can give you a better sense of what to expect once they know your history.

Full EMDR needs a trained therapist, so it’s not something to try on your own. A therapist helps you reprocess memories and stay grounded if one feels overwhelming, which matters most with intense trauma.

The poisoned public rights doctrine and Sun Valley Orchards

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

On Tuesday, Nov. 10, the court will hear argument in Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards, LLC. This case concerns the H-2A visa program for temporary alien immigrant workers. The issue is whether the Department of Labor’s Article II administrative law judges have the authority to collect ruinous fines from employers who violate their employment contracts with such individuals.

In early August of this year, I signed an amicus brief supporting Sun Valley Orchards along with former Attorneys General Edwin Meese III and Michael Mukasey and law professor Gary Lawson, prepared and filed by our lawyer Amit Vora. We argued that the respondent was entitled not only to a Seventh Amendment civil jury but also to an Article III federal court.

After reading the government’s opening and reply briefs by Solicitor General D. John Sauer, I stand by our argument that a DOL administrative law judge may not deprive Sun Valley of property by imposing ruinous fines for violating a contract that bound it to follow certain labor laws in employing foreign immigrant H-2A workers. Rather, Sun Valley has a Seventh Amendment right to have its claims heard by a jury if it desires to do so.

The government is obviously correct that the government has a sovereign right to control immigration into the United States under the H-2A program. It does not follow from that, however, that the government can condition the admission of foreign workers under this program on Sun Valley’s surrendering its constitutional right to have employment disputes with those workers adjudicated by Article III courts with a Seventh Amendment right to a jury trial. Constitutional rights cannot be taken away without due process of law or in violation of, for example, the First, Fourth, or Seventh amendments. But that is exactly what the government is trying to do in this case.

By way of example, in 1987’s Nollan v. California Coastal Commission, the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Antonin Scalia, held that the California Coastal Commission could not use its plenary power over zoning to condition a renovation construction permit on the Nolans ceding a public easement to walk along their valuable California coastal property, as doing so would violate the takings clause of the Fifth Amendment. Likewise, the government cannot impose unconstitutional conditions when using either its zoning power or its plenary power over immigration. Nor can the government use its commerce power or any of its other enumerated powers to violate one’s constitutional rights. The government can shut down the foreign immigrant H2A program. But it cannot use it as a cudgel to make Americans surrender their constitutional rights simply because the rights of immigrants are at stake.

Central to the government’s argument otherwise is the public-rights doctrine. This doctrine – properly understood – allows for privileges created by the government to be adjudicated by non-Article III judges, such as the privileges to receive social-security, disability, or welfare payments; the privilege to immigrate into the United States; or the privilege to receive tax rebates under the federal government’s partial waiver of sovereign immunity. With regard to such privileges, the Constitution’s guarantee of an Article III forum and the Seventh Amendment civil jury trial right are inapplicable because the receipt of these were not property rights recognized at common law, but privileges created by the government. The government argues that because immigration into the country is also a privilege, not a right, Sun Valley’s conduct related to this are not subject to Article III protections.

It is true that the public rights doctrine was profoundly altered (one could say poisoned), in the 1977 case of Atlas Roofing Co. v. Occupational Safety and Health Administration. In Atlas Roofing, Justice Byron White denied access to an Article III court and a Seventh Amendment civil jury trial to a party subject to an OSHA enforcement order. According to White, because such orders were based on “new statutory obligations,” they could be “committed exclusively to an administrative agency [for] deciding whether a violation has in fact occurred.” This was incorrect: while OSHA created new torts that can occur in the workplace, these were analogous to the kinds of tort actions that would have been heard at common law. Indeed, such torts are classic “Cases in Law,” which Article III gives sole jurisdiction over to life-tenured federal judges assisted by a civil jury if the plaintiffs choose one. Thus, Atlas Roofing Co. changed the law to say that the public-rights doctrine not only allowed Article II administrative law judges to deprive persons of privileges, but, for the first time, it also eliminated the guarantee of a Seventh Amendment jury trial when a person or entity was deprived of property rights by the government.

Fortunately, this dangerous precedent was limited by the more recent 2024 case of SEC v. Jarkesy. In Jarkesy, the Supreme Court held that when the SEC imposed civil penalties against a defendant for securities fraud, the Seventh Amendment entitled the defendant to a jury trial. This is because in 1787 a fraud case, like the employment contract case at issue in Sun Valley (or, for that matter, the property seized in Atlas Roofing), would have been “Case in Law” assigned to the sole jurisdiction of the Article III courts.

So the lesson of Jarkesy is this: While the vast majority of cases which ALJs decide only “privileges” and not “rights” only require an Article II hearing, when federal regulatory agencies impose ruinous fines on a corporation or person, those fines are deprivations of one’s right to property. And in depriving one of such property, it is not enough to have an administrative hearing: The case must be heard by an Article III court with the right to a Seventh Amendment civil-jury trial.

The public-rights doctrine then does not apply to enforcement orders that deprive a person of property by agencies such as OSHA in Atlas Roofing, the SEC in Jarkesy, or the DOL in Sun Valley. Nor does calling a case an immigration matter empower the government to take away the constitutional rights of Americans even if immigration into the United States is itself a privilege and not a right.

So let’s hope that the Supreme Court in Sun Valley rejects, as unconstitutional, the DOL’s attempt to funnel a private-rights dispute away from an Article III forum. And let’s further hope that the court clearly reframes the public-rights doctrine, as limited in Jarkesy, as a mere manifestation of the rights/privileges distinction – and continues to prevent the doctrine from taking the form that Justice White allowed for in Atlas Roofing: an ominous threat to Article III, the Seventh Amendment, and the due process clause.

Professor Steven Gow Calabresi is the Clayton J. & Henry R. Barber Professor of Law at Northwestern Pritzker School of Law.
