This Luxury Spa Is New England’s Best-Kept Secret

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Powered by Valmont at The Preserve Sporting Club & Resort, the Hilltop Spa is Rhode Island’s hidden gem

Rhode Island may not be the first place you think of when booking a luxury spa and resort getaway, but it should be. While the Ocean State is well known for its miles of Atlantic coastline, an exclusive, if-you-know-you-know destination is tucked away among 3,500 acres of rolling hills: Richmond’s The Preserve Sporting Club & Resort.

The Preserve is designed for nature lovers, with hiking trails, on-site equestrian training stables, and the nation’s only North American Bentley off-road track (the resort even picked me up in a Bentley truck from the Providence train station). The grounds also offer a range of activities, like clay shooting, archery, rock climbing, ziplining, and more, depending on the season. But beyond the private sporting club and resort’s recreational charms, The Preserve is equally intentional about members’ and visitors’ self-care, one reason the resort partnered with Valmont Skincare to develop the beauty brand’s fourth spa location, the Hilltop Spa.

Valmont’s 41-year-old brand is steeped in Swiss scientific expertise, which I experienced during my weekend-long stay at The Preserve and my visit to the Hilltop Spa. Beyond steam rooms, saunas, and private whirlpools in both the women’s and men’s locker rooms, the spa features a coed hydrotherapy area with two whirlpools, a cold plunge, and a private after-dark experience complete with champagne.

After a quick change into a fluffy robe, I relaxed with a brief aromatherapy session, then received the Preserve Custom massage, which was deeply restorative. A few minutes later, after lounging in the spa’s designated relaxation room and enjoying some healthy snacks, I moved on to my customized Hydration of the Bisses facial, a plumping 60- to 90-minute treatment that targets the lymphatic system to improve fluid flow. No matter which Valmont treatment you choose at the Hilltop Spa, each includes its own Butterfly Motion, a proprietary massage technique.

“Valmont’s signature facials target main skin concerns like hydration, lifting, and firming, and combine cellular cosmetics with exclusive massage techniques,” says Viviana Garrido, Valmont’s U.S. sales director. “Guests love the immediate glow, lifting effect, and sense of deep relaxation. What makes the experience unique is the combination of performing yet relaxing protocols in an immersive sensory journey inspired by the Swiss Alps, delivering results for both the skin and the mind.”

True to The Preserve’s ethos of holistic beauty and wellness in the treatment room and beyond, the spa is often a first, though likely recurring, stop for its guests. Beyond its Valmont-approved amenities, the sporting club offers yoga, Pilates, sound bathing, and personalized fitness training, some of which I also took part in during my stay.

“Each provides an opportunity for guests to reconnect with themselves through movement, mindfulness, and relaxation,” notes resort manager Clinton Cox. To mentally disconnect in warmer months and through mid-fall, The Preserve also offers its bespoke Tree House Experience, developed by Cox himself and designed for couples, close friends, or small groups. The two-hour immersive forest wellness journey combines therapeutic body treatments, guided stretching, a 90-minute massage, relaxation, and a specially curated culinary menu.

“At The Preserve, we believe skincare should extend beyond the treatment room,” explains Cox. “It is not simply about how your skin looks when you leave the spa; it is about investing in its long-term health, vitality, and appearance while embracing an experience defined by exceptional service, refined surroundings, and genuine hospitality.”

Garrido agrees, noting that the company partnered with The Preserve as its fourth spa location in the United States because of shared philosophies of exceptional craftsmanship for guest and user experiences, a deep respect for nature, and a shared commitment to longevity. “Its unique setting and reputation for luxury hospitality provide the perfect environment for Valmont treatments and skincare expertise,” says Garrido. “The partnership allows us to offer guests a truly immersive wellness experience where luxury, performance, longevity, and well-being come together naturally.”

With so much to try, you can tailor a visit to The Preserve to your unique beauty and wellness preferences, which is part of what makes the Rhode Island sporting club and resort so singular. Seemingly off the beaten path, but more like hidden in plain sight, it’s virtually a home away from home for New England residents—the property even has a selection of tiny homes you can stay in—and a short train ride for those in the tri-state area.

Sierra Club: We Cannot Support the Permitting Bill As Currently Written

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Democrats have been given a mandate to fight for a better deal for the American people.

Washington, DC — In the wake of this week’s overwhelming blue wave election, the Senate may still take a vote during the lame duck session on the “Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026” permitting bill.

While the bill contains provisions that would help boost clean energy and lower energy bills, it also includes troubling provisions that profoundly weaken key environmental protections and limit communities’ ability to have a say in what happens in their neighborhoods.

The Sierra Club cannot support this legislation without fundamental changes. Congressional Democrats must demand the following:

Sierra Club Executive Director Loren Blackford released the following statement:

“The Sierra Club supports updating and expediting transmission and clean energy permitting, and have long advocated for a bill that will lower costs, reduce air, water, and climate pollution, protect communities, and expand America’s grid. As written, this bill achieves critical wins on transmission and potential emissions reduction. However, this does not justify abandoning Tribal consultation, clean water safeguards, and wildlife protections, nor further enabling a rogue president that is waging a war on clean energy. As currently drafted, this bill will hand more power to the executive branch, erode democratic norms, and cause environmental harm to our water, wildlife, and public health protections far beyond the scope of federal permitting. The Sierra Club cannot support this bill as currently written.

“The American people sent a clear message that they are fed up with a lawless president who raises costs with his chaotic and vindictive energy policy. They want a congress that will act on behalf of their interests, not the corporate polluters Donald Trump and Congressional Republicans have prioritized. The current Republican Congressional leadership, which passed the largest attack on affordable clean energy in American history and stood by as Trump waged his expensive and illegal war on clean energy, will be out of power in a matter of weeks.

“Senate Democrats have been given a mandate to act decisively to deliver relief on energy bills and level the playing field for clean energy. They can and must fight for a better deal.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. The herring that fed medieval Europe never ran out, yet the fishery that caught them collapsed

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Overfishing, a cooling climate, shifting dunes and serfdom ended a medieval herring fishery, and the lessons apply to Baltic herring today.

When a fishery dies, the obvious explanation is that people caught all the fish. For the fishery that was once Europe’s main source of salted herring, that isn’t what happened. The herring were still there when the catch collapsed in the late 1500s.

A new study finds it took overfishing, a colder climate and a changing society, all at once, to end it.

For nearly 300 years, herring caught in the Sound, the narrow strait between Denmark and Sweden, was salted and shipped from the British Isles to northern Italy.

Around 1570, the catch fell apart within a few years. It took until the 1950s for catches there to reach the same size again.

A team of fisheries economists, ecologists and historians led by Kiel University and the German Centre for Integrative Biodiversity Research (iDiv) set out to find out why.

Their catch numbers are rebuilt from scattered old records, so they show a smoothed trend rather than a count for every year.

“None of these factors alone would have brought the fishery down. It is their interplay that explains the dynamics,” said Rudi Voss, the study’s first author and a fisheries economist in iDiv’s Biodiversity Economics group.

For most of the period, no continuous catch records exist. The team’s time series runs from 1200 to 1650. It was pieced together from customs records, toll books and accounts of the king’s right to buy herring first.

Where those records went silent, the researchers filled gaps using salt. Salt was the trade’s most important supply, so how much of it was available set a limit on how much herring could be preserved and sold.

Most modern records of a fish population cover a few decades. This one is long enough to follow plagues, wars and climate swings across centuries.

Each autumn, fishing fairs near the small towns of Skanör and Falsterbo became busy trading centers. Between 1524 and 1570, the fishery landed about 61,000 tons (55,000 metric tons) of herring a year on average.

The researchers paired the catch history with old herring prices from markets in England, Hamburg, Brabant and Utrecht. From the two together, they estimated the largest catch the herring could have sustained year after year.

During the boom, the catch ran above that limit. The herring population shrank to between 41 and 51 percent of the largest size the team estimated for it. It didn’t crash, though.

In an interview with Earth.com, Voss said the size of the catch stood out to him. “Looking back at our analysis, I was surprised by the huge amount of herring catch being realized by very simple means,” he said.

He added that effective rules would have been needed even then to keep the catch sustainable. A few did exist: trawl nets were banned in 1371, and there are signs that net mesh size was regulated in 1386.

A smaller stock likely made each fish more expensive to catch. Meanwhile, the Little Ice Age was setting in, and herring that spawn in autumn fare poorly after harsh winters, so the stock replaced itself more slowly.

The fishing towns had their own troubles. Shifting dunes destroyed their infrastructure in the 1570s, and fishermen left for a herring boom on the Swedish west coast.

In the early 1600s, nobles enforced serfdom on peasants, barring them from leaving their landlords’ fields each autumn to fish.

Buyers were also turning to the North Sea, where boats were seaworthy and the season lasted three times as long.

To weigh all of this, the team drew a map of nine factors, from climate and war to workforce and demand. They marked whether each one pushed the others up or down.

Across the fishery’s four phases, the map’s predictions matched the catch history. Colder weather alone couldn’t explain it.

For centuries, those pressures partly canceled one another out. In the early 1500s, they all pushed the same way, and the fishery boomed. By the middle of the 1500s, the pressures had turned, and all of them pulled the catch down.

The medieval fishery caught herring that spawn in autumn. Today the main fishery in the western Baltic targets a different group that spawns in spring. That stock is outside safe biological limits, and scientists have advised a catch of zero.

Overfishing drove that decline, the authors wrote, and warmer water now makes recovery harder, much as cooling weighed on the medieval stock.

“Today’s fisheries management in the EU is still not sufficiently able to deal with climate change impacts and increasing cumulative (socio-economic) pressures,” Voss told Earth.com.

He called for a faster move toward ecosystem-based management, which weighs everything around a fish population instead of one species alone, and said it must include social and economic factors.

“If we fail to do so, we might see a number of fisheries going to share the fate of this historic fishery,” he said.

The catch history is a best estimate, and its certainty varies. The 1200s are inferred from salt production and population, while the decline after the 1570s rests on sparse tax records.

The old herring prices couldn’t be adjusted for changes in the value of money, so the authors warn that their results should be treated with caution. Their map of nine factors shows which way each pressure pushed the catch, not how hard.

The analysis can’t say whether the dunes, the cold or the lost workers mattered most in the final years.

Voss put the lesson plainly. “I think that we need to acknowledge that sustainable fisheries can only succeed if we take social and economic conditions just as seriously as the health of the stock itself,” he said.

The western Baltic now faces the same question. A recent German commission on the future of Baltic Sea fisheries proposed improving infrastructure and diversifying the local economy alongside the zero catch.

Whether the spring herring recovers will show if that is enough.

The full study was published in the journal Nature Sustainability.

Melissa Joan Hart Explains It All: Hot Flashes, Menopause, and Turning 50

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Actor Melissa Joan Hart has a vivid memory of her mother standing in front of their open freezer to cool off. As a child, she didn’t understand why. Now, as a 50-year-old woman, Hart knows that her mom was experiencing hot flashes.

“[She’s] only 20 years older than me, so maybe I missed the part where she was going through [menopause], but there was no conversation about it,” Hart told Healthline. “I feel like it’s just becoming a hot topic with women, where…the taboo is going away from it now.”

Her own perimenopausal symptoms started with night sweats.

“[It] was really jarring, and the first few times, you’re not really sure what that was…do I need to turn the air down or what’s going on here?” Hart said.

Over time, she learned that she was experiencing moderate to severe hot flashes, which affect up to 80% of women during the menopause transition and can persist for 7 to 10 years.

Doctors categorize hot flashes as mild, moderate, or severe, said Diana Bitner, MD, OB-GYN, at Corewell Health.

“[So] if they’re mild, it’s a sense of warmth (maybe in the chest, or the neck, or the face). A moderate hot flash is where there’s associated sweating, and a severe hot flash is something that stops you in your tracks, wakes you up from the middle of the night,” Bitner told Healthline.

Hart has mostly experienced hot flashes while home or at her three sons’ activities, and they have yet to interfere with her work. However, according to research, one in three women report moderate to severe challenges in managing their work due to symptoms related to menopause.

“It’s just so sad that about 10% of women leave the workforce due to menopause,” said Bitner. “And so it can really affect our ability to perform at these key years of not only institutional memory, but also of earning power, and it can really affect the whole community.”

Bitner said women should feel empowered to bring up their menopausal symptoms or questions with their doctor.

“[This] is something that was taboo for so long…women didn’t really talk about this, whether it was because of embarrassment, or [they] weren’t sure if anyone was experiencing it,” she said. “[There’s] no reason, in this day and age, we need to suffer through it…it’s something that there are treatment options for.”

In fact, Bitner said providing specific details about their symptoms, such as how many mild, moderate, or severe hot flashes they’ve experienced over the past week, can help doctors better assess patients’ needs.

Hart plans to discuss treatment options for her hot flashes with her doctor soon. In the last few years, she said she has put emphasis on her midlife health.

“I just was sick of waking up with aches and pains in general [thinking], ‘is this the rest of my life?’” she said.

She started listening to self-help books in the car while driving her kids around and found ways to put herself first.

“My kids are a little bit older, so I kind of have that freedom to be able to do that,” she said. “[But] along with getting older, comes the menopause, comes that different time in life. And I think it’s a good time to reevaluate [how] our bodies can’t handle what [they] used to handle, and let’s figure it out.”

Hart has partnered with pharmaceutical company Bayer on its “Life Doesn’t Stop for a Hot Flash” campaign, which encourages women to discuss menopause symptoms with their doctors and promotes Lynkuet, a hormone-free prescription medication for moderate to severe hot flashes associated with menopause.

“It’s called ‘Life Doesn’t Stop for a Hot Flash’ campaign because it shouldn’t, because we should be able to push through, and this should be a time in our lives when our kids are getting older, and we’re able to kind of reinvent ourselves,” said Hart. “And I think it’s an exciting time, and it shouldn’t be something that we’re afraid of.”

She is embracing speaking openly about midlife health.

“I hit 50 this year, and it was, like, a huge milestone, and I think that’s something to be proud of, and not something to shy away from,” she said.

Hart also hopes to challenge the longstanding barriers women face in Hollywood as they age, including fewer opportunities for leading roles.

“[I] think we’re seeing that the generation before, and my generation, it feels like we’re pushing those boundaries…and we’re saying, you know, that women at this age have a lot to give, have a lot of information to share, have a lot of stories to tell and experiences and adventures still ahead of us, and, you know, we’re not ready to stop,” Hart said.

In everyday life, Bitner said that while some patients avoid talking about midlife issues like menopause with their doctors, she is seeing more patients who are confident being forthcoming.

As more women talk openly about menopause, she hopes to include men in the conversation, too. In fact, she invites her patients to bring their partners to appointments, so they can gain an understanding of menopause and the experiences their partners face, such as hot flashes, sleep issues, vaginal dryness, and discomfort or pain during intercourse.

“[Often], there’s, you know, comments that come up [from men who] say, ‘How do I talk about this without getting in trouble?’” said Bitner. “[We] want everyone to join this conversation…it doesn’t have to be just her issue because we know it’s not.”

Two New Commissioners Headed to the Arizona Corporation Commission

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Phoenix — Come January, the Arizona Corporation Commission (ACC) will be bi-partisan as two Democrats, Clara Pratte and Jonathon Hill, have been elected to the ACC. They will be joining current Republican commissioners Lea Márquez Peterson, Rachel Walden, and René Lopez. The election comes as the Commission is considering rate increases for Arizona Public Service Company (APS) and Tucson Electric Power Company (TEP), and mechanisms that would allow both utilities to increase rates every year with less stakeholder input, and as the Commission begins to consider the utilities’ proposed long-term resource plans.

As a recent Sierra Club report makes clear, the ACC wields a tremendous amount of power over the future of Arizona’s energy landscape, public health, and utility bill increases, especially as the state experiences a fossil gas rush to power data centers. Arizonans experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country and with Arizona’s largest utility, APS, opting to run the dirty Four Corners coal plant for more than another decade. Yet, up until now, the Commission has made a habit of increasing ratepayer bills, opting for expensive fossil fuels and cutting programs that require utilities to utilize solar energy to generate electricity and to conserve energy, lower electric bills, and invest in energy efficiency.

In response, Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr issued the following statement:

“These election results should act as a wakeup call to the rest of the Arizona Corporation Commission. Arizonans are tired of being put on the backburner, bearing the burden for expensive fossil fuels that harm our neighbors and contribute to climate change. We share in the optimism of our fellow voters and will continue to carefully monitor the ACC as our work to promote clean air, clean water, and healthy communities and a healthy climate enters a new chapter.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. A Simple, Heartwarming Way to Get Out of Your Own Head

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 5, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

There is no shortage of influencers out there providing advice and hawking products to improve our lives. So, whether it is how to maximize fitness gains, how to sleep better, or how and when to meditate, we are seemingly all on a quest to feel happier. Yet, what if there's another way to feel better that we may not think about enough?

Here's a newsflash: If you want to really move the feel-better needle, then help someone else. You don't have to do huge things to help others. I’m saying the more we notice people who could use something from us, the more it can positively change our moods, especially if we act on that urge to help them.

There are endless videos online of people doing kind things for strangers, including paying for gas, cleaning up yards, and providing money to those in need. These stories are heartwarming. But what if we are watching popular videos about kindness and not enough seeing the power of doing kind things ourselves? Do we want to settle for those vicarious doses of feeling moved by the kind actions taken by others and then just call it a day?

In my newly released book, Help Them: And Watch Your Own Life Get Better, I further discuss the powerful emotional health benefits we gain by directly helping others. Maybe someone you know could really feel good getting a phone call. Is someone you know going through a tough time and could really perk up with some encouragement? Or, and this is tough for all of us, maybe somebody needs the wonderful gift of you "just listening" without immediately jumping in with advice.

I don’t know about you, but for years I've been internally preoccupied (OK, I’ll admit it—often consumed) by my own thoughts. It seems to me, the questions that really weigh on all of us are things like: Am I really happy? Or, did I mess that up? And, of course, we go down "Regret Alley" with thoughts like, “If only I didn't mess that up, I'd be so much better off.” That said, do you agree that there is a point where self-focus becomes its own trap?

By helping others, at least for a while, you are thinking about something besides yourself. This notion is supported by research on kindness and helping behavior that has linked helping others with our own greater well-being. How about right now, you get back in touch with a time when you knew you made a difference? Didn’t it feel pretty darn good? Next, let's look at how to keep helping helpful versus it ever becoming harmful.

Like most things in life, there can be too much done sometimes when it comes to helping others. I have seen this play out with many of my parent-coaching clients, where helping is not always healthy. Specifically, I see this all the time with parents of struggling adult children. For example, a parent pays another bill, makes another phone call, fixes another problem, or bails their child out of another mess.

The danger is that sometimes helping becomes rescuing. Sadly, and I will even say tragically, rescuing can keep another person from developing the skills they need. So, a useful question is not just, "How can I help?", but more importantly, "Will my help actually help?" In some cases, giving support may be best through giving our listening ear than giving from our wallets. And, even further, sometimes we may help best if, after weighing the situation, we say no.

When it comes to our perceptions of what helping is, we tend to think meaningful acts have to be dramatic. But they don't have to be huge acts of kindness. How about you send that text you've been loosely debating sending to someone who may feel really good by hearing from you? Or, better yet, call them and let them hear your supportive voice. While you're at it, maybe tell that person why you really appreciate them.

It feels good to share that a teen counseling client of mine just told me the other day how great he felt by helping an elderly neighbor by raking some leaves (he politely refused her offer to pay him) at her house (she had recently lost her husband of 40 years). A parent I work with recently shared how good she felt about becoming more patient with her children when they would go into their long, colorful stories.

I hope this post inspires you to help others because it may turn out to be the best self-improvement plan you could ever embark on. When you do help others, you will feel less in your head because your focus has meaningfully shifted. The more we help others, the better we will feel. So, please: help them.

Yeah, I’ll Try the Red-Light Therapy Vibrator

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

It’s been a big few years for red-light therapy. You can get it in a skin-clearing LED face mask, a hair-growth-stimulating cap, or a sauna blanket you can self-zip into like a burrito, and I’ve cheered on every new format. I just assumed we’d eventually run out of places for it. Oh, but reader, turns out there was one place left.

LELO, the Swedish luxury sex toy brand, just put red LEDs inside a G-spot vibrator called the MONA Spectra, and the brand says you can get the benefits of red-light therapy (collagen! circulation!) along with, you know, the main event.

As someone who reads the ingredient list on a $14 serum before it gets anywhere near my face, I had questions. So yes, I tried the red-light therapy vibrator. Here's my honest review, plus what the science actually says about red-light therapy for your vagina.

At its core, the MONA Spectra is a G-spot vibrator. It borrows the curved silhouette of LELO's longtime MONA line, with a rounded tip angled toward the front wall of the vagina (aka G-spot territory) and soft, body-safe silicone wrapped in twisted ribs for extra texture. It has 10 vibration settings and 16 intensity levels, and it's fully waterproof, so the bath is fair game.

The twist (besides the literal twisted ribs) is a built-in array of red LEDs. Out of the box, it runs red light at 615 to 625 nanometers, and the LELO app unlocks near-infrared, blue and green light, plus extra vibration patterns.

If you've ever bought an LED face mask, you already know the pitch. Red light is thought to help your cells repair themselves, which is why it's become such a skin-care staple, and LELO is applying the same idea to vaginal tissue.

LELO says regular use may support collagen and elastin, tissue elasticity, natural lubrication, and sensitivity. The brand also says the LED array gives off a gentle warmth that can relax the pelvic floor muscles and increase blood flow, which, for the record, your body tends to appreciate during sex anyway.

The pitch, basically, is folding a wellness habit into something you might already be doing.

The short answer: it's promising, but it's early. Most of the research on red light and vaginal health has focused on menopause, when dropping estrogen can lead to dryness and thinner, more sensitive tissue. A 2019 review of the research so far concluded that red-light therapy appears to be safe and may help with those symptoms, but the authors called the evidence preliminary, and none of it was testing an at-home vibrator.

LELO ran its own four-week survey with 37 women, and nearly 8 in 10 of those using it a few times a week reported noticeably more natural moisture, while close to 70% said they felt more confident in their bodies. It's an encouraging start, and bigger studies will tell us more. Until then, LELO itself positions the MONA Spectra as an everyday self-care device rather than a medical one, which is the right way to think about it.

So for now, think of the red light as a nice extra. If you're dealing with dryness or pain during sex, talk to your OB-GYN, who can help you figure out what will actually make a difference.

If you've used a LELO vibrator before, the MONA Spectra will feel familiar. It has the same gently curved shape and soft, smooth silicone as the regular MONA, and the twisted ribs add a little extra texture. The vibrations are what you'd expect from LELO, with enough settings to keep things low-key or turn them all the way up.

The one difference is warmth: with the LEDs on, the toy gets a little warm, which LELO's instructions say is normal.

If you're buying it for the red-light benefits, give it time. LELO's own survey ran for four weeks, and the brand recommends sticking to 15 minutes of light a day.

Yes (which makes the price easier to swallow). The lights are optional, so if the red light ends up being the thing you skip, you still own a high-end G-spot vibrator, and a very good one at that.

From what researchers have seen so far, yes, as long as you're using a device designed for intimate use and following its instructions. The MONA Spectra checks those boxes: it's made of body-safe silicone that meets ISO 3533, the international safety standard for sex toys, and LELO says its LEDs are UV-free, so you're not exposing sensitive skin to the kind of light that causes damage. A few ground rules from LELO:

If you're pregnant or managing a health condition, check with your doctor before adding any light therapy device to your routine, and read LELO's user manual for the full list of warnings.

If you already have a red-light routine and you've been eyeing a nicer G-spot vibrator anyway, the MONA Spectra makes a lot of sense. If you mostly want a great G-spot vibe and the light therapy is a maybe, LELO's regular MONA has the same familiar shape for a lot less money.

Yes. LELO says it's 100% waterproof and safe for the bath or shower.

LELO says the app is anonymous and fully encrypted, and you don't need it to use the toy's basic settings.

$299 is a lot of money for a vibrator, no way around it. It's also in the same price range as plenty of red-light face masks, and none of those double as a sex toy. The MONA Spectra is a seriously good G-spot vibrator, and the red light is a fun bonus for anyone who's already bought into the wellness of it all. If you haven't, stick with a regular LELO vibrator and save the money.

Riskier decisions may offer an early warning of heavier drinking

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Changes in phone game decisions predicted heavier drinking the following month in a yearlong study of people with alcohol use disorder.

Alcohol has a reputation for making people take risks. But a yearlong study of people with alcohol use disorder found that changes in decision making came before heavier drinking.

When participants took certain risks in phone games, they drank more the following month. Heavier drinking, however, did not predict riskier choices the next month.

The researchers tracked changes within each person rather than simply comparing heavier and lighter drinkers.

Most participants had mild or moderate alcohol use disorder, making them a potentially important group for early intervention.

Hilmar Zech, a postdoctoral researcher at University Hospital Würzburg and TU Dresden in Germany, led the study with Lorenz Deserno.

The team also included co-authors at Charité in Berlin and the Central Institute of Mental Health.

“This temporal direction was specific and was not observed vice versa, even after accounting for the amount of previous drinking,” Deserno said.

The researchers followed 603 people, ages 16 to 65, for one year. All of them met at least two of diagnostic criteria for alcohol use disorder.

In the study, a standard drink held 0.4 ounces (12 grams) of pure alcohol. The participants averaged about three of those a day, and about five on the days they drank.

Every two days, each person reported what they had drunk from a list of 19 options, from a small beer to a bottle of wine.

Every four weeks, they also played four five-minute games on their phones. In one game, fruit fell from a tree and the player tapped to collect it, unless the fruit turned brown.

In another, red circles flashed on a grid and the player had to remember their positions. Those two measured self-control.

The other two measured decision making. In a gambling game, the player chose between a sure amount and a coin flip, in versions built around wins, losses, or both.

In a card game, the player could pay to flip over cards before guessing which of two face-down rows added up to more.

The mixed version offered a possible win or a possible loss on the same flip. In months when a person took that gamble more often, that person drank more the next month.

The card game gave the same result. When someone flipped fewer cards before guessing, they drank more in the month that followed.

In the loss version, the link was reversed. In months when a person gambled more to avoid a sure loss, they drank less the following month.

Earlier studies had compared people with alcohol use disorder with people without it at a single point in time.

Those studies found a similar pattern: people with alcohol use disorder were more willing to gamble when both winning and losing were possible, but less willing when the gamble involved only potential losses.

As far as Zech’s team knows, no study had watched those shifts come and go inside one person and then watched the drinking follow.

“We found that monthly fluctuations in aspects of decision making predicted subsequent alcohol consumption,” Zech said.

Next the team ran the question backward. Did heavy drinking in one month change how a person played the games the next month?

The researchers found no evidence of that pattern.

The forward link also held after the team accounted for the fact that one month’s drinking resembles the last.

In a second analysis built for a year with many zero-drink days, the loss-version result held up, and the other two showed the same direction with more uncertainty.

The two self-control games did not follow that pattern.

A person’s monthly ups and downs in avoiding rotten fruit or remembering circles had no link to the drinking that followed.

Working memory still mattered, but between people instead of within one person.

Participants who could hold more circles in mind drank less on average over the year than participants who could hold fewer.

Cameron Carter, editor-in-chief of the journal that published the study, explained why experimental tasks matter.

“Decision making and cognitive control cannot be accurately examined through interviews or self-reports and instead require experimental tasks,” Carter said.

The effects were small. Zech’s team wrote that they are likely too small to be useful in a clinic yet.

The team had also planned to split the sample in two, with the second half meant to confirm what the first half found.

The pattern was clear in the first half and not in the second, although the two halves did not differ from each other when the team compared them directly.

The team also split the 603 people into random halves 1,000 times. The direction came out the same every time, and a group of about 300 was often too small to detect the effect.

Of the 603 who started, 218 dropped out early. Their data stayed in, and leaving was not tied to how they played or how much they drank.

The self-control games may simply have been played too rarely. A few studies that checked several times a day did find self-control dipping before drinking, though only under certain conditions.

Once a month may be too rare to catch that. And a study that watches people without changing anything can’t say that the risky choices caused the drinking.

Whether a game score can predict heavier drinking over the following week, rather than month, is still an open question.

The team envisions an app that flags changes in a person’s risk-taking score and offers automated support or prompts them to seek help in person.

Whether that approach would work remains untested.

The full study was published in the journal Biological Psychiatry: Cognitive Neuroscience and Neuroimaging.

Melania Trump calls for lasting Ukraine truce as she hosts Russian and Ukrainian officials

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Melania Trump was awarded Ukraine's Order of Princess Olha, 1st Class for her "significant personal contribution to humanitarian support for Ukraine"

US First Lady Melania Trump called for a "more enduring" ceasefire in Ukraine as she welcomed Russian and Ukrainian delegations at the White House following last week's truce in the southern region of Kherson.

Hundreds of residents of the Russian-occupied Ukrainian town of Oleshky facing starvation and constant bombardment were able to evacuate during a six-day ceasefire, which both Moscow and Kyiv credited Trump with brokering.

"The lives of innocent people must transcend the division of war. I hope Oleshky inspires greater peace," she said.

The unusual White House event saw senior Ukrainian and Russian officials meet away from the setting of official US-brokered peace talks they have been holding without success since last year.

The delegations were made up of Ukraine's human rights ombudsman Dmytro Lubinets and his Russian counterpart Yana Lantratova, as well as Ukrainian MP Davyd Arakhamia and Kremlin envoy Kirill Dmitriev.

Melania Trump was credited by all for playing a key though unspecified role in the ceasefire, and all parties conveyed to her the gratitude of the Russian and Ukrainian presidents.

Melania Trump handed Dmitriev and Arakhamia "personal letters" for their respective presidents

The US first lady said she had "reviewed demining plans, aerial images, assessed roadways, and discussed evacuation routes, hospital access, vehicles, and medical resources" during the truce.

"Last week in Oleshky and neighbouring areas of Kherson, people experienced the simple joys of life for the first time since 2022," Trump said. "They walked down familiar streets without fear of explosions."

She thanked both Volodymyr Zelensky and Vladimir Putin, making no reference to the origins of the war, which began when the Russian president launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, and which has caused thousands of civilian casualties and the displacement of millions.

Dmitriev, a businessman and Kremlin special envoy who has previously represented Moscow during peace talks with Ukraine, thanked Trump for "saving and touching so many lives".

"Let us continue making a humanitarian contribution together; this is within our power," Dmitriev said, before gifting Melania Trump a framed drawing by an 11-year-old Oleshky evacuee.

He also praised Trump for her work on reuniting children separated from their families by the war. These include children allegedly abducted by Russia.

Arakhamia, a Ukrainian MP who heads Zelensky's party in parliament who has also been part of the Ukrainian negotiating team, said the Oleshky ceasefire had constituted the "biggest de-escalation since 2022".

"We are creating a momentum where both sides are learning theâ¦ mechanics of how to actually keep the peace when the peace comes," he added.

He acknowledged that the "political track" of negotiations to end the conflict was "complex" but said Ukrainians were asking Melania Trump "to keep going and find new missions and de-escalate more."

At the end of the event, Trump handed Dmitriev and Arakhamia two letters to be hand-delivered to their respective presidents.

The White House later said Melania Trump had invited her guests to view two historic artifacts which she had "personally selected as powerful symbols of peace".

One was an 18th Century medicine chest which was removed from the White House when the British set it on fire in 1812. "Once adversaries, the United States and Great Britain ultimately established a strong alliance," the White House said.

The second artifact was a cup gifted in 1899 to the French ambassador in recognition of his efforts to bring the Spanish-American war to an end.

Melania Trump has generally remained discreet on foreign policy matters, and neither Moscow nor Kyiv have elaborated on the nature or extent of her involvement in coordinating the delicate ceasefire - the most enduring and widespread of the war so far.

A source who works in the local government of the Kherson region told the BBC that work on the Oleshky evacuation had been ongoing for almost a year.

Oleshky evacuees arrived in Ukraine after a journey through southern Russia and Belarus

However, the source said, "at a certain point other individuals became involved, seemingly after most of the groundwork had already been laid and subsequently began taking credit for the progress that had been made."

It was difficult to determine exactly who had played what role and at which stage of the process, the source acknowledged, adding that "those who had been directly involved in the negotiations for nearly a year were eventually prohibited from communicating publicly about the matter."

Speaking after the meeting in the White House had taken place, President Zelensky said the success of the Oleshky ceasefire "had proved a very simple thing: when Putin wants the fighting to stop, it stops."

"The same applies to our people who are held in Russian captivity. When Putin wants, they go home."

A source close to American officials shared a similar assessment, implying to the BBC that despite the outward praise, "the person who made [the ceasefire] possible, whether we like it or not, is essentially Putin."

"And this was done in order to pass the ball to [Donald] Trump... so that it would have some positive effect for his midterm elections."

There has been no change whatsoever in Russia's position, Ukrainian political analyst Dmytro Levus told the BBC.

Moscow "agreed to it because there was pressure from the American side... In doing so, the Russians confirmed Zelenskyy's words that, when necessary, it turns out Russia can quickly stop firing and even maintain that ceasefire."

As the frontline region of Kherson experienced its first six days of peace in several years, the rest of Ukraine continued to come under Russian attack.

This week, several drone and missile attacks killed and injured civilians across the country. On Monday alone, more than 40 people were killed in a single Russian strike on an aid distribution centre in the region of Zaporizhzhia. More are thought to still be buried under the rubble.

Additional reporting by Anastasiia Levchenko in Kyiv

STAT+: Novartis cites cases of previously unknown tumors possibly tied to its SMA gene therapy

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Ed’s stories explore prescription drug pricing, affordability and access, as well issues surrounding patents, litigation, and legislation. He is also the author of the morning Pharmalittle newsletter and the afternoon Pharmalot newsletter.

Jason is a general assignment reporter, with particular focus on genetic medicine and rare disease. Confidential tips can be sent on Signal at JasonMast.77.

Novartis has acknowledged seven previously unknown instances in which tumors were found in children who were treated with its spinal muscular atrophy therapy, a surprising development that has raised concerns among families, physicians, and patient advocacy groups.

The cases, which for the moment are being seen as only a potential safety signal, were publicized on Thursday by an advocacy group and on a Facebook page after Novartis earlier this week disclosed the information to neurologists. One advocacy group, SMA Europe, issued a statement saying that regulatory authorities are now examining the cases.

The drugmaker issued its own statement saying that since its treatment — called Zolgensma — was approved in 2019, it has received nine reports of tumors in previously treated patients. Of those, two cases to date showed evidence that the tumors may be linked to the treatment itself.

Is MAHA dying—or will it be reborn? What's next after crushing midterm losses?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

RFK Jr.’s Make America Healthy Again movement was in trouble before voting began.

As Democrats blew away their Republican rivals in an unprecedented midterm sweep Tuesday, the Make America Health Again movement also got bowled over, raising questions about the fate of the motley movement.

MAHA—which has centered around anti-vaccine activist and current Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.—saw many of its favored candidates and allies lose, while vocal adversaries of Kennedy won. And the coalition was splintering under stress even before the voting started; in recent months, supporters have accused Kennedy and the Trump administration of inaction and betrayals in their two years in power.

Now hobbled in Washington, shedding supporters, and showing little to no influence in voting booths, is MAHA on its deathbed? Or will it be reborn—possibly into a new group? Could it be assimilated into a blue platform—after sloughing off the more repulsive, conspiratorial aspects of Kennedy’s ideology? These are the questions circling in the aftermath of Tuesday’s bloodbath.

In a social media poll Wednesday afternoon, Mary Talley Bowden—a dejected MAHA activist, anti-vaccine advocate, and doctor reprimanded for prescribing ivermectin to treat COVID-19—polled her followers about what should come next. She offered three options: Start a third party, join the Democrats, or overthrow the GOP. As of publication time, joining Democrats trailed at just 3 percent of the votes. The remaining votes were roughly split between overthrowing the GOP—whatever that means—and forming a third party, which had a small lead at 49 percent.

That MAHA could fade out entirely or fracture into negligible shards seems like a very real possibility. Its collection of disperate groups and varied agenda items has always made its cohesiveness seem tenuous. A Politico poll in June revealed that people who identified as MAHA had different ideas of what the movement’s priorities are (or should be), as well as different political leanings, and sometimes opposing ideologies.

Some within the movement embrace (or at least accept) Kennedy’s fringe beliefs, such as his zealous anti-vaccine views, his rejection of germ theory, and his false claim that 5G is damaging our brains and causing cancer. Some also believe, like Kennedy, in chemtrails and want all mRNA vaccines banned. There are members who support his plan to ban water fluoridation and also seem fine with his false claim that Lyme disease was created by the US military as a military weapon.

Others, meanwhile, have been attracted to MAHA by the more reasonable, non-absurd issues Kennedy touts. Those include improving the quality of Americans’ food and diets, reducing their exposure to environmental contaminants, and limiting the influence of industry and corporate interests on Americans’ health, particularly limiting the sway of pharmaceutical and chemical companies.

With various priorities, Kennedy and MAHA leaders have spent the past two years finding ways to disappoint their members. Some like Bowdon are angered that Kennedy has yet to ban mRNA vaccines. In February, Kennedy sparked particularly intense backlash among supporters for embracing Trump’s decision to increase domestic production of the pesticide glyphosate—a pesticide many in the MAHA movement wanted banned. Kennedy further fueled feelings of betrayal in September by appearing at a MAHA-branded summit that had hefty corporate sponsorships, including from behemoths such as Walmart, Anthropic, OpenAI, Grail, and UnitedHealth.

“MAHA was supposed to expose the revolving door between money, industry and government, not build its own,” Robert Malone, a fringe scientist and previous Kennedy ally, wrote in a scathing response to the summit.

Going into the midterms on Tuesday, the number of disenchanted MAHA members had mounted. In October, Tiffany Cianci, a former Kennedy supporter, published a MAHA scorecard, which lists 42 promises that Kennedy and leaders made to the MAHA members and then largely broke. The scorecard reports 37 promises broken, five partially fulfilled, and zero kept. One of the “partially kept” promises was removing synthetic dyes from foods, particularly children’s foods. Kennedy announced an effort to do that—with much ballyhoo—but later admitted that the FDA did not ban or even have an agreement with food companies to remove the dyes. Instead, Kennedy said he has an “understanding” with food companies. The dyes remain in foods.

In a press release, Cianci wrote that the scorecard revealed “an abysmal string of broken commitments and betrayals.” It also contained a direct quote from her, saying: “I believed deeply in the promise that MAHA represented, particularly the possibility of confronting chronic disease and building a healthier future for America’s children. But belief was never supposed to mean blind loyalty.”

The press release included quotes from several other prominent MAHA leaders, all expressing disappointment with Kennedy and the Trump administration. Later in October, Calley Means, a top ally and senior advisor to Kennedy, resigned from his position, reportedly “disillusioned” and frustrated by delays in food policy changes sought by MAHA.

Another prominent figure to fall out with Kennedy was Vani Hari, an online influencer who goes by “food babe” and has previously identified as a “MAHA Mom.” As voting results were streaming in Tuesday night, Hari posted a comment on social media that she had given Politico months earlier, saying: “Women don’t come out to vote for glyphosate and war,” referring to the pesticide Kennedy had embraced and the Iran war.

In the wake of the blue sweep, it’s clear that more than half of the 24 MAHA-endorsed candidates lost their races Tuesday. That includes Senate candidate Ken Paxton in Texas and Zach Lahn, who ran for governor of Iowa. Lahn in particular was considered a “dream MAHA candidate,” and his campaign was seen as a test of MAHA’s political influence. Kennedy himself headlined a political rally for Lahn last week, calling it “the most important election going on in this country.” Lahn lost to his Democratic opponent, Rob Sand, who flipped the seat with an 11-point spread, turning the office blue for the first time in 20 years.

Alex Clark Voetberg, who hosts a wellness podcast called Culture Apothecary for Turning Point USA, echoed Hari, pointing to the glyphosate betrayal as a reason for the defeats. “MAHA moms stayed home and it could’ve swayed some of these races that were unbearably close. Should’ve listened to our warnings on protecting glyphosate—but you needed Bayer for the war,” Clark Voetberg wrote on social media.

Otherwise, MAHA also lost allies, such as Senator Roger Marshall (R-Kan.), who founded a MAHA caucus in the Senate. Rep. Zach Nunn (R-Iowa) lost his seat in the House despite reports that Kennedy allegedly intervened in the race to help him win. Meanwhile, critics of Kennedy notched wins, including Representative-elect Richard Pan, a physician from California; Senator-elect Annie Andrews, a pediatrician from South Carolina; and Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, an epidemiologist with a medical degree from Michigan.

Many congressional Democrats are already raring to hold Kennedy to account and remove him—and several of the newcomers are eager to help make it happen. In August, Andrews laid out her plans for health priorities to South Carolina Democrats, saying, “I believe we need to fully fund the CDC and the NIH, and I believe we need to impeach and remove RFK Jr.”

El-Sayed has also sharply rebuked Kennedy and said he looks forward to holding him “accountable for all of the grift.” After Kennedy made egregiously bigoted remarks against Arabs and falsely claimed they don’t contribute to science and literature, El-Sayed called out Kennedy’s lack of qualifications to be US health secretary. “He is an expert in people who have contributed nothing to science,” El-Sayed said. (Kennedy has no background in science, medicine, or public health. He also has a loathsome record of making bigoted and racist comments.)

On Wednesday afternoon, Sen. Angela Alsobrooks (D-Md.) wrote on social media: “The time is now. RFK Jr. must resign or be fired. End of discussion.”

If Kennedy is removed from office, the outlook for MAHA in its current form seems even murkier. On the one hand, Kennedy’s removal may make it easier for those in the movement to shed the divisive, abhorrent aspects of his ideology and focus on broadly popular issues—healthy foods, environmental contaminants, and industry influence. Such a move may help MAHA cement itself as a sustainable political fixture. But some of the most vocal figures in the MAHA realm are doggedly loyal to Kennedy, throwing such a transition into doubt.

Zen Honeycutt, an anti-vaccine activist and founder of Moms Across America, is one of those Kennedy loyalists. Honeycutt drew attention before the election for reaching out to Democrats on common causes. Her group endorsed several blue candidates, including Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez of New York, Sen. Cory Booker of New Jersey, and Rep. Chris Pappas in New Hampshire (all of them won their races).

“We expect Democrats and Republicans to work together,” Honeycutt told Politico earlier this month. “Health is not a political issue. You know, poison is not partisan. Cancer is not choosy.”

The sensible comment might suggest Honeycutt doesn’t care which party or politician follows through with MAHA’s goals, just as long as they’re achieved. But this is not actually Honeycutt’s position. Her intentions were made clear in exchanges on social media with Bowden, who harshly criticized Honeycutt for crossing party lines, particularly for talking with Ocasio-Cortez. “You understand she’s trying to use you?” Bowden wrote.

Honeycutt explained that she was working to smooth Kennedy’s path in Washington, not hand power to Democrats, who she said had been “fascist in mandating vaccines.” She instead turned the criticism to Bowden, saying the MAHA infighting and mudslinging was what was hurting the cause and handing Democrats wins.

On Wednesday, Honeycutt reaffirmed her allegiance to Kennedy, warning Democrats that impeaching him would be “extremely unwise.” She reposted a comment from one of her followers, writing: “I’m willing to give Dems a chance with their new power before I judge. I’m an independent. Kennedy is my red line. If they cross it I want them to get absolutely fucked in 2028.”

Still, there are some inklings that part of MAHA could be absorbed into blue platforms—whether the leaders of the movement in its current form like it or not.

Earlier this month, Brinda Adhikari, a journalist and co-host of the podcast Why Should I Trust You?, argued that MAHA wasn’t dying but remaking the Democratic Party to be more like its old self. In a Substack post titled How MAHA Is Making Democrats Democrats Again, Adhikari pointed to a health policy document from House Democrats with a section, led by Ocasio-Cortez, titled “Reclaiming MAHA and Protecting American Families.”

Adhikari wrote that some of the issues touted by MAHA are a natural fit for the Democratic Party. She stretched back to Rachel Carson’s seminal Silent Spring in 1962 and the progressive environmental activism that elevated issues of health, pesticides, toxic chemicals, and corporate influence over public health. “These were once bread-and-butter Democratic issues,” Adhikari wrote. “But somewhere along the way, Democrats lost some of their identity as the party leading these fights.”

Adhikari and Democrats credit Kennedy with redirecting attention to these issues, which have broad support among voters.

“Americans’ concerns about chronic disease and food safety, especially that which is linked to corporate influence over environmental regulation and public health, are legitimate and long overdue for a serious legislative response,” the House Democrat’s policy document reads. “The Make America Healthy Again (MAHA) movement has galvanized a significant segment of the Republican Party and attracted many previously apolitical and non-traditional voters, yet many of the concerns raised by the movement are bipartisan in nature.”

A KFF poll in May found that 75 percent of Americans want stricter regulations on food additives—a prominent MAHA priority—and 64 percent want stricter regulation of pesticides in agriculture, another key MAHA agenda item.

But Kennedy and the Trump administration have not only failed to deliver on these issues, but they have also spoiled the movement, using “the legitimate concerns of American consumers about food safety to spread misinformation about vaccines, public health, and science,” the policy document reads. “Their rhetoric has systematically led to the erosion of trust in public health, the science community, and decreased vaccination rates, leading to fatal outbreaks in some cases.”

Democrats intend to reclaim the popular health and environmental issues, free of Kennedy’s corrosive anti-science rhetoric. The document outlines plans to restore regulations that protect clean water and air damaged by the Trump administration, advance policies to keep toxic substances out of food, and further Food is Medicine policies, which provide produce prescriptions to American families to improve nutrition and health.

Adhikari predicts a political shift. “I increasingly think MAHA’s most unexpected legacy may be pushing Democrats back toward their own roots: challenging corporate power, fighting dangerous chemicals in our food supply, and holding toxic polluters accountable,” she wrote.

MAHA leaders tied to Kennedy and the anti-vaccine movement, like Bowden and Honeycutt, are unlikely to get behind such a shift. This could cause MAHA’s political power—however strong it actually is—to fracture. MAHA leaders have claimed repeatedly that their followers have the ability to sway elections. If MAHA hadn’t been betrayed or at least disappointed by Kennedy and Trump in the last two years, MAHA could have prevented Tuesday’s Republican wipeout, they have said.

But the polling data doesn’t support the idea that keeping MAHA in its current form maximizes its political power. While MAHA does have significant support among voters—41 percent of US adults say they support MAHA—that support is primarily tied to the health, environmental, and food safety issues, which are popular among non-MAHA supporters, too. Further, just 4 percent of MAHA supporters cited vaccines and medical choice—Kennedy’s key issues—as their reason for supporting the movement, the KFF poll revealed.

Beyond MAHA’s primary issues, both voters overall and MAHA supporters specifically said MAHA issues weren’t top of mind this election; instead, both groups named the cost of healthcare as a bigger issue than those prioritized by MAHA (the MAHA movement is not associated with the issue of health care reform). A separate KFF poll on election day found that 50 percent of voters thought that the Democratic Party was better able to handle healthcare, while only 30 percent of voters said the same of the Republican Party.

For now, it’s unclear exactly how—or if—MAHA will influence the political landscape going forward. It could break apart, live on through revamped Democratic policies, or try to form a new political party entirely. And, apparently, there’s also the possibility it will try to overthrow the GOP.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Manhunt in western Turkey after eight killed in series of shootings

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The shootings took place in rural areas northeast of the city of Afyonkarahisar (pictured)

A manhunt is under way for a suspect in western Turkey after eight people were killed and one seriously injured in a series of shootings.

The attacks took place in the Afyonkarahisar province on Thursday, with victims found in several locations in three separate vehicles, according to local reports.

CCTV footage published in Turkish media showed a man in a black jacket, white trousers and glasses clutching a long weapon as he stood by a red minivan.

Turkey's state-run Anadolu Agency reported that three people were killed near Ayazini village and three others near Kiyir village. In Yukaritandir, two women were found injured and later died in hospital.

Two teachers were reportedly among the dead.

Local media said roads leading to the affected villages were closed by police, while officers launched a large-scale operation to find the suspect, who fled the scene.

The newspaper Milliyet reports that a vehicle allegedly taken by the attacker was later found stuck in mud, and that the suspect had abandoned it before fleeing on foot.

In a statement posted on social media, the Afyonkarahisar Governorate said "all relevant institutions of our state have been working in coordination since the very first moment and are meticulously carrying out the necessary work."

It added: "We pray to God for mercy on the deceased citizens and offer our condolences and patience to their grieving families and loved ones."

Nearly a billion people face heatwaves and wildfire smoke in the same year

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Heatwave wildfire smoke days now reach about 916 million people a year, and the heaviest burden falls on countries rarely linked to fires.

Most research on overlapping heatwaves and wildfire smoke has focused on the United States.

A global analysis now shows that people in Zambia, the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Malawi experience the most days each year when these hazards coincide.

Between 2000 and 2019, about 916 million people a year lived through at least one such day, and the number grew by 287 million every decade. The reason is mostly more heatwaves, not more smoke.

Yao Wu of Monash University‘s School of Public Health and Preventive Medicine led the count with Yuming Guo and Shanshan Li, in collaboation with researchers from Peking University and the University of Tasmania.

The team matched daily temperatures to estimates of fire smoke in the air, square by square, on a grid about 17 miles (28 kilometers) wide.

The researchers then overlaid population maps to estimate how many people were exposed.

Overlap days per person rose from 1.0 a year in the first decade to 1.7 in the second.

When the team split that growth into its parts, 70.5 percent came from people living through more overlap days and 29.5 percent from population growth.

In an interview with Earth.com, Wu explained what that increase means.

“In other words, the problem is growing mainly because people are experiencing these two hazards together more frequently, not simply because there are more people,” Wu said.

Heat explains most of that. Heatwave days increased in 87 percent of the squares on the map, but smoke days increased in only 28 percent of them, mostly in North America.

So a larger share of smoky days now coincides with a heatwave, while the share of heatwave days that also carry smoke has barely moved.

The two hazards also showed a clear timing pattern.

Across the globe, the chance of a smoke episode starting began to climb about two days before a heatwave began, and it peaked on the heatwave’s first day.

A smoke day, in this count, was any day when fine particles or ozone in the air exceeded the World Health Organization’s daily guideline.

At least half of that pollution had to come from landscape fires, a category that includes forest fires, grassland fires and the burning of crop waste.

A heatwave meant at least two consecutive days with temperatures above the local 90th percentile – hotter than 90 percent of days in the reference period.

That is the most lenient common definition, and the team chose it to catch as many hot days as possible.

Africa accounted for about 39 percent of all person-days of overlap, with one person-day representing one person exposed for one day.

People in low-income countries averaged 4.4 overlap days a year, against 0.4 in high-income countries.

That gap widened over the two decades, because the low-income countries also had the fastest increase.

Five countries accounted for half of all person-days between 2010 and 2019: the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Indonesia, Mexico, Brazil and Russia.

A different pattern emerged when the team compared annual exposure per person.

Zambia ranked highest that decade, averaging 28.8 days a year, followed by the Democratic Republic of the Congo at 24.3 and Malawi at 21.7.

“These findings highlight that some of the countries experiencing the greatest exposure are not necessarily those receiving the most international attention for wildfires,” Wu told Earth.com.

Asked by Earth.com which country’s trend surprised the team most, Wu named Cambodia.

It ranked 40th in overlap days per person in the first decade, at 1.6 days a year, and ninth in the second, at 12.1 days.

“What makes this particularly noteworthy is that Cambodia experienced substantial economic growth during this period, yet exposure to these overlapping environmental hazards also increased sharply,” Wu said.

Economic development, Wu said, does not necessarily translate into less exposure.

The Democratic Republic of the Congo showed the largest absolute rise, from 11.8 overlap days per person a year to 24.3. Europe and Oceania saw slight declines that were too small to separate from chance.

Wu’s advice for such a day is to guard against both hazards at the same time.

Check the local heat and air quality warnings, limit outdoor activity and especially hard exercise, and stay somewhere cool with clean air.

Air conditioning and air filtration help, and so does drinking enough water.

“The challenge is that protective measures for heat and wildfire smoke can sometimes conflict,” Wu said. “For example, opening windows may help cool a home but can also allow smoke indoors.”

For a household with neither air conditioning nor a filter, Wu suggested shading the windows and cutting indoor heat sources.

Another option is a public space that offers both cooling and cleaner air. Older adults, children and people with existing health conditions may need particular attention, Wu added.

The study counted exposure, not illness. It did not track anyone’s health, so it reports how many people breathed smoke on a hot day and nothing about what happened to them afterward.

Earlier work suggests that heat and smoke together may do more harm than the two added separately.

The smoke figures are also estimates from a computer model of the atmosphere, checked against ground monitors where they exist.

The model handles the height of a smoke plume imperfectly, which can overstate pollution near a fire and understate it downwind. Carbon monoxide was left out of the count.

And the record stops at the end of 2019. Wu said the heatwaves and fires since then “raise concerns that combined exposure may have continued to increase, particularly in low-income countries,” but the team can’t yet put a number on it.

The next step is to use these maps to study how combined exposure affects health, especially in countries that have received little research attention.

The findings could also help shape warning systems that address heat and smoke together.

The full study was published in the journal Science Advances.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/nabip-sues-cms-over-moratorium-new-aca-broker-registrations" hreflang="en">Court blocks CMS' moratorium on new ACA broker registrations</a>

📅 2026-11-05  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Court blocks CMS' moratorium on new ACA broker registrations" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

A federal court has granted a preliminary injunction to two key groups representing insurance brokers over the Trump administration's moratorium on new broker enrollments.

The District Court of Maryland issued the injunction after determining that the National Association of Benefits and Insurance Professionals and Health Agents for America "have shown that they are likely to succeed on the merits of their claims under the Administrative Procedure Act."

The court also said that the organizations made clear they would likely suffer significant harm if the moratorium stays in place.

NABIP said that the ruling means that it will restart training for new brokers beginning on Nov. 6.

"Today’s ruling is a significant victory for licensed agents and brokers and the consumers who rely on their expertise," said NABIP President Mychal Walker in an announcement (PDF). "With Open Enrollment already underway, every day matters."

"Lifting the moratorium immediately and beginning training tomorrow will help affected professionals get to work assisting individuals and families in finding coverage that fits their needs and budgets," said Walker.

NABIP initially sued on Oct. 15 to challenge the moratorium. The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services instituted the moratorium in late September as part of its broader push to combat fraud on the Affordable Care Act's exchanges.

The National Association of Benefits and Insurance Professionals is suing the Trump administration over its moratorium on new brokers entering the Affordable Care Act marketplaces.

The organization, the leading trade group for insurance brokers and agents, said the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services' moratorium impacts close to 19,000 brokers and agents who will be barred from registering ahead of open enrollment.

CMS unveiled the moratorium in late September as part of its ongoing push to root out fraud, waste and abuse. In addition, the agency said it would terminate more than 200 agents and brokers that failed to comply with marketplace standards.

The agency said new brokers and agents were disproportionately responsible for submitting inappropriate enrollments and engaging in behaviors at high risk for fraud.

In an announcement (PDF), NABIP says that it supports the broader effort to combat fraud, but that CMS' moratorium "threatens the livelihoods of thousands of legitimate, licensed professionals who have not been accused of fraud and limits consumers’ access to professional assistance as they navigate Open Enrollment."

The organization argues in the lawsuit that the rule instituting the moratorium violates both the Administrative Procedure Act and the Affordable Care Act. The regulation is unlawful as it is "arbitrary, capricious, and unsupported by sufficient evidence," NABIP said.

The 19,000 agents who would be barred from participating under the rule are part of a critical network of professionals who help consumers find coverage that meets their needs, the organization said.

NABIP President Mychal Walker said that CMS should look elsewhere in its fraud efforts.

"The existence of fraud is not in dispute. NABIP has raised these concerns with CMS repeatedly and has offered concrete solutions to address them," Walker said in the announcement. "What is in dispute is whether the appropriate response is to exclude thousands of legitimate agents based solely on whether they registered last year, without any individualized evidence about them."

"There is a better approach: target fraud where it occurs, strengthen the system’s security and hold bad actors accountable while preserving consumers’ access to trusted professionals," Walker continued.

CMS has taken steps to crack down on brokers as the agency views improper ACA enrollments as a key target in its fight against fraud. CMS officials have repeatedly raised concerns that people are enrolled in ACA plans without their knowledge, or despite being deceased.

Earlier this week, the agency revealed it will purge 189,000 enrollees who are currently listed as enrolled in both ACA plans and other coverage, such as Medicaid or Medicare.
