Early Medical Pain Can Produce a Lasting Memory

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

Several years ago, I worked with a woman who experienced trauma on the first day of her life. She was born with Rh incompatibility, a potentially deadly condition that occurs when an Rh-negative mother carries an Rh-positive baby and her immune system attacks the baby's red blood cells. This woman required a blood transfusion in which all of the blood in her body was drained and was replaced with transfused blood. This medical procedure saved her life, but she is experiencing lasting psychological adverse effects from the trauma associated with this procedure.

As a medical student, I remember watching an infant with his arms and legs strapped down to a board in order for the doctor to perform a circumcision. As the baby cried and screamed, the doctor performing the procedure said that the young boy would not remember this procedure years later. No anesthesia was used. Was the doctor correct? I have wondered about this for more than 40 years, and I can tell you that my research into epigenetic memory has taught me that he was probably wrong.

As a father, I took my 2-year-old daughter to a routine pediatrician's appointment for a vaccination. She cried after the shot, but otherwise seemed OK. The doctor told us she would probably have a sore leg the next day, but Tylenol would take care of it. Well, the next day, our daughter wouldn't walk on her sore leg. She cried and cried, despite our efforts to manage her pain with Tylenol and Ibuprofen. Well, years later, our daughter grew up to be one of the most courageous women I know. She climbed every 14,000-foot mountain in Colorado—all 58 of them! But she has had to deal with a lifelong fear of needles. If she has blood drawn, she has to lie down because she is likely to pass out if she sees the needle. For years, I have wondered: Is her fear of needles related to that early vaccination?

For much of the 20th century, physicians believed that newborns didn’t feel pain. Infants commonly underwent surgery with little or no anesthesia. That belief began to change in 1987, when a review published in The New England Journal of Medicine showed that newborns can experience pain, and they can also experience powerful stress responses due to the pain.

This raises the question: Can pain experienced early in life create a memory that persists for years? Research involving epigenetic memory suggests it is possible.

Epigenetics refers to chemical modifications that alter how genes are expressed without changing the DNA sequence. These modifications help cells “remember” what has previously happened.

In 2004, a study in Nature Neuroscience showed that rat pups receiving little maternal care developed epigenetic modifications to a gene in the hippocampus that left them with exaggerated stress responses as adults. Five years later, another study found similar changes in the brains of suicide victims who had been abused as children. These studies suggested that early trauma can be remembered by modifications to our cells’ DNA.

Strong evidence in humans linking medical procedures to epigenetic change comes from studies involving premature infants. These babies may undergo about 10 painful procedures per day, including heel lances, needle sticks, intubation, and suctioning.

An Italian study found that very preterm infants exposed to more skin-breaking procedures showed increased changes to the serotonin transporter gene between birth and discharge. Serotonin helps regulate mood, anxiety, and pain. A follow-up study linked these changes to greater negative emotionality at 3 months of age.

These changes may persist. Researchers in Canada found that at age 7, children born very preterm had greater epigenetic changes than full-term peers. Neonatal pain predicted that epigenetic changes were associated with more behavior problems later in life. This same group later connected greater neonatal procedural pain to physical changes in the brains of these children at the age of 8.

Healthy, full-term infants are not immune to these trauma-induced changes. Newborn circumcision is one of the most common surgical procedures performed in the United States, and historically it was often done without anesthesia.

In a 1995 study, researchers noticed that circumcised boys cried longer and showed more pain behavior during vaccinations at 4 to 6 months of age than uncircumcised boys. Their prospective 1997 study confirmed it. Circumcised infants reacted more strongly to routine vaccination months later than those who had received a topical anesthetic before circumcision.

In other words, a single painful event in the first days of life appeared to change the infant’s pain system, and pain relief partly prevented it. Whether epigenetic changes underlie this effect has not been directly tested, but the pattern of lasting sensitization from early pain fits closely with the previously described findings.

Toddlers and school-age children remember procedures as well. A review found that a minority of children develop posttraumatic stress symptoms after surgery, and 12 to 31 percent of children undergoing cardiac surgery develop posttraumatic stress disorder. Medical fear in childhood can also shape how people approach health care as adults.

The evidence is preliminary that these changes are due to epigenetic memory. But it is encouraging to note that epigenetic changes appear to be potentially reversible, and maternal sensitivity can reduce these effects in full-term infants.

Necessary procedures should not be avoided because of fear. But the evidence argues that we should be taking infant and child pain seriously. Strategies for reducing the effects of painful and other traumatic procedures include providing anesthesia for circumcision and analgesia for other painful procedures, offering skin-to-skin contact, or breastfeeding during needle sticks, avoiding unnecessary procedures, and preparing older children for what to expect.

The infant may not remember the procedure. But their body may.

Yemen: Houthi clashes near Taiz leave residents in a 'state of panic'

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Fighting between Iran-backed Houthi rebels and Yemeni government forces has intensified around the city of Taiz. Residents face growing shortages of food, water and medical supplies as thousands flee their homes.

Four months into the fighting in Yemen between the Iran-backed Houthi movement and the Saudi-backed Yemeni government, clashes are concentrated around Taiz in the country's southwest. Yemeni authorities have warned civilians of sniper fire from surrounding hillsides. Houthi forces have also largely blocked the delivery of goods to Taiz, which is under the control of government forces.

Taiz is Yemen's third-largest city, with a population of around 1 million people. However, fighting has displaced around 134,000 people in the Taiz governorate region, accordingto the International Organization for Migration. The governorate accounts for almost half of the more than 319,000 people displaced across western Yemen, the organization said on November 1, 2026.

"The Houthis have blocked fuel, water and medical supplies, basically all things required for hospitals to function properly," Niku Jafarnia, Yemen researcher at Human Rights Watch, told DW. "Even those who can reach hospitals may not be receiving the care they need," she said, adding that it has had a devastating impact on people's health, particularly those with chronic illnesses.

Ammar, a 45-year-old photographer in Taiz, told DW on Monday that he sees people in a "state of panic" and yet he feels that "the determination not to submit to the Houthis is very strong."

Afnan, the mother of a schoolchild in Taiz, told DW that "neighbors share the little food they have and help each other and many people in Taiz refuse to flee." She and the other residents who spoke to DW asked that their full names not be published, fearing reprisals.

Hanan, a 30-year-old mother of three, said that prices have skyrocketed while there is almost no petrol, cooking gas or drinking water left in the city. "This increases the mental load on people who are already scared by hearing the clashes of the fighting day and night," she said. Despite this, she numbers among those who have decided to stay.

Meanwhile, Nabil Shamsan, the governor of Taiz, told DW that negotiations are underway to bring food, fuel and water back to the city. While it remains to be seen when and under what conditions this might happen, international aid organizations remain extremely concerned.

"Fighting and insecurity around Taiz is making it unsafe for civilians to move freely and also for our staff to deliver aid," said Alison Bottomley, an advocacy adviser with the Norwegian Refugee Council in Yemen. "This is preventing us from reaching the 7,000 people we are supporting with emergency tents, blankets and educational activities for displaced children," she told DW, calling on both factions to allow "civilians and humanitarian workers to move safely."

During the previous Yemen conflict, which began in 2014 and largely ended with a UN-brokered truce in 2022, Ansar Allah, the formal name of the Houthi group, had already laid siege to Taiz in 2015 and 2016. That siege brought the population close to famine and caused a humanitarian crisis, but the Houthis never captured the city.

In turn, Taiz, as a bastion of resistance, represents something the Houthis have struggled to subdue for more than a decade, Hisham Al-Omeisy, a senior Yemeni adviser with UK-based advisory firm Coral Core Consultants, told DW. "The city symbolizes a deeply rooted tradition of political pluralism, civic resistance and rejection of the Houthi model of rule," he said, adding that the city's continued resistance is both a military obstacle and a political challenge to the Houthis' claim that their authority across Yemen is inevitable.

Following the truce in 2022, the Houthis controlled the capital Sanaa, most of northern Yemen and parts of the center, while the internationally recognized government controlled much of the west and the south including the city of Aden. However, fighting reignited in late July and the clashes intensified in September, as the Houthis seized territory from forces loyal to the internationally recognized government.

Control of the strategically important Bab el-Mandeb Strait, which connects the Red Sea with the Gulf of Aden, is contested. The precise territorial situation remains unclear, while the Houthis retain the capacity to threaten maritime traffic. The conflict continues to escalate, alongside an exchange of missile and drone attacks between the Houthis and Saudi Arabia.

Data on the number of people injured or killed in Yemen varies because of the ongoing fighting and separate statistics given by the Houthi movement and the government's health authorities. According to the latest WHO Health Cluster situation report, at least 1,677 people had been killed and 7,395 injured as of October 25, 2026.

Meanwhile, seizing Taiz is highlighted as a key strategic step by analysts. "Taiz would give the Houthis a major advantage in consolidating their hold over northern Yemen while opening the way for further expansion toward the south and the Red Sea coast," Al-Omeisy said, adding that controlling Taiz would give them stronger cards in any future negotiations.

"Taiz is far more than simply another military front for the Houthis," he noted.

"However, the Houthis may not actually need to capture Taiz to achieve many of their objectives," Hisham Al-Omeisy said. "If they can isolate the city, sever its remaining supply lines and neutralize it as a military threat, they could consolidate their positions along the western coast while tying down significant government forces," he added.

"My greater concern is that the Houthis could use gains around Taiz to progressively weaken and fragment the anti-Houthi camp, rather than immediately launch a full-scale offensive against Aden," Al-Omeisy told DW, adding that the Houthis have repeatedly demonstrated an ability to exploit divisions among their opponents, and those divisions remain one of the government's greatest vulnerabilities.

A collection of intimate interviews with the late Jo Spence provides further texture to her life and legacy

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Jo Spence (1934-92) was a London-born and based British photographer, writer, class and health activist and educator. She is best known for challenging the assumptions of what photography could be, for example, by using her own image to question common tropes around female beauty, health, identity and societal expectations. Spence’s achievements centred on the small home and studio on Islington’s Upper Street that she shared with her partner and collaborator Terry Dennett (1938-2018).

The Unknown Recordings is an intimate self-portrait in text form. There are pictures, but surprisingly few. Still, in that respect, Spence’s disarmingly frank autobiography Putting Myself in the Picture (1986) could not be surpassed. Forty years on, this publication makes sense of a textual account largely in the form of transcripts of key historic recordings made with the artist.

The verbatim transcript of Spence’s interview for the British Library’s Oral History of British Photography, recorded in 1991, extends to more than 100 pages. It covers every aspect of her life from childhood experiences to early working life, before progressing to her photographic work, collaborations and politics, as well as her experience of breast cancer and leukaemia.

The conversation between Spence and the interviewer flows, with few fragments and diversions, in a manner that allows us (almost) to hear it. Generous in her sharing of extremely personal ideas and experiences, Spence does not shy away from difficult topics, least of all her own experience of chronic illness and her attempts to regain power over her own body and identity. Her friendship with artist Rosy Martin and their development of phototherapy, a form of psychological therapy centred on photography, is especially raw.

The transcript of a short personal recording made in 1982, after the breast cancer diagnosis, is more unsettling. This is Spence recording her own thoughts, filled with distress, tears and self-analysis: while it became part of her archive and she often blurred the line between private and public, it does feel a little like eavesdropping. Recorded over two days, it is intensely emotional, as Spence struggles to process and come to terms with the various stressors in her life. In a particularly poignant moment, Dennett is recorded shouting words of support, presumably from another room.

Spence’s interview with visual activist and educator Andrew Dewdney and curator Shirley Read has a political slant. Recorded in the Cockpit Arts Workshop in London in 1989, this conversation gives acute insight into Spence’s commitment to a political as well as photographic practice. It ranges across her ideas relating to different areas of her work, such as the Photography Workshop, Hackney Flashers, Half Moon Photography Workshop and Camera­work magazine. Ultimately, these projects perhaps coalesced around Spence’s focus on people’s right to speak and be seen. It is a powerful conversation.

A series of four appendices act as a kind of postscript to Spence’s voice. There is the documentation of Spence’s sacking from Half Moon over ideological differences; recently discovered images of Spence’s retrospective of 1994; photographs, thoughts and exchanges with artist Pru Clements; and Spence’s CV from March 1987.

This section also includes Dennett’s reworked introduction to Spence’s Putting Myself in the Picture. Here, he makes it clear that Spence’s work was done under difficult circumstances but that she remained focused on the importance of empowering others. Rather than concluding in some fashion or other, together these last documents point the reader to other rich visual and textual archival material that surrounds Spence’s life and legacy. She extended the possibilities of photography to speak about the unspeakable and photograph the unphotographable. Spence was not within the cultural elite, but found a way to tell her own story and hoped it would be a model for others to follow.

• Jo Spence: The Unknown Recordings, Museums etc, 256 pp, 30 colour and 8 b/w illustrations, £35 (pb), £25 (e-b), 1 January

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/humana-athenahealth-collaborate-expanded-electronic-prior-authorizations-nationwide" hreflang="en">Humana, athenahealth collaborate on expanded electronic prior authorizations nationwide</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Humana, athenahealth collaborate on expanded electronic prior authorizations nationwide" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Humana and athenahealth are expanding electronic prior authorizations nationwide in a newly announced collaboration.

Through athenaPayer, Humana is able to connect with more than 180,000 providers across the athenaOne network, which the partners said will create a more connected experience between the health plan and clinicians.

“Every phone call, fax and portal log-in adds work for a practice trying to get a patient the care they need,” said Paul Brient, athenahealth chief product and operations officer, in a statement. “Prior authorization shouldn’t require care teams to navigate a different process for every health plan. By connecting health plans and practices through one solution with a common workflow, we’re creating a more scalable way to exchange information, reducing work for both providers and payers while, most importantly, helping patients access care faster.”

Athenahealth internal data show more than 6,000 providers processed nearly 35,000 electronic prior authorizations with Humana in September, with more than 70% completed without requiring additional information from staff.

The collaboration reflects Humana’s broader strategy to improve care access through requiring less prior authorizations and making the process simpler when needed, executives said.

“Our goal is straightforward: help people get the right care at the right time while reducing unnecessary burden on the physicians and care teams who serve them,” said Traci Granston, M.D., Humana vice president of clinical innovation and trend, in a statement. “… This collaboration with athenahealth is another step forward, ensuring that providers spend less time navigating administrative processes and more time focused on patient care.”

In June 2025, insurers nationwide—including Humana—agreed to take steps to reform prior authorization, including supporting electronic prior authorizations.

Low-Calorie Diet and Supervised Exercise May Reverse Early Onset Type 2 Diabetes

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

The RESET for Remission trial, presented at the 2026 European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting and published in The Lancet Regional Health Americas on October 1, found that more than half of adults with early onset type 2 diabetes entered remission after six months on a low calorie diet and supervised exercise program.

Early onset type 2 diabetes is when the condition is diagnosed before age 45.

Those with early onset type 2 diabetes may be at a higher risk of complications like long-term damage to the capillaries (microvascular complications), adverse cardiovascular outcomes, and earlier death.

Typical treatment for early onset type 2 diabetes is often a combination of lifestyle changes, like diet and exercise, and medications.

However, this new study suggests that a supervised low calorie diet and exercise program may help some people with early onset type 2 diabetes enter remission.

The RESET for Remission trial included 96 adults from England and Canada who were between the ages of 18 and 45, had early onset type 2 diabetes, obesity, and were not on insulin treatment.

The participants had an average age of 38 and an average body mass index (BMI) of 35.2 kg/m2. They were randomized into a control group that received usual care alone and the intervention group, which ate a low calorie diet and participated in supervised exercise programs.

During the first 12 weeks, participants in the intervention group followed an 800–900-calorie-per-day diet and completed twice-weekly supervised aerobic and resistance exercise sessions, along with one independent exercise session per week.

For weeks 13–24, they transitioned to a weight-maintenance diet and independent exercise.

Both groups were supervised by physicians, and the intervention group was also closely supervised by licensed dietitians.

The low calorie diet consisted of 30% protein, 50% carbohydrate, and 20% fat. Participants were also encouraged to drink at least 2 liters of calorie-free fluids per day and given access to a fiber-based laxative as needed.

In the first two weeks, all meals were replaced with specially formulated nutritional products (Optifast). Then, in weeks 3–12, the participants maintained the same calorie intake but had more flexibility in their food choices.

This second phase of the diet included one conventional meal per day, with daily intake targets that included protein, fruit, and vegetables. Typically, this meant 60 to 90 grams of protein from all sources combined, one portion of fruit, and two portions of non-starchy vegetables.

At the end of 24 weeks, 54% of the intervention group entered remission of type 2 diabetes, compared with only 4% in the control group. Researchers defined remission as an HbA1c level below 6.5% without glucose-lowering medication for at least 12 weeks.

“This study proves what most physicians have advocated for quite some time. Clearly, diet and exercise can work to improve or even resolve diabetes,” David Cutler, MD, board certified family medicine physician at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline.

Cutler also added that this study provides a clear example of how difficult it can be to enter diabetes remission with diet and exercise alone. He stated that the small number of participants in the study also makes him wonder if these findings can be applied to all populations.

Another limitation of this study, as Cutler pointed out, was that the strict dietary limits imposed on study participants would be very difficult for the average adult to adhere to.

The study also followed participants for only six months, so it’s unclear how many would remain in remission over the longer term.

“It is worth trying diet and exercise first because they can work to achieve meaningful weight loss and remission of diabetes,” he said.

It may be possible to prevent or delay the onset of type 2 diabetes by:

The National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases (NIDDK) notes that losing 5%–7% of your body weight can help reduce your risk of type 2 diabetes.

Early onset type 2 diabetes is a growing issue worldwide, coinciding with the rise of obesity.

Research from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) projects that the number of people in the United States under age 20 with type 2 diabetes could reach 220,000 by 2060. This is an increase of 700%.

People diagnosed with type 2 diabetes at a younger age face more decades of living with the condition, along with a higher risk of developing complications earlier in life and reduced life expectancy. These complications can develop as early as midlife and include:

Speak with a healthcare professional about your risk of type 2 diabetes and changes you might be able to make in order to prevent or delay diagnosis.

A later diagnosis can help reduce the risk of some complications of the condition.

GLP-1s May Help Lower Diabetes, Heart Risks Better Than Standard Prevention

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

GLP-1 drugs are more effective in helping prevent type 2 diabetes than the commonly prescribed medication metformin, according to a new study.

Researchers said GLP-1s such as Ozempic and Mounjaro were more likely to reduce blood sugar to healthy levels in people with prediabetes and obesity.

They added that people prescribed GLP-1 medications had a lower risk of progressing from prediabetes to type 2 diabetes.

The researchers noted that GLP-1 users also had a lower risk of death from any cause as well as fewer heart attacks, strokes, and heart failure events compared with metformin users.

“The great news with prediabetes is that it’s still possible to reverse the progression of the disease,” said Vinicius de Sousa Barzon Serra, MD, a study author and research fellow in cardiovascular medicine at the Mayo Clinic in Scottsdale, Arizona, in a press release. “This research may add to the case for considering GLP-1 medications earlier for certain patients, rather than waiting until they develop type 2 diabetes.”

Serra noted that metformin still remains an effective treatment for people with prediabetes and type 2 diabetes.

“This is less about replacing one medication with another and more about discussing additional options with specific patients,” he said.

The researchers presented their findings at the American Heart Association Scientific Sessions 2026 in Chicago. The research has not been published yet in a peer-reviewed journal.

David Cutler, MD, a family medicine physician at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, wasn’t involved in the study, but said the findings were “quite impressive.”

“It elevates the comfort of prescribing GLP-1 drugs as a front-line treatment,” he told Healthline.

Robert Eckel, MD, a former president of the American Heart Association who was likewise not involved in the study, agrees the research provides more options for patients and prescribers.

“GLP-1s provide another option for prevention, perhaps as an add-on therapy. However, costs and access to these medications are important factors,” said Eckel, an endocrinologist at the University of Colorado School of Medicine, in the press release.

For their study, the researchers analyzed the medical records of 256,000 adults in the United States with prediabetes and obesity. About 76% of the subjects were women, and the average age of the participants was 52.

About half of those studied were taking GLP-1 drugs with the active ingredient semaglutide, tirzepatide, liraglutide, or dulaglutide.

The other half had been prescribed metformin, a medication used to help manage blood sugar levels in adults and some children with type 2 diabetes by decreasing glucose production in the liver, reducing glucose absorption from food, and improving the body’s insulin sensitivity.

The researchers reported that the people in the study who were taking GLP-1 drugs had a 1.5 times higher chance of reversing high blood sugar to healthy levels compared to people who were using metformin.

The researchers also noted that GLP-1 medication users had a 31% lower risk of developing type 2 diabetes and a 41% lower risk of death from any cause.

They added that GLP-1 medications were associated with 15% fewer heart attacks, 20% fewer strokes, 25% fewer cases of heart failure, and 8% fewer cases of atrial fibrillation (AFib), a common type of irregular heartbeat.

The researchers cautioned that their study is observational and, therefore, cannot prove a cause-and-effect relationship. They also noted they relied on prescription records, so the frequency and dosage at which patients took their medication was not reflected.

In addition, the GLP-1 drugs were grouped together, so individual differences between medications were not considered.

Eckel said in the news release that the benefits for people taking GLP-1 drugs were probably mainly due to weight loss. Eckel noted that the research results open up a wide range of possibilities.

“This is an important finding because the GLP-1 receptor agonists are commonly used today and this is a population that in the transition from prediabetes to diabetes are at increasing risk for cardiovascular disease,” Eckel said in a taped interview alongside the study findings.

Cutler pointed out that several GLP-1 drugs also target blood sugar levels and insulin production. They could be effective in people with prediabetes who don’t have weight management issues.

Cutler noted that these people, however, might be reluctant to take GLP-1 drugs because they may not be experiencing symptoms of their prediabetes but could develop side effects with the medications.

“They would not be getting any immediate benefits,” Cutler said. He added that metformin, which was first used clinically in 1958, remains an effective alternative. “It’s been proven safe, effective, and it’s very cheap,” Cutler said.

It’s estimated that 40 million people in the United States have diabetes, with more than 90% of those cases being type 2 diabetes, a chronic condition characterized by high blood glucose levels that results from the body’s ineffective response to insulin or insufficient insulin production.

It’s also estimated that 115 million adults in the United States have prediabetes, a condition in which a person has higher-than-normal blood sugar levels but not high enough to be diagnosed as type 2 diabetes.

Around 5–10% of people with prediabetes develop diabetes every year. More broadly, up to 70% of people with prediabetes eventually develop diabetes.

Medication is sometimes prescribed for people with type 2 diabetes and prediabetes. Lifestyle changes can help reverse type 2 diabetes and prediabetes, as well as prevent the progression from prediabetes to type 2 diabetes.

Cutler said the lifestyle changes needed to prevent type 2 diabetes are pretty clear.

“The most important thing for people with prediabetes to know is they should lose weight, reduce their carbs, exercise daily and, if that doesn’t work, then consider medication,” he said.

Losing Weight in Your 50s May Protect Your Heart Health Later in Life

📅 Nov 02, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Bezzy communities provide meaningful connections with others living with chronic conditions. Join Bezzy on the web or mobile app.

Can't get enough? Connect with us for all things health.

Losing weight in your 50s, even a modest amount, may help protect your heart health later in life, according to new research.Adults who lost weight in their early 50s saw their blood pressure, “good” cholesterol and inflammation change at about the same rate as adults who stayed at a healthy weight, according to a study from researchers at University College London and the University of Glasgow.Adults who gained weight in their early 50s saw those same measures worsen faster by their early 60s, the researchers reported. The authors posted the study on a research-sharing website on September 4. Other scientists have not yet peer-reviewed the work.

Charis Bridger-Staatz, PhD, a senior research fellow at UCL, led the study. She told Healthline the main takeaway for adults over 50 was that “it’s not too late to try and do things that are going to improve your health.”Nearly half of U.S. adults ages 40 to 59 had obesity from 2021 to 2023, the highest rate of any adult age group, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). Among all U.S. adults, around 31.7% were overweight, according to the CDC.

The findings can apply to Americans because British and U.S. adults share similar weight patterns, said Solveig Cunningham, PhD, a professor of global health at Emory University’s Rollins School of Public Health. Cunningham, who was not involved in the research, told Healthline that Americans tend to gain weight across adulthood.

“By the time you get into your 50s, close to the majority of Americans already have obesity. Many others have overweight,” she said.The World Health Organization (WHO) calls obesity a chronic disease. Healthcare professionals diagnose overweight and obesity using a person’s weight and height, or body mass index (BMI).

Bridger-Staatz said the team set out to learn who lost weight and kept it off before GLP-1 weight loss medications became available, and whether that weight loss improved health over time.

She said researchers had long assumed that “once people had become overweight or developed obesity, people didn’t really lose weight and certainly not sustained it.”The team also asked, she said, “can we try and say something positive about health that isn’t just this kind of like doom and gloom?”The researchers used the British 1958 National Child Development Study, which has followed about 17,500 people born during one week in 1958. Of those, 6,309 took part in a health check in their early 60s, according to the report.The team compared each person’s highest weight between ages 23 and 42 with their weight between ages 50 and 55. Researchers sorted people by whether they gained or lost more than 5% of their body weight and kept the change. For a 200-pound person, 5% equals 10 pounds.To track heart health, the team compared blood tests and blood pressure readings at ages 44 and 62 with those of people who maintained a healthy weight.“We know everyone’s heart health is kind of going to get a little bit worse over that time because you are aging and that’s expected,” Bridger-Staatz said. The question, she said, was, “Does it slow the rate at which people’s heart health is getting worse?”

Weight loss slowed the decline in heart health for people who were overweight and for people with obesity, according to the report.People who were overweight and lost weight had the strongest positive results. By age 62, their body fat-to-lean tissue ratio matched that of the healthy-weight group. The authors wrote that differences in body fat may partly explain why weight changes affected heart health.People with obesity who lost weight had less body fat at 62 than people in the other obesity groups, according to the report.Sudhakara Chennareddy, MD, prevention cardiologist in the Center for Cardiovascular Prevention, Metabolism and Lipids at Georgia Heart Institute, told Healthline those results are what he’d expect. He was not involved in the research.“Even losing 5% has beneficial effects on the cardiovascular side effects,” he said. Long-term blood sugar was the exception. For people with obesity who lost weight, it rose more than in the healthy-weight group, though the authors reported that the result was not statistically significant.

A 2025 study of more than 15,000 U.S. adults, published in Endocrinology, Diabetes & Metabolism, found that adults who had previously had obesity tended to have higher blood sugar than those who had never had it, even after their BMI dropped.

“It’s not the patient’s fault… We need to treat this one like any other chronic disease, like we treat hypertension or we treat diabetes,” Chennareddy said.Compared with the healthy-weight group, people who gained weight in their early 50s had faster increases in blood pressure, blood fats, and inflammation, and declines in “good” cholesterol. Obesity is a chronic metabolic disease that leads to inflammation in the body, which can raise the risk of heart attack and stroke and worsen cholesterol levels, Chennareddy said. “The more weight they gain, they develop prediabetes and diabetes and fatty liver disease as well,” he said.These changes were largest for people who developed obesity or already had obesity and gained more weight, the researchers reported. This group also saw faster increases in long-term blood sugar levels.About 37% of participants gained more than 5% of their body weight in their early 50s. Only about 6% lost weight and kept it off, according to the study.“To me, that’s sort of the stronger message,” Cunningham said of the findings. Her advice for adults in their 50s is to avoid gaining weight.

People with obesity who lost and then gained the weight back saw faster drops in “good” cholesterol and faster rises in inflammation and long-term blood sugar.

By age 62, they weighed slightly less, on average, than people whose obesity remained steady, according to the report’s data. They also carried more body fat and had more inflammation.Weight cycling can do more harm than steady weight, Chennareddy said. He gave the example of someone who gains 20 pounds, loses 30 and then gains 20 again. “That is even more detrimental,” he said.When people lose weight, the body slows its resting metabolism, he said. When the weight comes back, “most of the weight they gain is fat, not muscle,” he said. Even someone who loses and regains 5% to 7% of their body weight may end up with slightly less muscle and more fat, he said.Some research has shown that weight fluctuation is associated with a higher risk of high blood pressure and cardiovascular disease-related death.

The study’s weight changes took place between about 2008 and 2013, according to the study’s timeline.

Wegovy, a GLP-1 medication for weight management, launched in the United Kingdom in September 2023, according to the UK Department of Health and Social Care.Cunningham said the weight-cycling findings raised questions for people who take GLP-1 medications. She said drugmakers’ own data show that “once you get off of those, your weight is going to go right back to where it was before, possibly even worse.”That could create the same up-and-down pattern, Cunningham said. She added that researchers don’t yet know “what happens in the really long term” for people who take these medications.

The study showed links between weight changes and heart health and cannot prove that weight changes caused the results, according to the authors. They wrote that the small number of people who lost weight made those results less precise.Bridger-Staatz also said the team did not look at why or how people lost weight.

Cunningham said adults lose muscle and bone mass with age, and that the loss is especially pronounced for women in their 50s and older. “Maintaining muscles and maintaining bones is so, so important,” she said.Staying active helps people keep muscle, even without weight loss, Cunningham said. “Even a little bit of activity…just keep moving,” she said.GLP-1 medications work best as part of a “comprehensive plan,” Chennareddy said. With his patients, he said he recommends continuous improvement rather than setting a goal to lose a specific amount of weight within a set period.Patients who treat weight loss as “a gradual process” do better long term, he said.“The most important thing is not to do fad diets,” Chennareddy said.For people losing weight, Chennareddy recommended:

Anyone considering changes to their weight can talk with a healthcare professional about their weight history and goals.

Leading Medical Groups Team Up to Redefine Female Heart Health

📅 Nov 01, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Bezzy communities provide meaningful connections with others living with chronic conditions. Join Bezzy on the web or mobile app.

Can't get enough? Connect with us for all things health.

A quartet of medical organizations has published a comprehensive set of reports calling for a coordinated plan of action to improve heart healthcare for women throughout their lifetimes.

The reports detail how cardiovascular health issues can be prevented, monitored, and treated from the early years of menstruation to peak fertility age to pregnancy to perimenopause to postmenopause stages.

The report authors call for healthcare professionals to identify heart health risks early in girls and women and to coordinate care across medical specialties.

They stress that females have specific biological factors that increase their risks of cardiovascular disease. Those components include hormonal and emotional factors.

The authors note that heart disease is the number one cause of death for females in the United States, as well as the number one cause of death during pregnancy. Yet, only 44% of females are fully aware of the dangers they face from heart disease.

The organizations issuing the reports are the American Heart Association (AHA), the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP), the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG), and the Society for Maternal-Fetal Medicine.

The groups presented their findings in a trio of reports published in the AHA’s flagship journal, Circulation on October 26.

“Women experience their healthcare across clinical specialties, but it often is fragmented or disconnected,” said Sadiya Khan, MD, the chair of the collaborative writing group for the reports and a professor of cardiovascular epidemiology at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine in Chicago, in a news release.

“Specifically, these reports connect the dots for clinicians, for patients, and across health domains so that care is holistic and begins in adolescence, recognizing that women’s heart health is uniquely shaped by biological transitions not experienced by men — periods, pregnancy, perimenopause, and menopause.”

Experts not involved in the reports told Healthline the updates were a long time coming.

Nicole Weinberg, MD, a cardiologist at Pacific Heart Institute’s Women’s Heart Center in Santa Monica, California, said the reports present a positive framework for women’s health.

“Women have unique cardiovascular risk factors related to pregnancy and menopause, and certain traditional risk factors, including diabetes, may increase their risk of heart disease more significantly than they do in men,” noted Nissi Suppogu, MD, a cardiologist and medical director of the Women’s Heart Center at MemorialCare Heart & Vascular Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California.

“Recognizing these differences is essential to identifying women at risk earlier, improving prevention strategies and ensuring they receive appropriate treatment,” she said.

“I am incredibly excited to see these new reports on women’s heart health,” added Svati Shah, MD, the president-elect of the AHA, as well as the associate dean of Translational Research and the director of Precision Genomics Collaboratory in the Duke School of Medicine in North Carolina.

“They are a call to action for increasing education, research, and coordinated clinical care for women’s heart health,” Shah said.

The organizations spelled out their recommendations in three reports.

The first report is a presidential advisory that lays out a “female-specific life course approach to the prevention and management of cardiovascular disease.”

In it, the authors state that “girls and women uniquely experience fragmented care” across medical fields, including pediatrics, obstetrics, and gynecology.

The authors call for a “unified life course approach that addresses the female-specific interconnectedness of hormonal, psychosocial, and cardiovascular health.”

This report breaks down the life cycle for girls and women into five stages.

“Emerging data suggest that unique biologic transitions or life stages experienced by girls and women are linked with changes in [cardiovascular disease] risk, which may be due in part to physiologic changes in sex hormones,” the authors wrote.

Weinberg said this life-stage breakdown is important, especially for risk reduction.

She said issues with menstruation during the teen years may be a harbinger of heart health problems later in life. The same is true for health issues during pregnancy, especially high blood pressure or high blood sugar levels.

“We need to be aware of these heart disease risks so we’re not caught by surprise 10 to 20 years down the road,” Weinberg said.

Suppogu agreed. “Medical professionals should emphasize that heart health is a lifelong priority and prevention should begin early. Encourage girls and women to establish healthy habits, understand their cardiovascular risk factors, and maintain regular checkups, even when they feel healthy.”

“Women’s heart health has a unique constellation of risk factors and manifestations that are shaped by biological transitions that vary across a woman’s lifetime,” said Shah.

The second report is a science advisory that recommends a comprehensive plan for women’s cardiovascular care.

In it, the authors call for a system that “integrates assessment of hormonal, psychosocial, and cardiovascular health across life stages.”

They propose a “national infrastructure” of centers that would coordinate clinical care, education, training, research, and community engagement around women’s heart health.

“These centers can reduce fragmentation, enhance earlier risk identification, and expand opportunities to increase representation of women in clinical research,” the authors wrote. “In doing so, they offer a bold opportunity for a scalable and equitable model to improve cardiovascular health for all women.”

To illustrate their point, the authors noted that only about 60% of women with hypertensive disorders during pregnancy in the United States have a follow-up visit with a healthcare professional about their condition within the first 6 months after giving birth.

“Given that pregnancy is a cardiovascular stress test and hypertensive disorders of pregnancy are risk-enhancing factors for long-term [cardiovascular disease] risk, this represents a major missed opportunity to address cardiovascular risk,” the authors wrote.

“Pregnancy and the postpartum period are important windows into cardiovascular health throughout the life course,” said William Grobman, MD, the vice-chair of the writing group for these reports and a professor of obstetrics and gynecology at The Warren Alpert Medical School of Brown University in Providence, Rhode Island, in the press release.

“An adverse pregnancy outcome can unmask increased cardiovascular risk, and that information should guide care well beyond pregnancy,” he continued.

The third report is a scientific statement that suggests a “consensus guideline for the female-specific prevention and management of cardiovascular disease.”

In it, the authors state that there is “growing recognition that the optimal prevention and management of cardiovascular disease in girls and women requires attention to sex-specific considerations relevant to the interconnected domains of hormonal, psychosocial, and cardiovascular health across the life course.”

The authors note several age-related warning signs of heart issues. These are spelled out in the AHA press release.

Females comprise around half the population, and their cardiovascular health “must be a part of everyday care across medicine,” said Mariell Jessup, MD, the chief science and medical officer of the American Heart Association, in the press release. “These publications give clinicians and health systems a shared foundation for recognizing risk throughout a woman’s life.”

In a scientific statement published in February 2026, the AHA projected that 60% of females in the United States will have at least one type of cardiovascular disease by 2050.

The organization also predicted that 25% of U.S. women will have diabetes and 60% will have obesity by that year if current trends continue.

The authors of that report listed several steps they said could reverse these trends. Among them:

Experts say women can also take action on their own to reduce their risks. Suppogu suggests the following:

These recommendations can also be found in the AHA’s Life’s Essential 8 tips for a healthy heart.

“Most importantly, we need to educate women that heart disease is not inevitable, and early intervention can help prevent it and improve long-term health,” Suppogu said.

Weinberg advised women to include plenty of fruits and vegetables and a minimal amount of meat in their daily diet. She said that women should have regular check-ups with their doctor, and that adopting a healthy lifestyle doesn’t have to be complicated.

Khan said women’s health is a community effort. “All girls and women deserve more healthy years to live their fullest lives,” she said in the press release.

“Advancing women’s health is a responsibility we all share. This investment improves not only women’s health but also the health of their families and their communities.”

How Non-Dairy Creamers Doubled Coffee’s Calories For Many Americans

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Bezzy communities provide meaningful connections with others living with chronic conditions. Join Bezzy on the web or mobile app.

Can't get enough? Connect with us for all things health.

Coffee consumption among U.S. adults remained relatively stable between 2003 and 2018, but the average number of calories consumed from coffee beverages increased by nearly 40%, according to a study published in the Journal of the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics.

By 2017–2018, U.S. adults consumed an average of about 13 ounces of coffee beverages per day. Meanwhile, the average daily calorie intake from these beverages had increased from 43 to 60 calories since 2003–2004.

By 2018, non-dairy creamer had become the largest source of calories added to coffee, surpassing sugar or syrup and dairy milk.

The researchers analyzed data from eight consecutive cycles of the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES) from 2003 to 2018. The study included 30,459 U.S. adults ages 20 and older who provided two 24-hour dietary recalls.

The researchers categorized coffee beverages based on whether they contained added ingredients. These additions included:

The researchers found that coffee intake remained stable, while the average calorie intake from coffee beverages increased.

The absolute increase in caloric intake is about 17 kcal per day, which is only about 1% of a 2,000 kcal diet.

Coffee consumption was also higher among men, non-Hispanic white adults, and people with higher incomes. Those who slept fewer hours or worked longer hours also tended to drink more coffee.

In 2018, the most common coffee additions by weight were:

The researchers found no significant changes in the amounts of dairy milk, sugar or syrup, and sugar substitutes added to coffee between 2003 and 2018.

However, consumption of non-dairy additions increased during this period. Non-dairy creamer consumption rose from 3.37 to 8.08 grams per day, while non-dairy milk increased from 0.27 to 2.45 grams per day.

By 2017–2018, non-dairy creamer contributed the most calories among coffee additions:

In 2003–2004, non-dairy creamer contributed an average of 7.7 calories per day, meaning its calorie contribution more than doubled by 2017–2018.

Kristin Kirkpatrick, president of KAK, Consulting, LLC, and a dietitian at the Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness & Preventive Medicine in Cleveland, Ohio, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that these findings didn’t surprise her.

“The variety of coffee drinks, both at coffee houses and grocery stores, as well as options for creamers in the grocery store, make it easy to trade in black coffee for something closer to dessert,” she said.

She also added, however, that not all non-dairy creamers are created equal.

“Some are quite natural, with minimal ingredients, including no added sugars and no additives. Others are ingredient-heavy with either a large amount of sugar (real or artificial) and additives, so it’s really based on the type that is purchased,” she noted.

More than 60% of Americans drink coffee daily. Research suggests that drinking coffee with little to no added sugar or saturated fat may offer health benefits.

Previous research has associated moderate coffee consumption, typically around 3–5 cups per day, with a lower risk of several health conditions, including:

“These benefits make sense. After all, coffee beans are indeed a deeply hued plant, with multiple bioactive compounds that may strengthen health,” said Kirkpatrick.

She also cautioned consumers to be mindful of any caffeine sensitivities they may have. However, the benefits of coffee have been seen in studies assessing both caffeinated and decaf options.

However, adding ingredients high in sugar or saturated fat could offset some of coffee’s potential health benefits.

Kirkpatrick noted that some studies suggest milk may enhance antioxidant activity. The current study did not examine whether coffee additions reduced its health benefits, but found that these ingredients contributed additional calories.

Other research has raised concerns about the potential health effects of consuming excessive added sugar and certain food additives.

“I suggest that my patients try to embrace a black cup of coffee as opposed to starting the day with sugar,” Kirkpatrick said.

She suggested that certain additions, such as chicory root or cinnamon, may enhance your brew while still providing benefits. Plain milk can also provide protein and calcium without added sugar.

“Starting the day with [something] sweet may make it difficult to limit sugary foods later in the day. Starting the day with a more bitter approach may [be] an important factor in keeping sugar down, as bitter compounds may positively impact appetite-regulating pathways,” said Kirkpatrick.

Letter From the Editor-in-Chief: What a Decade of Bedtime Stories Taught Me About Health

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Bezzy communities provide meaningful connections with others living with chronic conditions. Join Bezzy on the web or mobile app.

Can't get enough? Connect with us for all things health.

I’ve read to my children each night at bedtime for as long as I (and they) can remember. We started with the likes of “Goodnight Batcave” and the lesser-known-in-the-US but oh-so-wonderful collection of verse-driven classics from former UK Children’s Laureate Julia Donaldson.

We moved on to B.J. Novak’s anarchic love letter to the power of words (and to great typography), “The Book With No Pictures.”

We eventually worked our way up to Roald Dahl’s wickedly dark back catalog, discovering more complex characters, language and plotting as we went.

When my eldest, Amelia, hit 6, it was time to roll out the big guns.

Over the last 5 years (she’s now 11), I’ve read the seven Harry Potter books aloud twice, complete with all the voices: once to Amelia on her own, once to both her and her brother, Henry, two years her junior.

I had feared she wouldn’t be keen on another helping, but Amelia immersed herself once more in the world of Hogwarts with no hesitation and took immense joy in watching Henry discover for himself every twist and turn on Harry’s journey.

We have done the same thing with Tolkien’s “The Hobbit” and his fantasy masterpiece “The Lord of the Rings,” and are currently midway through “The Two Towers” on our second go around.

There’s a huge amount of reciprocal joy we take from this nightly ritual, beyond the practical benefits of providing a calming demarcation between the end of the day and the wind-down to sleep.

For me, it comes as either a break in my working day or a signal that it’s over, depending on how hectic things are.

For the kids, it’s an all-too-rare chance to get me all to themselves, no deadlines, screens, or other distractions to pull me away from being anything but 100% present.

For all three of us, it’s a literal (and literary!) warm blanket — a chance to escape under the covers to familiar worlds and familiar faces. It is, in short, incredibly good for our well-being.

And yet, in the world of health information — including on this site — thousands, if not millions, of reading hours are dedicated to the benefits of nutrition, sleep, and exercise as the building blocks of good health. Reading itself rarely makes that shortlist.

For me, it unequivocally deserves a place on it. Research seems to support that case. Even if we need more of it to really understand how and why.

A recent editorial in Psychological Medicine co-authored by Barbara Sahakian, Professor of Clinical Neuropsychology at Cambridge University, draws on a broad range of research linking reading for pleasure to better mental health and greater empathy.

The positive impact of reading on young people — just like Amelia and Henry — lso carries evidence.

Among more than 10,000 US adolescents, those who started reading for pleasure early had fewer mental health problems, spent less time on screens and slept more. Although, per an earlier column of mine, I can’t vouch for the sleep side of things when it comes to early bird Henry.

For many, reading can be a form of therapy that improves well-being. Bibliotherapy is a complementary therapeutic tool for emotional release and reduced stress and anxiety, with its roots traced back to ancient civilizations.

There’s even research suggesting a link between a love of reading and a lower risk of cognitive decline.

In a 2003 study of people aged 75 and over, reading was associated with a 35% lower risk of dementia over roughly five years.

A separate 14-year study from 2022 found that people who read at least weekly saw less cognitive decline, whatever their level of education.

A happy (and healthy) by-product of this love of being read to is that both my kids have now started reading to me at bedtime. Henry recently read “Jeffy the Burglar’s Cat” to us, a lesser-known but no less compelling chapter book by Ursula Moray Williams that was a staple of my own childhood. And we’ve enjoyed several books from Amelia, including the wistful tale of friendship and the circle of life, “Charlotte’s Web” by E.B. White – not a dry eye in the house for the final few chapters of that one.

They’ve also gone off and re-read the Harry Potter books and “The Hobbit” to themselves.

The research above links reading to many health benefits: better attention and memory, fewer mental health problems, more sleep, less time on screens, and greater empathy. I haven’t measured any of those impacts empirically in our house. Nor am I naive to the fact that as they get older, they’ll ultimately grow out of me reading to them, with school, extracurricular activities, their friends, and inevitably screens competing for more and more of their time.

Come what may, though, I know that a child who considers missing a reading session as the most cardinal of cardinal sins, and who scrambles for the chance to read aloud to their dad at bedtime is, at that moment, concentrating, empathizing, discovering and growing.

Researchers are clear that studies espousing the benefits of reading show association rather than proof, and it’s evident that more experimental work and deeper research are needed to ensure the complex cause and effect stands on firm scientific ground.

Regardless, it’s a healthy habit Amelia, Henry and I are very happy to keep, and with a bit of luck, I’ll get to pass it on to their kids one day.

A list of recommended bedtime reads for you and your loved ones:

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

Until the ban takes effect, you can still purchase full-spectrum and broad-spectrum CBD. The legislation changes may bring an industry-wide restriction to hemp-derived products.

Every product you see in our articles goes through an intense vetting process. We choose them based on their indicators of safety, quality, transparency, and adherence to industry standards.

Our vetting process for this article swayed toward CBD products that met the following criteria:

CBD and THC have been cited to help various anxietiesTrusted Source and promote relaxation, but these claims are mixedTrusted Source and still need to be thoroughly researched.

CBD and THC gummies should not be anyone’s first-choice treatment for any health condition. Speak with a healthcare professional before trying CBD products to ensure there are no adverse reactions to any medications or any health conditions.

Whenever you’re taking CBD or THC, keep these factors in mind.

There are three different types of CBD: isolates, broad-spectrum, and full-spectrum. Full spectrum CBD products contain THC which can trigger an entourage effect in some people.

However, it’s important to note that CBD and THC can work one way in one person, but works a different way in the next person.

The effects THC has can vary greatly from one person to another, even if it’s a smaller dose. Lower doses generally may help you relax while higher doses may make you feel anxious or paranoid.

CBD will not get you “high”, unlike it’s cousin THC. Experts generally recognize CBD as safe, though there are some side effects that may occur, like fatigue, diarrhea, appetite changes, and weight changes.

There is some research that suggests CBD may interact with the liver, potentially interfering with the metabolism of other medications. If you take any medications or supplements, talk with a healthcare professional before taking any CBD or THC products.

Research notes that THC can induce symptoms of psychosis in people who do not already have the condition, and THC at high doses can increase anxiety. Other research also cautions against THC use if you have cardiovascular or respiratory conditions.

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA)Trusted Source warns that THC can pose health risks and causes adverse effects like hallucinations, tremors, anxiety, and more.

Stricter legislation is coming in, and it may affect access to CBD and THC products. The federal government is changing how they define “hemp” at the federal level. The new definition seeks to ban hemp-derived THC, the cannabis compound that causes a “high.”

The current law states that hemp is any part of the cannabis sativa plant that contains up to 0.3% of THC by dry weight. The change will restrict any hemp-derived products to a total of 0.4 milligrams (mg) of THC per container. This is much stricter than the current law and will impact access to products with a higher THC content.

The new law also bans synthetic cannabinoids, affecting CBD manufacturing. Cannabinoids, like CBD or THC, occur naturally in the hemp plant. However, chemical synthesis is often necessary to make CBD available on a commercial level, and this is where we may see further restrictions.

States with established recreational and medical cannabis regulations may be less affected by the ban. Meanwhile, those without existing regulations, such as Texas and Kentucky, may see a bigger impact.

Full-spectrum and broad-spectrum CBD products are most likely to be affected as they contain trace amounts of THC.

The ban is currently due to take effect by mid-December. It’s unclear exactly how these changes will affect the industry, but until then, people can still purchase CBD THC gummies and other CBD products, like oils and topicals.

Gummies that contain THC, or tetrahydrocannabinol, do get you high. THC is the main psychoactive component of cannabis, so even a small amount could make you feel the effects strongly.

If you decide to venture out, make sure you’re following the suggested dose on the packaging and remember that every person is different when it comes to how THC affects them. Take it slow and one step at a time to avoid any anxiety or adverse effects.

Gummies can typically take from 30 minutes to 2 hours to kick in. “They have to be digested and processed through the liver before entering your bloodstream,” says Alex Nguyen, PharmD, RPh, CPh.

“CBD may help soften some of THC’s more intense effects while contributing its own calming and anti-inflammatory properties. Many individuals find the combination more balanced and effective than either one alone.

“Keep in mind that even small amounts of THC can affect people differently, so starting with a low dose is always the safest approach,” he says.

CBD gummies with up to 0.3% THC by dry weight are currently federally legal in the United States. This can mean that a product can legally include up to 10 mg of THC.

However, in November 2025, a new ruling was passed that allows no more than 0.4 mg total THC per container.

That means that a standard 10-count package of CBD gummies can legally include a maximum of only 0.04 mg of THC per gummy. This ruling goes into effect in November 2026.

CBD gummies are not legal in all 50 states. A person should always check local laws before buying CBD gummies of any kind.

📅 Aug 05, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Though GLP-1 agonists can be beneficial for weight loss, it’s important to keep in mind that there is limited research on the long-term effects of these medications.

Additionally, research from 2022 suggests that weight regain is likely once you stop taking GLP-1 agonists.

Therefore, it’s best to pair GLP-1 agonists with modifications to your diet and lifestyle. A healthcare professional, such as a doctor or registered dietitian, can also provide ongoing guidance and support to promote long-term weight loss.

GLP-1 agonists are currently approved for three conditions: type 2 diabetes, obesity, and heart disease.

Keep in mind that these medications should be used alongside other lifestyle modifications, including changes to your diet and regular physical activity.

A doctor or other trusted healthcare professional can help determine whether a GLP-1 agonist may be beneficial for you, depending on your health concerns and goals.

Though GLP-1 agonists may support weight loss and help manage blood sugar levels, it’s important to be aware of the possible side effects, such as vomiting, stomach pain, fatigue, constipation or diarrhea, mood changes, and more.

Less common but more severe side effects include:

Because of the risk of side effects, it’s important to check in regularly with a healthcare professional. Additionally, it’s important to discuss any concerns you have about potential side effects with your doctor or other healthcare professional to make sure the medication is right for you.

When you lose a lot of weight quickly, your body loses fat, but it also loses lean mass, including muscle and bone density. This is problematic because muscle is the “engine” of your metabolism, helping you burn energy even when you are resting.

Research shows that people taking GLP-1 medications can lose a significant amount of muscle alongside fat. For example, in studies, about 15% to 40% of the total weight lost was lean mass. Exercise, such as strength training, can help preserve and build muscle while the medication helps you lose fat.

Exercise is also vital for bone health. Rapid weight loss is often linked to a reduction in bone mineral density, which can make bones weaker and more prone to fractures later in life.

A 2024 analysis of a randomized clinical trial found that people using the GLP-1 liraglutide without exercising lost weight but had decreased bone density in the hips and spine. People who combined liraglutide with regular exercise experienced the best weight loss and preserved their bone mineral density.

Exercise also has a lot of other benefits that GLP-1 medications can’t provide, like:

When used along with a balanced diet and exercise, Mounjaro can be an effective off-label treatment for weight loss and long-term weight management. How quickly the drug starts to work and the amount of weight loss can vary between people.

To learn more about what to expect when using Mounjaro for weight loss, talk with your doctor. They can tell you how soon you may see results and help determine what a safe weight-loss goal is for you based on your specific health factors.

If you’ve tried every app and trend to lose weight and nothing’s stuck, listen up. This app isn’t about food restriction; it’s about checking under the hood (your brain) to see how things function and what makes it tick.

Noom Weight is more than your average weight loss app. It’s giving ‘therapist, coach, dietitian,’ but also ‘bestie who’s been through this before, and wants to follow you on your journey’.

It’s a mix of psychology, coaching, flexible food tracking, and a community of folks who have been where you are (yup, even there).

Noom’s goal isn’t to fix you. It wants to be the guardrails in the noisy world of wellness, helping you figure out what your body actually needs to live its best life.

The magic touch is its psychology-based approach. It’s less about what you’re eating and more about the why.

A gut check, essentially (pun-intended), to help you understand your habits. All without the side of guilt.

There are no ‘forbidden’ foods here. Noom uses a color-coded system to help you understand which foods offer the most nutrients without the side of guilt.

The psychology-based program helps you understand and unlearn your old habits and helps you build new ones that can last longer than before.
